# Review appendix — full verified findings

Companion to `REVIEW.md`. Produced by a five-lens adversarial review workflow (22 agents): each lens's findings
were judged independently by three verifiers — **internal** (paper text + arithmetic only), **external** (primary
sources re-fetched: BOE, AEAT, Hacienda/Moncloa, INE, IEF, Skatteetaten, SSB, ESTV, impots.gouv, journal pages),
and **advocate** (the strongest honest live response Olga Cantó and Francisco García-Rodríguez could give — these
feed the rehearsal). Tag = majority verdict. Nothing below is asserted beyond what its verdicts support.


## Lens: Data and replicability

**Lens summary.** The data infrastructure is real, well-documented, and — unusually for tax-register work — obtainable: the Panel de Hogares is a joint INE-AEAT-IEF product with waves 2016-2023 that includes the modelo 714 Wealth Tax declarations, a wealth file built from informative returns and the Catastro, and a real-estate module with cadastral values, and the IEF delivers the anonymized files free to any researcher (including foreign ones) who signs its data-request protocol, so Max could request the same 2022 wave. The paper as written, however, is not reproducible: there is no code or parameter appendix, the Swiss simulation is entirely unparameterized, the Norwegian and French designs as described deviate from the actual 2022 statutes (missing Norway's 1.1% top bracket and housing-valuation kinks; France's 30% main-residence abatement and 75% income cap, the latter contradicting the paper's claim that Spain's cap "has no analogue in the foreign systems"), and pivotal Spanish rules (whether the 60% IRPF-IP cap, the family-business exemption, and the EUR 300,000 residence exemption apply in Sims 1-3 and Piketty) are never stated. The baseline is never validated against AEAT's published 2022 IP statistics, which show 185,101 positive-liability taxpayers and EUR 1,250M of cuota versus the paper's 170,404 and EUR 1,125.58M (-7.9% and -10.0%), and the weighted 44.86M individuals reveal an undisclosed exclusion of the Basque Country and Navarre (6% of Spain's population, both with their own wealth taxes). The single cadastral-to-market ratio is printed with two different values on the same page (3.0838 in the equation, 3.1125 in the bullet below it), and its formula divides EFF market values by PH cadastral values under one property index, so it is not computable as written. None of this likely overturns the qualitative ranking (broad bases dominate rate tinkering), but revenue levels, taxpayer counts, and regional composition claims carry unacknowledged data uncertainty, and the fix is cheap: publish the parameter tables and code.

### [CONFIRMED] The Panel de Hogares is genuinely accessible to outside researchers — replication is possible in principle, and the paper understates who produces it
*id `data-access` · would change conclusions: low · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** The PH is a joint product of INE, AEAT and the IEF (the paper credits only AEAT and IEF), with waves 2016-2023, and it includes exactly the wealth content the paper needs: an M714_2022 file with the Wealth Tax declaration variables, a PATRIMONIO2022 file built from informative returns and the Catastro, and a real-estate module with cadastral values. Access is free for researchers and analysts upon signing the IEF's data-request protocol, with anonymized microdata files delivered to the user — so an outside researcher like Max could request the same 2022 wave. This makes the study replicable in principle, though the files cannot be redistributed, so a conventional public replication package with data is impossible; code plus parameters would still make it re-runnable by anyone who signs the protocol.

**Paper evidence.** Sec. 4.1, p.7: 'a database compiled by the AEAT (Agencia Espanola de Administracion Tributaria (AEAT) and provided by the Instituto de Estudios Fiscales (IEF)'; p.3: 'information on individual income and wealth for the 2016-2023 period'; p.8 lists the PH's wealth advantages ('the Wealth Tax (IP) declarations').

**External evidence.** IEF DT 5/2024 'Panel de hogares: Ejercicio 2022' (https://www.ief.es/docs/destacados/publicaciones/documentos_trabajo/2024_05.pdf): 'El Instituto Nacional de Estadistica, la Agencia Estatal de Administracion Tributaria y el Instituto de Estudios Fiscales ponen a disposicion publica el Panel de Hogares'; lists files 'M714_2022: Son las variables de la declaracion del Impuesto sobre el Patrimonio' and 'PATRIMONIO2022' (informative models + Catastro). IEF PH page (https://www.ief.es/investigacion/Est_panelHogares.vbhtml): follow-up 'entre 2016 y 2023'; 2023 wave 1.19M households / 3.55M individuals; Modulo Inmobiliario with 'el valor catastral'. IEF access page (https://www.ief.es/Investigacion/Est_peticion.vbhtml): 'de modo gratuito... sujeta a la firma de un Protocolo que fija las condiciones para la recepcion y utilizacion de los ficheros de microdatos anonimizados'.

**Suggested question.** Which of the IEF's five PH products did you use (the full panel, the submuestra, or the Panel de Patrimonio), and did you use any variables beyond the standard IEF release — so that another team requesting the 2022 wave under the IEF protocol would receive exactly your input data?

- **internal verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** Everything this finding asserts ABOUT THE PAPER checks out exactly: Sec. 4.1 (p.7) credits the PH only as 'compiled by the AEAT... and provided by the Instituto de Estudios Fiscales (IEF)', and a grep of paper.txt and slides.txt finds zero occurrences of 'INE' or 'Instituto Nacional' anywhere; p.3 states the data cover 'the 2016-2023 period'; pp.7-8 confirm the PH includes IP declarations, cadastral-valued real estate, and non-filer records. However, the load-bearing claims — INE co-production, the M714_2022 and PATRIMONIO2022 files, and free access upon signing the IEF protocol — rest entirely on external IEF sources that cannot be checked from the paper, slides, or arithmetic, so under an internal-evidence-only standard the finding is not decidable. Nothing in the paper contradicts any part of it.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** Every external claim checks out against primary sources I fetched. IEF DT 5/2024 (downloaded from ief.es) opens: 'El Instituto Nacional de Estadística, la Agencia Estatal de Administración Tributaria y el Instituto de Estudios Fiscales ponen a disposición pública el Panel de Hogares', lists 'M714_2022: Son las variables de la declaración del Impuesto sobre el Patrimonio' and 'PATRIMONIO2022' ('a partir de la información recogida en distintos modelos informativos y en el Catastro'), plus 'MÓDULO INMOBILIARIO DEL PANEL DE HOGARES 2016/2022' with variables VALCAT/URBAVALORC 'Valor Catastral', and says 'Se pone a disposición de los investigadores 5 productos'. The IEF Carta de Servicios (hacienda.gob.es) states the IEF makes tax microdata available 'de modo gratuito' subject to 'la firma de un Protocolo', and the signed-protocol PDF (PeticionDatos.pdf, 19 Apr 2024) bars distribution to third parties — supporting both the access claim and the no-redistribution caveat. AEAT's own sede page confirms the same three institutions and routes requests through the IEF. Grep of the paper and slides finds zero mentions of INE ('Instituto Nacional' / 'INE': no matches), while Sec. 4.1 (p.7) credits only 'compiled by the AEAT... and provided by the... IEF' — the understated-provenance point is verified.
- **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** The paper credits only AEAT and IEF (Sec. 4.1, p.7: 'compiled by the AEAT... and provided by the Instituto de Estudios Fiscales') and never mentions INE, the access route, or the M714/PATRIMONIO modules; grep confirms no data-availability language anywhere. AEAT's own Panel de Hogares page confirms joint production ('El Instituto Nacional de Estadistica, la Agencia Estatal de Administracion Tributaria y el Instituto de Estudios Fiscales ponen a disposicion publica el Panel de Hogares') and directs researchers to an IEF request link; search results confirm the IEF request-protocol document (PeticionDatos.pdf) with conditions for receiving anonymized microdata files and that five PH products are offered to researchers. Direct fetches of ief.es failed on a certificate error, so the 'gratuito' quote and 2023-wave sizes rest on the finder's fetch plus corroborating snippets, but the substance (outside access under protocol; redistribution barred; code+parameters the missing piece) is solid and the authors would confirm it from their own experience obtaining the data. Survives fully.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** We thank the discussant for confirming, rather than doubting, the accessibility of our data. The Panel de Hogares is indeed a joint product of INE, AEAT and the IEF; our Section 4.1 wording describes the flow by which researchers receive it — AEAT compiles the tax records and the IEF delivers the files — and we will credit all three institutions explicitly. We used the standard IEF release of the 2022 wave, including the Wealth Tax declaration variables and the wealth built from informative returns and cadastral records, so a team signing the IEF protocol would receive exactly our input data. We agree that, since the protocol bars redistribution of microdata, publishing our parameters and code is the right route to effective replicability, and we are open to doing so for the journal version.


### [CONFIRMED] No code, no availability statement, and no parameter tables for the 17 regional regimes the paper claims to replicate 'in full detail'
*id `no-replication-package` · would change conclusions: medium · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** The published WP-95 PDF contains no replication package, code link, or data-availability statement, and no GitHub or supplementary material exists on the EQUALITAS site or elsewhere that I could find. Footnote 6 claims the baseline 'replicates in full detail the regional heterogeneity of the tax, including the specific rate schedules, exemption thresholds, and deductions and allowances applicable in each autonomous community' — but not one regional schedule, rebate, or deduction is tabulated anywhere in the paper, so the claim is unverifiable and the baseline unreproducible. The paper also contradicts itself on which year's law is simulated: the body says 'the benchmark is the legislation applicable to fiscal year 2023' while footnote 6 and everything else say 2022.

**Paper evidence.** Fn. 6, p.9: 'The simulation of the Spanish Wealth Tax (IP) replicates in full detail the regional heterogeneity of the tax... applicable in each autonomous community for the fiscal year 2022'; body p.9: 'the benchmark is the legislation applicable to fiscal year 2023'; Table A.2 (p.40) gives only one-line qualitative descriptions per design. Grep of the full paper text finds no 'replication', 'code', 'github', or 'available upon request' statement.

**External evidence.** WP-95 PDF fetched from https://equalitas.es/sites/default/files/2026-04/WP-95.pdf — identical to the paper, no availability statement (verified by text extraction and grep). GitHub/web search for replication code for this paper ('wealth tax Spain microsimulation panel de hogares') returned no repository.

**Suggested question.** Would you commit to publishing, alongside the working paper, the full parameter tables for the 17 regional IP regimes and the four foreign designs, plus the tax-calculation code? Since the IEF delivers the PH free to any researcher who signs its protocol, code plus parameters is all that separates this from a fully replicable study.

- **internal verifier — CONFIRMED.** The paper itself (which is the WP-95 text) contains no replication, code, or data-availability statement: grep finds zero hits for replication/github/'available upon request'/supplementary/repository, and 'code' appears only in 'JEL Codes' and 'Code général des impôts'; the slides' only pointer is a QR under 'Working paper available at:' (slides final page, verified visually) — the WP itself, not materials. Footnote 6 (p.9) is quoted verbatim ('replicates in full detail the regional heterogeneity... applicable in each autonomous community for the fiscal year 2022'), yet no regional schedule, rebate, or deduction is tabulated anywhere — Table A.2 (p.40) gives one-line qualitative entries ('Regions may modify rates and grant full rebates') and the text names only the state 700k/300k thresholds, 0.2%-3.5% rates, and the Madrid/Andalusia 100% rebate example. The 2023/2022 contradiction is real and internal: body p.9 says 'the benchmark is the legislation applicable to fiscal year 2023' while fn.6 on the same sentence says 2022, fn.2 (p.5) says 'our baseline is the 2022 IP framework', and Table 1 (p.11), the abstract context, and the slides all say 2022. The finder's negative web search (no repo on EQUALITAS or GitHub) is external and not re-verified here, but it is secondary to the confirmed in-paper absences.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** I re-downloaded https://equalitas.es/sites/default/files/2026-04/WP-95.pdf and text-extracted it: it is the identical paper (same title, Table 3 row 170,404/1,125.58, Table A.1 1,046,976 households), and grep finds no replication, code, GitHub, supplementary, or availability statement — the only 'replicat*' hits concern replicating tax legislation. GitHub API repository search returns 0 results and web search finds no code. Stronger still: the Research Square preprint of this exact paper (rs-9834492, posted 5 June 2026, under review at International Tax and Public Finance) carries an empty declarations field — only 'No competing interests reported', no data/code availability, and its sole file is the manuscript PDF. The internal contradiction is verified in the source text: body p.9 'the benchmark is the legislation applicable to fiscal year 2023' versus fn.6 'applicable in each autonomous community for the fiscal year 2022', and Table A.2 (p.40) gives only one-line qualitative descriptions per design — no regional schedule, rebate, or deduction is tabulated anywhere.
- **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** Grep of the full text finds no replication, code, github, or availability statement of any kind; the only occurrences of 'replicate' are modeling claims (pp.2, 9, fn.6). Footnote 6 (p.9) claims the baseline 'replicates in full detail' all regional schedules, thresholds and deductions, yet no regional parameter appears anywhere — Table A.2 (p.40) is one qualitative line per design — so the claim is unverifiable as published. The 2023/2022 contradiction is verbatim: body p.9 'the benchmark is the legislation applicable to fiscal year 2023' versus fn.6 'for the fiscal year 2022', Table 1 (p.11) 'Spanish Wealth Tax (2022)', and fn.2 (p.5) 'our baseline is the 2022 IP framework'. My independent search also found no repository or supplementary material (only a Research Square preprint of the same manuscript, rs-9834492/v1). The authors can only promise fixes, so the finding survives intact.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** This is a working paper and we did not prepare a replication package at this stage; the regional parameters we coded come directly from the 2022 state and autonomous-community legislation, so they are reproducible from public statutes, but we accept that footnote 6's claim cannot be verified from the paper as it stands. We are happy to commit to an appendix tabulating the 17 regional schedules, thresholds, deductions and rebates, plus the four foreign designs, and to release the tax-calculation and index code alongside the journal version — the IEF protocol restricts the microdata, not our code. The sentence on p.9 referring to 'fiscal year 2023' is a leftover from an earlier draft: as footnote 6, Table 1 and footnote 2 state, everything is simulated under the 2022 framework, and we will correct it.


### [CONFIRMED] The Swiss simulation has no parameters at all, and the Norwegian and French designs as described deviate from the actual 2022 statutes
*id `foreign-designs-unreproducible` · would change conclusions: medium · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** Sim. CH (EUR 4.84bn revenue, 12.4M taxpayers) rests on 'the structure of the Canton of Zurich' with no schedule, threshold, or multiplier given: the real Zurich tax has a statutory scale of 0.5-3 per mille above allowances of CHF 80,000/159,000, which is then multiplied by cantonal (about 98%) plus municipal (about 119% in Zurich city) Steuerfuss — a factor of about 2.2 that determines whether the top marginal rate is 0.3% or about 0.65%, and the paper never says which was applied. Sim. NO's 'flat 1% (0.3% + 0.7%)' matches Norway's 2023 split, not 2022 (0.7% municipal + 0.25% state, with a 0.4% state rate — 1.1% total — above NOK 20M that the paper omits entirely), and the 25%-of-market housing valuation actually applies only to primary dwellings up to NOK 10M, with secondary dwellings at 100%. Sim. FR is described as taxing wealth 'above EUR 800,000' on p.5 but with a 'EUR 1.3 million threshold' in Table A.2 (both are elements of the real IFI — liability triggers at 1.3M, the scale starts at 800k — but the paper never says which it implemented), omits the IFI's 30% main-residence abatement and 1.3-1.4M decote, and its justification for dropping Spain's 60% cap — that it 'has no analogue in the foreign systems' — is factually wrong for France, whose IFI is capped at 75% of income.

**Paper evidence.** p.10: 'The simulation follows the structure of the Canton of Zurich, applying a progressive schedule with reduced rates'; Table A.2 (p.40) CH row: 'Cantonal thresholds / Progressive (low rates)'. p.10: 'a flat 1% rate applied to the tax base, after adjusting housing values to 25% of their estimated market value'; Table A.2 NO row: 'Low threshold (EUR 160,000 individual)... State-municipal split rate (0.3% + 0.7%)'. p.5: 'The French IFI taxes only net real-estate wealth above EUR 800,000 with marginal rates between 0.5% and 1.5%' vs Table A.2 FR row: 'EUR 1.3 million threshold'. p.10: 'the simulations of international models do not incorporate Spain's joint IRPF-IP tax-liability limit, as this mechanism is specific to the Spanish institutional framework and has no analogue in the foreign systems.'

**External evidence.** Skatteetaten 2022 rates (https://www.skatteetaten.no/en/rates/wealth-tax/?year=2022): municipal 0.7%; state 0.25% on NOK 1,700,001-20,000,000 and 0.4% above; threshold NOK 1.7M single / 3.4M married. Skatteetaten housing valuation (https://www.skatteetaten.no/en/person/taxes/get-the-taxes-right/property-and-belongings/houses-property-and-plots-of-land/tax-value/taxable-value-of-residential-properties/): primary dwelling 25% up to NOK 10M with the excess at a higher rate; secondary dwellings 100% (the 2022 above-10M rate was 50% per search results, later 70% — exact 2022 excess rate from search results only). Service-public.gouv.fr F563: IFI liability above EUR 1,300,000; 30% abatement on the main residence. Fondation de France IFI page (https://ifi.fondationdefrance.org/calculer-ifi): bareme starts at EUR 800,000 (0.5% ... 1.5% above 10M); decote for 1.3-1.4M; 'plafonnement... ne depasse 75% des revenus mondiaux nets'. Canton Zurich scale (secondary, https://neho.ch/de/blog/vermogenssteuer-zurich): 0.5-3 per mille above CHF 80,000/159,000; Steuerfuss canton 98%, Zurich city 119%.

**Arithmetic.** Zurich top marginal if base tariff only: 3 per mille = 0.30%. With Steuerfuss (98% + 119% = 217%): 3 per mille x 2.17 = 6.51 per mille = 0.65% — a factor of 2.17 on every liability, unresolvable from the paper. Norway top: paper 1.0% flat vs actual 2022 1.1% above NOK 20M (about EUR 1.9M) — understates the top by 0.1pp — and 0.95% below NOK 20M — overstates by 0.05pp. NOK 1.7M at 2022 exchange rates is roughly EUR 160-170k, so Table A.2's EUR 160,000 is a fair approximation.

**Suggested question.** For Sim. CH, did you apply the Zurich statutory scale alone (top 0.3%) or multiply by the cantonal-plus-communal Steuerfuss (top about 0.65%)? And given that the real French IFI caps liability at 75% of income and abates the main residence 30%, would you re-state the comparability footnote and tell us how Sim. FR would change with those two features?

- **internal verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** All paper-side premises verify exactly: the CH simulation is described only as 'the structure of the Canton of Zurich, applying a progressive schedule with reduced rates' at 70% market valuation (p.10), with no rate, threshold, or multiplier anywhere (Table A.2, p.40: 'Cantonal thresholds / Progressive (low rates)'; the 0.1%-1% range on p.5 is generic background, not the simulated schedule); NO is 'a flat 1% rate... adjusting housing values to 25%' (p.10) with A.2's '(0.3% + 0.7%)' split and EUR 160,000 threshold; FR carries the internal 800k (p.5, and p.10 attributes 'above 800,000EUR' to the ISF) vs 1.3M (Table A.2, slides) ambiguity with no statement of which was implemented; and the 'no analogue' sentence (p.10) is quoted verbatim; Table 3 figures (CH 4,842.96 MEUR / 12,400,000; NO 15,456.06; FR 1,444.10) verified on the page render. The statutory-deviation half (Zurich Steuerfuss x~2.17, Norway 2022 0.95%/1.1% rates and secondary dwellings at 100%, IFI 75% cap, 30% abatement, decote) rests on external statutes not checkable under this lens, though the finder's arithmetic on its own premises is correct (3 per mille x 2.17 = 6.51 per mille). One overstatement: 'omits the IFI's 30% main-residence abatement and decote' is an inference from silence — p.10 claims the microdata were 'adapted to reflect as accurately as possible the valuation, exemption, and computation rules' of each legislation and p.5 lists 'exemptions for the primary residence' as an IFI feature, so the paper never documents whether these were implemented rather than demonstrably omitting them.
  - *Corrected claim:* The Swiss simulation is parameter-free in the paper (no schedule, threshold, or cantonal/communal multiplier is ever given, so whether the top rate is 0.3% or ~0.65% cannot be determined); the Norwegian and French designs as DESCRIBED conflict with the finder's cited 2022 statutes (external verification required), and internally the paper never resolves whether Sim. FR uses the 800k (p.5) or 1.3M (Table A.2/slides) threshold, never documents whether the IFI's main-residence relief or decote were implemented despite claiming rules were reflected 'as accurately as possible' (p.10), and is ambiguous between 'primary residences' (p.5) and all 'housing values' (p.10) for the 25% Norwegian valuation; the 'no analogue' justification for dropping the 60% cap is unverifiable from the paper and contested externally for France's 75% plafonnement.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** Zurich: the ESTV Kantonsblatt ZH confirms the § 47 StG wealth-tax Grundtarif (0‰ zero bracket then ½‰ rising to '3 ‰ für Vermögensteile über' ~CHF 3.3M) and that 'Der Steuerfuss gilt gleichmässig für alle Steuerarten' with canton and municipalities each setting a Steuerfuss in percent of the einfache Staatssteuer; zh.ch confirms the cantonal Steuerfuss was 99% for 2022–23 (100% in 2003–21, so the finder's '~98%' is the 2024+ value) and the City of Zurich municipal 119%, so the ~2.2x ambiguity (0.30% vs ~0.65% top) is real, and the paper (p.10, Table A.2) indeed gives no schedule, threshold, or multiplier. Norway: skatteetaten.no's own 2022 page shows municipal 0.7% + state 0.25% (1.7M–20M) and 0.4% above NOK 20M (top 1.1%), while the 2023 page shows 0.7% + 0.3% = 1.0% — the paper's '0.3% + 0.7%' split matches 2023, not 2022, and omits the above-20M rate; skatteetaten's 2022 housing page confirms primary dwellings at 25% up to NOK 10M then 50% of the excess, but secondary dwellings at 95% in 2022 (not 100% — that is the 2023+ value), a small correction. France: impots.gouv.fr confirms the barème starting at 0.5% on 800k–1.3M up to 1.5% above 10M with liability triggered at 1.3M (service-public.gouv.fr F563), the 30% main-residence abatement, the 1.3–1.4M décote (17,500 − 1.25×P), and the plafonnement — IFI plus income taxes capped at 75% of revenues — so the paper's claim that Spain's joint cap 'has no analogue in the foreign systems' is factually wrong for France.
  - *Corrected claim:* As the finding states, except: in 2022 Norwegian secondary dwellings were valued at 95% of market value (100% only from 2023), and the Zurich cantonal Steuerfuss in 2022 was 99% (98% applies from 2024), making the combined city-of-Zurich multiplier 218% (factor 2.18).
- **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** The CH leg is incontestable: the paper's entire disclosure is 'the structure of the Canton of Zurich... a progressive schedule with reduced rates' at 70% of market value (p.10; Table A.2 p.40), and Swiss sources confirm the Zurich tariff is a basic per-mille scale (max 3 per mille 'einfache Staatssteuer') multiplied by cantonal plus municipal Steuerfuss (city of Zurich 119%), so the undisclosed multiplier decision changes every liability by a factor around 2.2. Skatteetaten confirms 2022 Norway was 0.7% municipal + 0.25% state (NOK 1.7M-20M) + 0.4% above NOK 20M — the paper's 'flat 1% (0.3% + 0.7%)' matches the 2023 split and omits the 1.1% top bracket that exists in BOTH years; Skatteetaten also confirms the 25% valuation is primary-dwelling-only up to NOK 10M (70% above, currently) with secondary dwellings at 100%, against the paper's blanket 'housing values to 25%'. impots.gouv.fr and service-public.gouv.fr confirm the IFI facts: scale from EUR 800k, liability at EUR 1.3M, decote 1.3-1.4M, 30% main-residence abatement, and a plafonnement at '75 % du total des revenus mondiaux nets' — so p.10's 'no analogue in the foreign systems' is factually false for France, and the paper's own 800k (p.5) vs 1.3M (Table A.2) ambiguity is verbatim. The authors' 'stylized/inspired' defense is undercut by their own intro ('fully replicate the current Norwegian, Swiss and French wealth taxes', p.2) and Sec. 4.2 ('as accurately as possible', p.10).
  - *Corrected claim:* Same finding, with the year framing adjusted: the paper's fn.3 (p.5) cites 2023 Norwegian/Swiss/French sources, so the authors can answer the 2022-vs-2023 label — but the deviations survive under either year (the 0.4% Norwegian state rate above NOK 20M exists in 2022 and 2023; secondary dwellings are at or near full value in both years, with the exact 2022 above-10M primary rate and secondary percentage not pinned to a primary 2022 source in this review); the Swiss parameter void and the false French 'no analogue' claim are year-independent.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** These simulations are deliberately stylized applications of the foreign structures to Spanish microdata — the abstract says designs 'inspired in' those taxes, and footnote 3 points to the 2023 sources we followed, which explains the 0.3%+0.7% split. We accept, however, that the paper must state the exact Zurich tariff we applied and whether cantonal and communal multipliers were included, and likewise disclose that our flat 1% for Norway abstracts from the 0.4% state rate above NOK 20 million and from the primary/secondary dwelling distinction; we will add these parameters and, where material, a sensitivity. On France, we will state precisely how the EUR 800,000 scale and EUR 1.3 million liability threshold were implemented, and we concede that the sentence claiming Spain's income cap 'has no analogue' abroad overstates the case given the IFI's 75% plafonnement — we will reword it, while noting that with Sim. FR raising only EUR 1.44 billion these refinements are second order for our cross-design conclusions.


### [CONFIRMED] The cadastral-to-market ratio is printed with two different values on the same page, its formula is not computable as written, and a single national scalar contradicts how cadastral values are institutionally set
*id `cadastral-ratio` · would change conclusions: medium · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** Equation A.1 prints R = 3.0838 while the explanatory bullet directly below says the ratio 'result[s] in a value of 3.1125' (the slides use x3.08) — a 0.93% discrepancy that changes every property value and every threshold-crossing count. As written the formula is also not computable: the numerator sums market values 'according to the EFF' while the denominator sums cadastral values 'in the Household Panel', yet both carry the same property index i and a single N, so the actual procedure (which totals, whose weights, which property universes) cannot be reconstructed; its provenance is an unpublished doctoral dissertation (Petrov 2024). More fundamentally, Spanish cadastral values are set per municipality at 50% of market value at each revision (coefficient RM = 0.5) and then drift with local prices, with statutory update coefficients that depend on the year each municipality's ponencia de valores entered into force — so true cadastral/market ratios vary systematically by municipality and revision vintage, and one uniform 3.08 overstates wealth where revisions are recent and understates it in high-appreciation markets like Madrid (the empirical dispersion magnitude is UNVERIFIED, but a mean of 3.08 against the 2.0 set at revision implies large average drift and therefore large vintage differences).

**Paper evidence.** Eq. A.1, p.39: 'R = sum V_i^market / sum V_i^cadastral = 3.0838' with bullet: 'R represents the relationship between the market value and the cadastral value of real estate, resulting in a value of 3.1125'; V_i^market 'according to the EFF (2022 wave)', V_i^cadastral 'in the Household Panel (2022 wave)' (verified visually on the page render, source/pages/paper-41.png). Slides: 'Real estate revalued from cadastral to market value using EFF ratio (x3.08)'. Sec. 4.2, p.9: baseline uses 'the value declared' under the highest-of rule — so which valuation concept feeds each scenario's base versus the ranking variable is also unstated.

**External evidence.** BOE Orden de 14 octubre 1998 (https://www.boe.es/buscar/doc.php?id=BOE-A-1998-24160): 'se aplicara un coeficiente de relacion al mercado (RM) de 0,5 al valor individualizado resultante de la Ponencia de valores'. Catastro (https://www.catastro.hacienda.gob.es/es-ES/procedimientos_valoracion_colectiva.html): update coefficients apply 'en funcion del ano de entrada en vigor de la correspondiente ponencia de valores del municipio' — i.e., cadastral-market alignment is managed per municipality by revision vintage.

**Arithmetic.** 3.1125 - 3.0838 = 0.0287; 0.0287 / 3.0838 = 0.93% discrepancy in every adjusted property value. At revision, market/cadastral = 1/0.5 = 2.0; the estimated national mean of 3.08 implies average drift of 54% (3.08/2.0), which can only arise from stale vintages with heterogeneous local appreciation.

**Suggested question.** Which ratio did the simulations actually use — 3.0838 or 3.1125 — and how is R computed given that the numerator is EFF data and the denominator is PH data? Have you tested region- or vintage-specific ratios, since a uniform scalar will misclassify who crosses the EUR 300,000, EUR 1.3M, and Piketty thresholds, and Sim. FR's base is entirely real estate?

- **internal verifier — CONFIRMED.** Visually verified on printed p.39 (paper-41.png): Eq. A.1 prints 'R = ... = 3.0838' and the first bullet directly beneath says 'resulting in a value of 3.1125'; the discrepancy is 0.0287, i.e. 0.93% of 3.0838 (recomputed), and the slides say 'EFF ratio (x3.08)'. The non-computability premise holds: numerator and denominator share one property index i and a single N while V_i^market is 'according to the EFF (2022 wave)' and V_i^cadastral is 'in the Household Panel (2022 wave)' — two different datasets whose records cannot share i — and the only stated justification ('the EFF displays similar mean, median, and density functions to those of the Household Panel') cannot rescue the formula as written; provenance is Petrov (2024), listed in the references as a doctoral dissertation. The uniform-scalar critique has direct internal support the finder undersold: the paper itself concedes (pp.7-8) that cadastral undervaluation is worst 'in areas with strong real estate appreciation or infrequent cadastral updates', then applies one national R anyway; the RM=0.5/vintage institutional mechanism is external (BOE/Catastro, not re-verified here, empirical dispersion flagged UNVERIFIED by the finder), with correct conditional arithmetic (3.08/2.0 = 54% average drift). The p.9 'value declared' quote for the baseline is also accurate, so the which-concept-feeds-what question is well-posed.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** The internal discrepancy is verified visually on the page render of printed p.39: Eq. A.1 prints 'R = ΣV_i^market / ΣV_i^cadastral = 3.0838' while the bullet directly beneath says 'resulting in a value of 3.1125' (0.93% apart, recomputed), the slides use '×3.08', and the formula genuinely shares one property index i and one N across an EFF numerator and a PH denominator, so the procedure is not reconstructible as written; Petrov (2024) is listed in the references as a doctoral dissertation (Universidad de Alcalá). The institutional claims check out against primary sources: BOE Orden of 14 October 1998 fixes 'un coeficiente de relación al mercado (RM) de 0,5' applied to the ponencia value (cadastral = 50% of market at revision), and the Catastro/Hacienda page confirms update coefficients apply 'en función del año de entrada en vigor de la correspondiente ponencia de valores del municipio' — i.e., cadastral-market alignment is managed per municipality by revision vintage, so a national mean of ~3.08 (vs 2.0 at revision) mechanically implies heterogeneous drift that one uniform scalar cannot capture. The finder appropriately marked the empirical dispersion magnitude UNVERIFIED.
- **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** Verified visually on the p.39 render (paper-41.png): Equation A.1 prints '= 3.0838' and the bullet directly beneath says 'resulting in a value of 3.1125' — a 0.93% contradiction on the same page (0.0287/3.0838), with the slides using 'x3.08'. The formula is indeed not computable as written: V_i^market is 'according to the EFF (2022 wave)' while V_i^cadastral is 'in the Household Panel (2022 wave)' under a single property index i and one N, so the matching procedure cannot be reconstructed, and its source (Petrov 2024) is listed as a Universidad de Alcala doctoral dissertation (p.36). The uniform-scalar critique is strengthened by the paper's own Sec. 4.1 (pp.7-8), which concedes cadastral undervaluation is worst 'in areas with strong real estate appreciation or infrequent cadastral updates' — acknowledging exactly the vintage heterogeneity a single national R ignores. The notation in Equation A.1 is admittedly loose: the numerator and denominator are totals computed separately in the EFF and the PH, matched at the distribution level following Petrov (2024), which shows the two sources have similar means, medians and densities precisely to justify this — we will rewrite the formula so the procedure is reproducible. We already flag in Section 4.1 that cadastral staleness varies across areas, and we agree that municipality- or vintage-specific ratios are the natural refinement; note, though, that the Spanish IP baseline uses the declared values in the register (p.9), so the scalar mainly affects the foreign designs and the ranking variable, and we will report how sensitive the threshold-crossing counts are to reasonable regional variation in R.

The internal filer/taxpayer gap of just 4 weighted individuals is irreconcilable with footnote 9's claim that fully rebated filers 'such as in Madrid and Andalusia' are excluded, since AEAT shows 45,264 zero-cuota declarants in 2022 — so either the PH's M714 module or the authors' filter is missing essentially all fully-rebated and other zero-cuota declarants (and possibly non-resident 'obligacion real' declarants, who are in AEAT's totals but outside a resident household panel — UNVERIFIED). This matters most for Sim. 2 and Sim. 3, whose revenue comes precisely from un-rebating Madrid and Andalusia filers, and whose business assets appear in the PH only via IP declarations (p.8).

**Paper evidence.** Table 3, p.21: IP row Ntp = 170,404, T = 1,125.58 MEUR; Table A.1, p.38: 'Wealth Tax filers 170,408' (weighted); fn. 9, p.20: 'Individuals whose tax returns result in a positive effective tax liability after applying regional deductions and allowances are considered taxpayers. Those whose entire liability is fully rebated—such as in Madrid and Andalusia in the 2022 fiscal year—are excluded.'; p.8: 'only those who file the Wealth Tax have their business assets from self-employment recorded in the PH'.

AEAT publishes the full tables at sede.agenciatributaria.gob.es (2022 tabulation page fetched; structure confirmed, national and by-CCAA tables).

Revenue: 1,250 - 1,125.58 = 124.42 MEUR -> 10.0% below actual cuota. Zero-cuota declarants in AEAT: 230,365 - 185,101 = 45,264; in the paper: 170,408 - 170,404 = 4.

The AEAT-side anchors (230,365 declarants; 185,101 positive-cuota; EUR 1,250M; 45,264 zero-cuota) are external and not checkable under this lens — the headline gap percentages stand or fall with them — though the finder's arithmetic on those numbers is correct (26.0%, 7.9%, 10.0%, 45,264), and the non-resident caveat is properly flagged UNVERIFIED.

The near-zero gap between filers and effective taxpayers is a fair catch: we will verify whether the PH Wealth Tax module as delivered contains the fully rebated Madrid and Andalusia declarations or only positive-liability returns, and reword footnote 9 accordingly. ### [CONFIRMED] The data silently exclude the Basque Country and Navarre — 6% of Spain's population and two operating foral wealth taxes — while the paper presents results as 'Spain'
*id `foral-exclusion` · would change conclusions: medium · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** The PH covers only the Territorio de Regimen Fiscal Comun, explicitly defined by the IEF as the national territory minus the Basque Country and Navarre, and the paper's own weighted total of 44,857,347 individuals matches that universe (within 0.6%) rather than Spain's 47.4M — yet the words Basque, Navarre, foral, or common fiscal territory appear nowhere in the paper or slides. All headline numbers presented as national — EUR 15.5bn under Sim. NO, EUR 12.7bn under Sim. 3, the child-benefit amounts, '44.9 million individuals' — therefore omit about 6% of the population, including two of Spain's wealthiest regions, which levy their own wealth taxes under foral regimes with different thresholds. This is a scope limitation, not an error, but for a paper whose central theme is regional harmonization of wealth taxation, leaving two自-governing wealth-tax systems out of 'Spain' without a word is a disclosure failure.

**Paper evidence.** Table A.1, p.38: Individuals, weighted 44,857,347. Slides: 'Only 170,404 effective taxpayers out of 44.9 million individuals (2022)'. Grep of the full paper and slides text: zero matches for 'Vasco', 'Basque', 'Navarr', 'foral', or 'common fiscal territory'.

**External evidence.** IEF DT 5/2024 (https://www.ief.es/docs/destacados/publicaciones/documentos_trabajo/2024_05.pdf), footnote: 'a nivel del Territorio de Regimen Fiscal Comun (territorio nacional menos Pais Vasco y Navarra)'. INE Cifras de Poblacion 1 January 2022 (https://www.ine.es/prensa/cp_e2022_p.pdf): Spain 47,432,805; Pais Vasco 2,177,271; Navarra 659,232.

**Arithmetic.** 47,432,805 - 2,177,271 - 659,232 = 44,596,302 (common-territory population). Paper weighted total 44,857,347 is +0.59% vs that and -5.43% vs national. Excluded population: 2,836,503 = 5.98% of Spain.

**Suggested question.** Your weighted population is 44.9 million — the common fiscal territory, excluding the Basque Country and Navarre, which have their own wealth taxes. Shouldn't the paper state this scope explicitly, and how would including the foral regimes (or at least rescaling) change the national revenue figures and the harmonization narrative?

- **internal verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** The internal premises are fully confirmed: Table A.1 (p.38, visually verified) reports 44,857,347 weighted individuals; the slides say 'Only 170,404 effective taxpayers out of 44.9 million individuals (2022)'; and a grep of both paper.txt and slides.txt run this session returns zero matches for Vasco/Basque/Navarr/foral/'common fiscal'/'régimen fiscal' — the scope is never disclosed while the title, abstract, and all framing present results as Spain. The decisive identification of 44.86M with the Territorio de Régimen Fiscal Común rests on external sources (the IEF documentation and INE population figures) that cannot be checked under this lens, so the finding is not decidable from the paper alone; the arithmetic on those external figures is correct (47,432,805 - 2,177,271 - 659,232 = 44,596,302; paper total +0.59% vs that, -5.43% vs national; excluded 2,836,503 = 5.98%). The 'scope limitation, not an error' framing is appropriately calibrated if the external premise holds — and nothing internal contradicts it, since AEAT-sourced data and the ~44.9M total are consistent with a common-territory universe.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** The IEF's own documentation of the exact dataset (DT 5/2024, downloaded) contains the footnote: 'a nivel del Territorio de Régimen Fiscal Común (territorio nacional menos País Vasco y Navarra) dado que en la Agencia Estatal de Administración Tributaria no se tiene información de los contribuyentes de las haciendas forales' — the PH structurally excludes the two foral territories. INE's Cifras de Población press release (downloaded PDF) gives 1 Jan 2022 population: Spain 47,432,805; País Vasco 2,177,271; Navarra 659,232 — all three match the finding exactly, and the arithmetic recomputes: common territory 44,596,302, paper's weighted 44,857,347 is +0.59% vs that and −5.43% vs national, excluded share 5.98%. My grep of the full paper and slides returns zero matches for Vasco/Basque/Navarr/foral/común — the scope is nowhere disclosed. As a corroborating norm, even EUROMOD's Spain country report explicitly discloses that foral PIT rules are approximated, which this paper never does for a wealth tax where both foral regions run their own regimes.
- **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** Grep of both the paper and the slides returns zero matches for Basque/Vasco, Navarr-, foral, or common (fiscal) territory, while the slides present '170,404 effective taxpayers out of 44.9 million individuals' and the paper's Table A.1 (p.38) reports 44,857,347 weighted individuals. INE's Cifras de Poblacion at 1 January 2022 confirm Spain 47,432,805, Pais Vasco 2,177,271, Navarra 659,232; recomputed, the common territory is 44,596,302, so the paper's total sits +0.59% above that and -5.43% below the national figure, with 2,836,503 people (5.98% of Spain) excluded — the arithmetic identifies the PH universe as the common fiscal territory beyond reasonable doubt, matching the IEF's own DT 5/2024 description (finder's quote; ief.es direct fetch blocked by certificate error, existence confirmed by search). The authors would concede instantly and add a sentence; their genuine push-back — that foral wealth taxes are outside state legislative reach, so harmonization is naturally scoped to the common territory — reframes but does not remove the disclosure failure for headline 'Spain' revenue and population figures.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** The discussant is right and we will state it explicitly: the Household Panel is built from AEAT records and covers the common fiscal territory — the Basque Country and Navarre administer their own wealth taxes under the foral regimes and are outside AEAT's registers — which is exactly why our weighted population is 44.9 rather than 47.4 million. This is the standard scope of every Spanish tax-register study, but we agree the paper should say so in Section 4.1 and beneath the headline tables, and we will add it. On substance we would note that state legislation cannot reform the foral wealth taxes, so a harmonization exercise is properly defined on the common territory; the qualitative conclusions are unaffected, though we accept that figures labelled as national should carry the caveat and that the excluded regions are among Spain's wealthiest.

NO) — the same denominator across rows of Table 4, as it should be.

### [PLAUSIBLE] No existing open tool could have produced this study — EUROMOD's Spain model cannot simulate the IP and PolicyEngine has no Spain model — which makes publishing code the binding constraint on replicability
*id `open-tooling-gap` · would change conclusions: low · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** The JRC's official EUROMOD country report for Spain (2021-2024 systems) codes the Wealth Tax as included-but-not-simulated with the explicit reason 'Lack of data on wealth impedes simulation': EUROMOD runs on EU-SILC, where wealth is proxied crudely (financial assets imputed from investment income; secondary residences unobservable), so the standard open European microsimulation stack cannot reproduce any of this paper's eight scenarios. The JRC's EWIGE 2 project (2019) grafted HFCS wealth data onto EUROMOD for 17 EU countries including Spain, but it is a one-off research extension, not a maintained open model (whether it specifically simulates the Spanish IP is unconfirmed). PolicyEngine has no Spain model. The honest implication is that the authors' gated-register-plus-bespoke-code approach was necessary — and therefore that the reproducibility burden falls entirely on them: since the PH itself is free to any researcher who signs the IEF protocol, releasing the parameter tables, tax-calculator code, and index code (all standard: Kakwani, Suits, Reynolds-Smolensky, the Diaz et al. level/distance decomposition, and the Duran-Cabre & Esteller-More projection are cheap to recompute given microdata) — plus a small synthetic PH sample for testing — would make all eight scenarios re-runnable at essentially zero cost.

**Paper evidence.** The paper positions itself in the microsimulation literature (Sec. 4.2, p.10: 'their use in the context of wealth taxation remains comparatively limited. This study contributes to bridging that gap'); methodology equations 1-9 (pp.12-16) are all standard published indices; slides: 'Existing microsimulation evidence in terms of redistribution is limited.'

**External evidence.** EUROMOD Country Report Spain 2021-2024 (https://euromod-web.jrc.ec.europa.eu/sites/default/files/2025-04/Y15_CR_ES_final.pdf): Table 2.2 row 'Wealth tax / twl / I I I I / Lack of data on wealth impedes simulation'; 'The Wealth Tax is included in the micro-data as reported in SILC'; 'wealth-related information in EU-SILC is very limited... financial assets are proxied dividing the investment income... Secondary residences cannot be accounted for due to lack of data'; input data 'coming mainly from... EU-SILC'. JRC EWIGE 2 page (https://joint-research-centre.ec.europa.eu/reports-and-technical-documentation/ewige-2-update-and-extension-euromod-wealth-taxation-project_en): HFCS second wave integrated into EUROMOD, '17 EU countries (6 original from the pilot study and 11 new ones)', published 2019. PolicyEngine's country coverage (US, UK, Canada, Israel, Nigeria; no Spain) is internal knowledge of Max's own organization.

**Suggested question.** Given that EUROMOD explicitly cannot simulate the IP ('lack of data on wealth impedes simulation') and the PH is free to any researcher under the IEF protocol, your code is the only missing ingredient for full replicability — would you consider releasing the tax logic and index code, perhaps with a synthetic sample, so the community can build the open Spanish wealth-tax microsimulation capability that currently does not exist anywhere?

- **internal verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** The paper-side premises verify: Sec. 4.2 (p.10) states microsimulation's 'use in the context of wealth taxation remains comparatively limited. This study contributes to bridging that gap' (verbatim); the methodology consists entirely of standard published tools (Eqs. 1-9, pp.12-16: Kakwani, Suits, Reynolds-Smolensky, the Díaz et al. level/distance decomposition, the Durán-Cabré-Esteller-Moré long-run projection, and a logit), so re-implementation given data and parameters is indeed cheap; and the slides say 'Existing microsimulation evidence in terms of redistribution is limited.' The core claim — that EUROMOD's Spain model codes the IP as not simulable for lack of wealth data and that EWIGE 2 is a one-off — rests on JRC documents that cannot be checked under this internal-evidence lens (the paper cites EUROMOD only via Sutherland & Figari 2013 and never discusses its IP capability), so the finding is not decidable from the paper alone; PolicyEngine's lack of a Spain model is consistent with the organization's own documented country coverage (US/UK/Canada/Israel/Nigeria) available in this session's context. Nothing in the paper contradicts the finding, and its implication chain (bespoke code is the binding replicability constraint) coheres with the confirmed no-replication-package finding.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** The EUROMOD claims are verified verbatim from the JRC's Y15 Spain country report (downloaded): Table 2.2 codes 'Wealth tax / twl / I I I I / Lack of data on wealth impedes simulation' for 2021-2024, 'The Wealth Tax is included in the micro-data as reported in SILC', and 'wealth-related information in EU-SILC is very limited... financial assets are proxied dividing the investment income... Secondary residences cannot be accounted for due to lack of data', with input data 'coming mainly from... EU-SILC'. The EWIGE 2 claims are confirmed (JRC page: HFCS second wave, 2019, '17 EU countries (6 original from the pilot study and 11 new ones)', a one-off research report) — but the finder's hedge can now be resolved: the JRC117204 PDF (downloaded) contains a full Spain annex (A.17) with 'Net wealth tax rates, Spain' and 'Validation of simulated wealth tax revenues (in million euro), Spain', so EWIGE 2 DID simulate the Spanish IP, on stale HFCS survey data (Spain reference year 2010) in an unmaintained extension. This slightly softens the absolute 'no existing open tool could have produced this study' — the defensible version is that no maintained open model on adequate data can, which still makes code release the binding constraint. PolicyEngine's coverage (US, UK, Canada, Israel, Nigeria; no Spain) is the discussant's own organization's model roster.
  - *Corrected claim:* EUROMOD's maintained Spain model cannot simulate the IP ('Lack of data on wealth impedes simulation') and PolicyEngine has no Spain model, but EWIGE 2 (JRC, 2019) did add a Spanish net-wealth-tax simulation — including revenue validation tables — to EUROMOD on second-wave HFCS data (Spain reference year 2010); it remains a one-off, unmaintained research extension on stale survey data, so no maintained open tool on adequate data could have produced this study, and publishing code remains the binding constraint on replicability.

### What the verifiers flagged as missed by this lens
- **[internal]** Three internal items this lens missed, all verifiable from the paper's own tables: (1) The reported average tax rates t are inconsistent with Table A.1's implied aggregate wealth. Table A.1 gives mean net wealth EUR 160,539.8 across 44,857,347 weighted individuals, implying total net wealth of ~EUR 7.20 trillion; dividing each design's revenue T by that total gives rates systematically ~25-50% BELOW every printed t in Tables 3/5 (e.g., Sim. NO: 15,456.06M/7,201bn = 0.215% vs printed 0.29%; Sim. Piketty: 0.220% vs 0.30%; Sim. 3: 0.176% vs 0.24%; the gap factor is roughly constant ~1.3-1.35 across all eight designs), even though Table 5's note defines ts/r as 'total tax liabilities divided by total net wealth'. This implies the t denominators use a different wealth total (~EUR 5.3-5.4tn, plausibly unadjusted cadastral-valued wealth) than Table A.1's (plausibly market-adjusted) mean — never stated, and it sharpens the cadastral-ratio question of which valuation concept feeds each statistic. (2) The overlap matrix (Table A.8, p.47) shows IP∩Piketty = 166,827 < 170,404: about 3,577 current IP taxpayers would NOT pay under Piketty's design, which is documented (Tables 2 and A.2) as an exemption-free tax on all net wealth above EUR 200,000 — yet every IP payer has taxable wealth above the EUR 700k threshold, so the IP payer set should nest inside Piketty's. Pension exemptions stated on p.5 explain the analogous non-nesting for NO (162,546) and CH (167,326), but nothing documented explains Piketty's — a concrete pipeline question. (3) The slides conflate the paper's two headline concepts: the slides' data page says 'Wealth Tax filers: 170,404 (weighted)' while the paper defines 170,404 as effective TAXPAYERS (fn.9) and 170,408 as filers (Table A.1). Minor additional note: Eq. A.1's stated feasibility rationale ('the EFF displays similar mean, median, and density functions to those of the Household Panel') is self-undermining as written — if the two value distributions were similar the ratio would be ~1, not 3.08; the sentence presumably means similar population coverage, which the paper should say.
- **[external]** Four items surfaced during verification that the lens missed: (1) The paper is under journal review — a Research Square preprint of the identical manuscript (rs-9834492, posted 5 June 2026) shows submission to International Tax and Public Finance on 27 May 2026, and that preprint's declarations are empty (no data/code availability statement there either); this gives Max a concrete, constructive hook — Springer journals request availability statements, so committing to parameters+code now costs the authors nothing later. (2) Prior art for external validation exists in the open-model literature the paper ignores: EWIGE 2's Spain annex includes 'Table A.17.8 Validation of simulated wealth tax revenues (in million euro), Spain' — even the 2019 HFCS-EUROMOD extension benchmarked simulated Spanish IP revenue against official figures, which this register-based paper never does. (3) A slides-vs-paper inconsistency to add to the version-control cluster: the authors' own IARIW slides state 'Wealth Tax filers: 170,404 (weighted)' while paper Table A.1 says 170,408. (4) The EUROMOD Spain country report explicitly discloses that País Vasco and Navarra PIT rules 'are assumed to be the same as in the other regions' — showing that disclosing foral-territory treatment is a standard practice in Spanish microsimulation documentation, which strengthens the foral-exclusion disclosure critique. Also note for the discussant's page references: the supplied PNG mapping is off by one in places (printed p.14 is paper-16.png, not paper-15.png; Eq. A.1/printed p.39 is paper-41.png).
- **[advocate]** Four data-side items the lens missed, all usable by Max: (1) The paper touts the PH's 2016-2023 longitudinal structure ('The panel structure allows tracking households over time', p.7; '2016-2023 period', p.3) but uses only the 2022 cross-section — none of the panel dimension is exploited, so the data pitch oversells the design actually used. (2) Table A.8 (p.47) contains unexplained overlap anomalies that point at inconsistent wealth concepts across scenarios: IP-and-Sim.NO overlap is 162,546 and IP-and-Piketty is 166,827, i.e., ~7,900 and ~3,600 weighted CURRENT IP taxpayers (base above EUR 700k) would owe zero under near-universal designs with EUR 160k/200k thresholds — only explicable if the scenario bases use different wealth concepts (declared IP wealth vs market-adjusted reconstructed wealth, pensions, valuation discounts), which the paper never reconciles; this is a concrete question Max can ask. (3) The authors' slides claim a wealth-tax-financed child benefit 'could cut disposable income inequality by 4-5 Gini points' — Table 4 (p.25) actually shows 4.2-4.9 PERCENT reductions (0.3779 to 0.3595-0.3620, i.e., 1.6-1.8 Gini points on the 0-100 scale): a percent-vs-points conflation in their own conference slides. (4) The paper is also posted as a Research Square preprint (rs-9834492/v1), implying a journal submission is underway — useful leverage for the replication-package ask, since the target journal will require a data-availability statement. Also supporting: Sec. 4.1 (pp.7-8) itself concedes cadastral staleness varies with 'infrequent cadastral updates', which is internal ammunition for the uniform-ratio critique in the cadastral-ratio finding.

### Sources this lens fetched or saw
- [Skatteetaten — wealth tax rates, income year 2022](https://www.skatteetaten.no/en/rates/wealth-tax/?year=2022) — Norway 2022: municipal 0.7%; state 0.25% on NOK 1,700,001-20,000,000 and 0.4% above NOK 20M (total 0.95%/1.1%); tax-free threshold NOK 1.7M single, 3.4M married — versus the paper's flat 1% (0.3%+0.7%) with no top bracket.
- [Skatteetaten — taxable value of residential properties](https://www.skatteetaten.no/en/person/taxes/get-the-taxes-right/property-and-belongings/houses-property-and-plots-of-land/tax-value/taxable-value-of-residential-properties/) — Primary dwellings valued at 25% of market value only up to NOK 10M with the excess at a higher rate; secondary dwellings at 100% — versus the paper's blanket 25% housing valuation.
- [Service-Public.gouv.fr — IFI (fiche F563)](https://www.service-public.gouv.fr/particuliers/vosdroits/F563) — IFI liability triggers above EUR 1,300,000 of net taxable real estate; the main residence gets a 30% abatement (abatement absent from the paper's Sim. FR description).
- [Fondation de France — Calculer son IFI](https://ifi.fondationdefrance.org/calculer-ifi) — IFI bareme starts at EUR 800,000 (0.5% to 1.5% above 10M); decote for 1.3-1.4M; plafonnement caps IFI plus income taxes at 75% of worldwide net income — contradicting the paper's claim that Spain's cap 'has no analogue in the foreign systems'.
- [IEF Documentos de Trabajo 5/2024 — Panel de hogares: Ejercicio 2022](https://www.ief.es/docs/destacados/publicaciones/documentos_trabajo/2024_05.pdf) — PH is a joint INE-AEAT-IEF product; five researcher products; file inventory includes M714_2022 (Wealth Tax declaration variables) and PATRIMONIO2022 (informative models + Catastro); 2022 wave has over 1M households / 2.9M persons; coverage is the Territorio de Regimen Fiscal Comun, defined as 'territorio nacional menos Pais Vasco y Navarra'.
- [IEF — Panel de Hogares product page](https://www.ief.es/investigacion/Est_panelHogares.vbhtml) — Waves 2016-2023 exist (2023: 1.19M households, 3.55M individuals); real-estate module carries cadastral values; data requested via the 'Protocolo de peticion de datos' link.
- [IEF — Protocolo de Peticion de Datos Fiscales](https://www.ief.es/Investigacion/Est_peticion.vbhtml) — Access conditions: free of charge for researchers and analysts; requires signing a protocol governing receipt and use of anonymized tax microdata files — i.e., delivered files, not on-site-only access.
- [INAP news — release of the PH datasets](https://laadministracionaldia.inap.es/noticia.asp?id=1513027) — July 2022 public release of the PH (2016-2019) by AEAT, INE and IEF; the 2019 wave had 950,000 households — the figure the paper's text mistakenly attaches to 2022.
- [Infobae/EFE — AEAT 2022 Wealth Tax statistics](https://www.infobae.com/espana/agencias/2024/09/04/los-espanoles-mas-ricos-declararon-un-patrimonio-medio-de-375-millones-en-2022/) — AEAT Estadistica IP 2022: 230,365 declarants; 185,101 with positive cuota; EUR 1,250M cuota a ingresar; EUR 864,940M declared wealth; 2022 was the first ITSGF year — the external benchmark the paper never reports.
- [AEAT — Estadistica IP 2022 tabulation page](https://sede.agenciatributaria.gob.es/AEAT/Contenidos_Comunes/La_Agencia_Tributaria/Estadisticas/Publicaciones/sites/patrimonio/2022/docf6fe66e200d44af358751b933ce68845be65a882a.html) — The official 2022 IP statistics exist with national and by-autonomous-community tables (page structure; detailed figures live in the interactive tables).
- [BOE-A-1998-24160 — Orden de 14 de octubre de 1998 (coefficient RM)](https://www.boe.es/buscar/doc.php?id=BOE-A-1998-24160) — Cadastral values are set with a market-reference coefficient RM = 0.5 — i.e., 50% of market value at the time of each municipal revision, the institutional anchor for cadastral/market ratio drift by vintage.
- [Catastro — procedimientos de valoracion colectiva](https://www.catastro.hacienda.gob.es/es-ES/procedimientos_valoracion_colectiva.html) — Cadastral values are set per municipality via ponencias de valores, and statutory update coefficients depend on the year each municipality's ponencia entered into force — so cadastral/market alignment varies by municipality, contradicting a single national ratio.
- [JRC — EWIGE 2 (EUROMOD Wealth Taxation Project)](https://joint-research-centre.ec.europa.eu/reports-and-technical-documentation/ewige-2-update-and-extension-euromod-wealth-taxation-project_en) — The only EUROMOD wealth-tax capability is a 2019 research extension integrating HFCS data for 17 EU countries including Spain — a one-off project, not a maintained open model.
- [EUROMOD Country Report Spain 2021-2024 (Y15)](https://euromod-web.jrc.ec.europa.eu/sites/default/files/2025-04/Y15_CR_ES_final.pdf) — EUROMOD Spain runs on EU-SILC; Table 2.2 codes the Wealth Tax 'I' (taken from data, not simulated) with the comment 'Lack of data on wealth impedes simulation'; EU-SILC wealth information is 'very limited' (financial assets proxied from investment income, secondary residences unobservable).
- [EQUALITAS Working Paper 95 PDF](https://equalitas.es/sites/default/files/2026-04/WP-95.pdf) — The published version matches the reviewed paper exactly and contains no replication package, code link, or data/code availability statement.
- [INE — Cifras de Poblacion, 1 January 2022](https://www.ine.es/prensa/cp_e2022_p.pdf) — Spain 47,432,805 residents; Pais Vasco 2,177,271; Navarra 659,232 — which shows the paper's weighted 44,857,347 individuals equals the common fiscal territory (national minus the two foral regions), not Spain.
- [Neho.ch — Vermogenssteuer Kanton Zurich (secondary source)](https://neho.ch/de/blog/vermogenssteuer-zurich) — Canton Zurich wealth tax: allowances CHF 80,000/159,000; statutory progressive scale 0.5-3 per mille; Steuerfuss multipliers (canton about 98%, Zurich city 119%) that roughly double statutory rates — none of which the paper reports for its Sim. CH.


## Lens: Design of the simulations

**Lens summary.** The eight scenarios are a clean and genuinely useful design-comparison grid, but the design of the exercise has four load-bearing weaknesses. First, the '2022 baseline' is not 2022 law: the ITSGF accrued on 31 December 2022 (Ley 38/2022, verified in the BOE), raised EUR 623M — about half of Sim. 2's incremental revenue — and by 2023 had induced Madrid to end its rebate, so the paper's flagship 'remove regional rebates' reform is partly the status quo; relatedly, Sim. 2's own description ('harmonised', EUR 700k) is contradicted by the overlap matrix, which shows no baseline taxpayer dropping out despite three regions' 500k thresholds. Second, the paper appears to tax declared (cadastral-anchored) values in the IP baseline but EFF market values (x3.08) in the foreign/Piketty designs — with the slides flatly contradicting the paper — so an unknown share of the headline cross-design revenue gaps is a valuation-base change rather than 'breadth of base'. Third, the long-run equivalized-Gini results contain a demonstrable mechanical artefact: under uniform growth the reported +1.04-point rise at T=25 under the baseline is roughly six times larger than the maximum movement the tax could generate given the paper's own concentration indices, and in the wrong direction, so the 'inequality rises for decades' finding should not survive scrutiny as stated. Fourth, the treatment of the 60% IRPF–IP cap is inconsistent (excluded from France on a factually wrong premise, unspecified for Sims 1–3 and Piketty) even though the cited literature finds this cap is the single largest behavioural leakage in the Spanish tax. The static, fixed-population assumption is honestly disclosed, but its bias is scenario-specific in ways that could reorder the designs, and the foreign 'replications' are stylised in directions that mostly understate Norway's top-end strength. None of this destroys the paper's central qualitative message — incidence and base breadth dominate rate schedules — but the quantitative comparisons need re-labelling, one table likely needs correcting, and the baseline needs to be reconciled with the law actually in force in 2022.

### [CONFIRMED] The '2022 baseline' omits the ITSGF, a tax legally in force for tax year 2022 — and ~49% of Sim. 2's incremental revenue is therefore already being collected under actual law
*id `itsgf-baseline` · would change conclusions: high · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** The paper dates the ITSGF as 'applicable on 2023' (p.5) and 'introduced in December 2022 for 2023–2024' (fn.2, p.5), and excludes it from the baseline. Both datings are wrong: Ley 38/2022 entered into force 29 Dec 2022 with devengo on 31 December of each year, so the first accrual was 31 Dec 2022 — tax year 2022, the paper's own baseline year — and RDL 8/2023 extended it indefinitely. The ITSGF is precisely a state top-up that neutralizes regional rebates above ~EUR 3.7M (crediting IP paid), so Sim. 2 ('remove regional rebates') is partly already law: ITSGF collections for devengo 2022 were EUR 623M (12,010 taxpayers, EUR 555M from Madrid), i.e. 48.7% of Sim. 2's incremental revenue of EUR 1,279.6M. By 2023 the margin closed further: AEAT attributes 90.6% of the EUR +676.3M jump in IP receipts to Madrid ending its 100% rebate (ITSGF receipts fell to EUR 35M), so the paper's flagship 'feasible reform' is to a large extent describing the status quo for large fortunes.

**Paper evidence.** p.5: 'in December 2022, the central Government approved an additional Wealth Tax... (Impuesto Temporal sobre las Grandes Fortunas, ITSGF) applicable on 2023 as a state-level surcharge to offset regional rebates above €3.7 million net wealth, crediting any IP paid. Note that our baseline is the 2022 IP framework while the state solidarity surcharge (ITSGF) is not included in our simulations.' Footnote 2 (p.5): 'The ITSGF, introduced in December 2022 for 2023–2024... is not modelled here to preserve comparability with foreign systems and pre-reform incidence.' Also an internal inconsistency on the baseline year itself: p.9 says 'the benchmark is the legislation applicable to fiscal year 2023' while its own footnote 6 says 'fiscal year 2022' and Table 1 (p.11) says 'Spanish Wealth Tax (2022)'. Table 3 (p.21): IP revenue 1,125.58M; Sim. 2 revenue 2,405.17M. Limitations (p.34): 'Future research could incorporate the new state-level solidarity tax'. (The official name is also Impuesto Temporal de Solidaridad de las Grandes Fortunas, not 'sobre las Grandes Fortunas'.)

**External evidence.** FETCHED https://www.boe.es/buscar/act.php?id=BOE-A-2022-22684 (Ley 38/2022 consolidated): 'El impuesto se devengará el 31 de diciembre de cada año' (Art. 3.Diez); entry into force 29/12/2022; 'aplicable en los dos primeros ejercicios en los que se devengue a partir de la fecha de su entrada en vigor' (Art. 3.Veintiocho) => first ejercicio = 2022; prorogued by RDL 8/2023 'en tanto no se produzca la revisión de la tributación patrimonial'; scale 1.7%/2.1%/3.5% above EUR 3M with EUR 700k mínimo exento; IP quota credited. FETCHED https://www.lamoncloa.gob.es/serviciosdeprensa/notasprensa/hacienda/paginas/2023/200923-impuesto-solidaridad-grandes-fortunas.aspx: EUR 623M, 12,010 declarants, Madrid 10,302 contributors / EUR 555M (first filing campaign, July 2023). FETCHED https://sede.agenciatributaria.gob.es/static_files/Sede/Tema/Estadisticas/Estadisticas_impuesto/Irpf_patrimonio/Patrimonio/Documentacion/Infografia.pdf (AEAT, parsed with pdftotext): 2023 IP receipts 1,926.3M, '676,3 millones de euros más que en el ejercicio anterior, explicado el 90,6% del incremento por la eliminación de la bonificación aprobada por la Comunidad de Madrid'; ITSGF (modelo 718): 35M in 2023 'frente a los 630 millones del ejercicio anterior'.

**Arithmetic.** Sim.2 incremental revenue = 2,405.17 − 1,125.58 = EUR 1,279.59M. ITSGF devengo-2022 share of it: 623/1,279.59 = 48.7% (630/1,279.59 = 49.2% using AEAT's figure). Madrid share of ITSGF: 555/623 = 89.1%. Actual 2022 IP receipts implied by AEAT: 1,926.3 − 676.3 = EUR 1,250.0M, vs paper baseline 1,125.58M => paper baseline is 10.0% below actual collections. Actual 2023 IP+ITSGF = 1,926.3 + 35 = EUR 1,961.3M.

**Suggested question.** Ley 38/2022 sets the ITSGF devengo on 31 December of each year and entered into force on 29 December 2022, so it applied to tax year 2022 — your baseline year — and raised EUR 623M, about half of Sim. 2's incremental revenue; by 2023 Madrid had ended its rebate and IP receipts rose EUR 676M. Given that, should we read the IP baseline as a counterfactual 'pre-ITSGF world' rather than 2022 law, and how much of Sim. 2's and Sim. 3's gain survives once the ITSGF is netted out?

- **internal verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** Every internal premise verifies exactly. p.5: 'applicable on 2023' and the fn.2 wording are verbatim; the baseline-year inconsistency is real (p.9 text says 'the benchmark is the legislation applicable to fiscal year 2023' while its own fn.6 says 'fiscal year 2022', Table 1 p.11 says 'Spanish Wealth Tax (2022)', and the slides say 'as legislated in 2022'); Table 3 gives IP 1,125.58M and Sim.2 2,405.17M, so the incremental 1,279.59M and 623/1,279.59 = 48.7% check out; the limitations line (p.34) confirms the ITSGF is absent from all simulations. The name point is even corroborated internally: the paper's own bibliography entry (Martinez, Diaz & Marin 2022) is titled 'El Impuesto Temporal de Solidaridad de las Grandes Fortunas...', while the text (p.5) calls it 'Impuesto Temporal sobre las Grandes Fortunas' (the paper's English rendering 'Temporary Solidarity Tax on Large Fortunes' matches the official name, so the Spanish parenthetical dropped 'de Solidaridad'). However, the decisive facts - that Ley 38/2022's first devengo fell on 31 Dec 2022 (making 2022 the first accrual year), the EUR 623M/12,010-taxpayer collections, and AEAT's 2023 attribution to Madrid - are statutory/statistical facts outside the paper, which asserts the opposite dating; they cannot be decided from the paper alone, so from this lens the finding is plausible rather than confirmed, and nothing internal contradicts it.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** Every external claim checks out against primary sources I fetched. BOE consolidated Ley 38/2022 (in force 29/12/2022) sets devengo 'el 31 de diciembre de cada año' and applicability 'en los dos primeros ejercicios en los que se devengue a partir de la fecha de su entrada en vigor' — so the first ejercicio is 2022, the paper's baseline year, making the paper's 'applicable on 2023' / 'for 2023–2024' (p.5 and fn.2) wrong; the consolidated text also confirms the 1.7/2.1/3.5% scale above EUR 3M taxable (≈3.7M wealth with the 700k mínimo exento), the IP-quota credit, the 60% joint limit (Art. 3.Doce.1), and the indefinite prorogation 'en tanto no se produzca la revisión de la tributación patrimonial' (RDL 8/2023, confirmed via Garrigues/Pérez-Llorca notes). Moncloa (20 Sept 2023) confirms EUR 623M, 12,010 declarants, Madrid 10,302/EUR 555M for the first (July 2023) campaign, and AEAT's own Estadística Patrimonio 2023 infographic books '630 millones' of modelo-718 receipts to 'el ejercicio anterior' (=2022) versus 35M in 2023, plus '1.926,3 millones... 676,3 millones más que en el ejercicio anterior, explicado el 90,6%... por la eliminación de la bonificación... de Madrid' — all verbatim as claimed. I recomputed the arithmetic: 2,405.17−1,125.58=1,279.59M; 623/1,279.59=48.7%; 555/623=89.1%; implied actual 2022 IP receipts 1,926.3−676.3=1,250.0M, 10.0% above the paper's simulated baseline. Paper-side quotes and the 2022-vs-2023 baseline inconsistency (p.9 vs fn.6 vs Table 1) verified in the text; BOE also confirms the official name is 'de Solidaridad de las Grandes Fortunas', not the paper's 'sobre'. The only caveat is one the finding already flags: 623M measures ITSGF at its own scale above ~3.7M, so 48.7% quantifies overlap with Sim. 2, not identity.
- **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** All paper quotes verified (p.5 and fn.2 dating; p.9 'fiscal year 2023' vs fn.6 'fiscal year 2022' vs Table 1 '2022'; Table 3 revenues; p.34 limitation). I re-fetched the BOE consolidated text of Ley 38/2022 this session: 'El impuesto se devengara el 31 de diciembre de cada ano' (Art. 3.Diez), entry into force 29/12/2022, and 'aplicable en los dos primeros ejercicios en los que se devengue' (Art. 3.Veintiocho) — so the first accrual was 31 Dec 2022, the paper's own baseline year, and both of the paper's datings ('applicable on 2023'; 'for 2023–2024') are wrong; the BOE page also confirms the official name is 'de Solidaridad de las Grandes Fortunas' (the paper's own reference list, Martinez/Diaz/Marin entry, uses the correct name the body text gets wrong). Arithmetic recomputed: Sim.2 incremental = 2,405.17 − 1,125.58 = 1,279.59M; 623/1,279.59 = 48.7%. The paper's deliberate-exclusion defense (stated on p.5/fn.2/p.34) addresses none of this: the '2022 baseline' is in fact a pre-ITSGF counterfactual, and roughly half of Sim.2's headline incremental revenue was already being collected under 2022 law.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** We take the point on the dating and will correct it: the discussant is right that Ley 38/2022 entered into force on 29 December 2022 with accrual on 31 December, so the ITSGF's first ejercicio was 2022 and about 620–630 million euros of what Sim. 2 raises was already being collected on 2022 wealth. Our exclusion, however, was deliberate and stated three times — p.5, footnote 2, and the limitations — because we wanted a clean IP-only benchmark comparable with the foreign designs and with pre-reform incidence. We would push back on reading Sim. 2 as the status quo: the ITSGF only claws back above 3.7 million euros at 1.7% and up, while Sim. 2 removes rebates for every taxpayer, which is why it adds 31,283 payers, most of them below the ITSGF's reach, and Sim. 3 goes much further. In revision we will relabel the baseline as the pre-ITSGF 2022 framework, add an ITSGF scenario, and report Sim. 2 and Sim. 3 revenue net of what the ITSGF already collects.


### [CONFIRMED] Tax-base composition and valuation differ across scenarios without an asset-by-asset map
*id `valuation-base` · would change conclusions: high · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** Tables 1/A.2 label IP, NO, CH, Sims 1–3, and Piketty only as taxes on 'net wealth' and never publish an asset-by-asset base map. The paper states that pensions are exempt in Norway and Switzerland (p.5), but the methods never explain whether pension rights are present in the PH wealth measure or how the simulation removes them. Business assets are recorded only for IP filers (p.8), while liabilities are not decomposed, leaving their treatment across the foreign and Piketty designs unclear. The paper also states that baseline IP taxes 'the value declared' for real estate, while Norwegian and Swiss simulations tax 25% and 70% 'of their estimated market value' — market value being cadastral x~3.08 via the EFF ratio; the base used for Sim. FR, Sims 1–3 and Piketty is never stated. If the baseline and Spanish reforms tax declared values while NO/CH/Piketty tax market-derived values, then part of the cross-design revenue and incidence gaps (e.g. Piketty 15,861M vs Sim. 1 3,709M) reflects revaluation rather than design breadth. The authors' own slides contradict the paper by stating that ALL simulations revalue real estate to market value (x3.08), and the appendix prints two different values of the ratio itself (3.0838 in Eq. A.1 vs 3.1125 in the bullet below it).

**Paper evidence.** p.5: Norway's and Switzerland's pension assets are exempt. pp.7–8: the PH includes financial assets and liabilities; business assets from self-employment appear only for IP filers; no asset inventory or pension implementation follows. Table A.2 labels most bases only 'net wealth'. p.9: 'Under the IP, real estate must be valued at the highest of its cadastral, verified, or acquisition value. In the simulation of the current Spanish tax, this poses no difficulty, as the dataset records the value declared.' p.10: Norway 'adjusting housing values to 25% of their estimated market value'; Zurich 'valuing real estate at 70% of its estimated market value'. Nothing on the valuation base for Sim. FR, Sims 1–3, or Piketty (pp.10-11; Table A.2, p.40, is silent on valuation). Slides (p.8 of deck): 'All simulations use 2022 AEAT Household Panel microdata. Real estate revalued from cadastral to market value using EFF ratio (×3.08).' Appendix p.39 (Eq. A.1): 'R = ... = 3.0838' immediately followed by 'resulting in a value of 3.1125' (verified visually on the page render).

**External evidence.** FETCHED AEAT Infografía (URL above): among actual 2022–23 IP filers, declared real estate is only 18.5% of declared patrimonio while capital mobiliario is 75.9% — i.e. legal (declared) values suppress housing precisely in the segment the broad-base scenarios (NO/CH/Piketty, millions of new taxpayers from ~p67 up) would tax, where housing dominates portfolios. The x3.08 EFF-to-cadastral ratio is the paper's own measure of the wedge.

**Arithmetic.** If a household's real estate enters the baseline at cadastral V but enters NO/CH at 0.25 and 0.70 of 3.08V, the effective housing valuation relative to the IP base is 0.25×3.08 = 0.77V (NO) and 0.70×3.08 = 2.16V (CH) — so the Swiss design taxes housing on a base ~2.2x the Spanish legal base before any rate applies. Piketty on market values vs Sim. 1 on declared values would mechanically multiply the housing component of the base by ~3.08, i.e. much of the 15,861/3,709 = 4.3x revenue gap could be valuation, not schedule.

**Suggested question.** Could you publish an asset-by-asset map for every scenario: which property value enters, whether pension rights and business assets are included, which liabilities are netted, and which exemptions and caps apply? Then, as a decomposition, what does Piketty raise when run on the same asset and valuation base as the IP, so we can separate the base-definition effect from the rate-schedule effect?
