FR, Sims 1-3, or Piketty (the p.8 data section says EFF ratios approximate market values 'when interpreting wealth levels and their distribution', supporting the mixed-base reading without resolving the tax-base question). The slides contradiction is verbatim: 'All simulations use 2022 AEAT Household Panel microdata. Real estate revalued from cadastral to market value using EFF ratio (×3.08)' (Eight Simulated Designs slide). The appendix inconsistency is confirmed in both the text extraction and the page image of printed p.39: Eq. A.1 prints R = 3.0838 and the bullet directly beneath says 'resulting in a value of 3.1125'. Arithmetic verifies (0.25x3.08=0.77; 0.70x3.08=2.16; 15,860.95/3,709.39=4.28x), so the conditional inference - that if bases differ, part of the cross-design gap is revaluation of housing rather than design - follows and bears directly on the p.22 conclusion quoted; only the supplementary AEAT 18.5%-housing-share figure is external and unverified here.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** All paper and slide quotes verified in the source files: p.9 'the dataset records the value declared' for the IP simulation; p.10 NO at '25% of their estimated market value' and Zurich at '70% of its estimated market value'; no valuation stated for Sim. FR, Sims 1–3 or Piketty anywhere including Table A.2 (p.40); slides p.8 'All simulations use 2022 AEAT Household Panel microdata. Real estate revalued from cadastral to market value using EFF ratio (×3.08)' — directly contradicting the paper's baseline description. The 3.0838 (Eq. A.1) vs 3.1125 (bullet below) inconsistency is confirmed visually on the printed-p.39 render. The external fact is confirmed from AEAT's own statistics infographic: among IP declarants, bienes inmuebles are 18.5% of declared patrimonio vs capital mobiliario 75.9% (note: this is the ejercicio-2023 statistics, the latest AEAT publishes in that infographic; the composition point is directional and robust). - **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** Every documentary element is verified in the sources: p.9 says the IP simulation taxes 'the value declared'; p.10 says NO and CH tax 25% and 70% 'of their estimated market value'; the paper and Table A.2 (p.40) are silent on the base for FR, Sims 1–3 and Piketty; the slides (deck p.8) state 'Real estate revalued from cadastral to market value using EFF ratio (×3.08)' for ALL simulations, contradicting p.9; and the appendix prints the ratio as 3.0838 in Eq. A.1 and 3.1125 in the bullet directly beneath it. The best author defense — legal valuation rules are part of design, and Norway's 25% is statutorily defined on market value — is coherent for NO/CH but cannot specify what Piketty and Sims 1–3 tax, cannot reconcile the slides, and leaves the headline 'breadth of the base' conclusion (p.22) unable to separate schedule effects from a ~3.08x housing revaluation. Arithmetic verified: 0.25×3.08=0.77, 0.70×3.08=2.16, Piketty/Sim.1 revenue ratio 15,860.95/3,709.39=4.3. The finding survives: the authors must concede the paper as written is not replicable on this point and that the same-base decomposition is needed.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** Our intention is that the wealth distribution — rankings, Lorenz curves, Ginis, and the denominators of effective rates — is always measured at EFF-adjusted market values, while each simulated tax applies its own statutory valuation rule: declared values for the IP and the Spanish reforms, and market-derived values with the 25% and 70% discounts for Norway and Zurich, because those percentages are defined in law on market values — applying them to cadastral values would itself be unfaithful. In that sense we regard valuation rules as part of design, and the cross-country comparison is the policy-relevant one. We concede, however, that the paper never states the base scenario by scenario, that Table A.2 should carry a valuation column, that the slide bullet saying all simulations revalue at 3.08 is a simplification that contradicts p.9, and that the 3.0838/3.1125 inconsistency in Equation A.1 must be fixed. The decomposition the discussant asks for — Piketty's schedule run on the declared-value base — is cheap and informative, and we will add it, because we agree that 'broaden the base' and 'assess housing at market value' are different policy messages.


### [CONFIRMED] The long-run rise in the equivalized net-wealth Gini (+1.04 pts by T=25 under the baseline) is mechanically impossible given the model's own assumptions — it must be a construction artefact
*id `equivalized-longrun-artefact` · would change conclusions: high · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** Table 5 (p.26) shows the equivalized net-wealth Gini rising from 0.6402 to 0.6506 at T=25 under the IP, and by +0.8 to +1.0 points under CH/FR/Sim.1/Sim.2, remaining above the initial level even at T=100 (IP: 0.6486). Under the stated projection — uniform constant growth g for everyone, fixed households, tax the only distributional force — the pre-tax distribution is scale-invariant (Gini unchanged by uniform compounding), and with the paper's own equivalized tax concentration index for IP (Ct = 0.9744, Table A.3) the cumulative tax of 0.50% at T=25 can move the Gini by at most ~0.17 points, downward. A rise of +1.04 points is therefore not producible by the tax; the paper's explanation — household size plus tax rates 'too small... to offset the natural growth of wealth at the top' (p.27) — contradicts its own assumption that growth is uniform (there is no differential 'natural growth at the top' to offset). The near-identical jump across designs whose cumulative tax rates differ by a factor of 4, and its absence in the individual-wealth panel, point to an error or undisclosed step in how the equivalized series is built in the projection.

**Paper evidence.** Table 5 bottom panel (p.26): Gpre 0.6402; Gpost at T=25: IP 0.6506, CH 0.6487, FR 0.6503, Sim.1 0.6485, Sim.2 0.6496 (vs NO 0.6392, Sim.3 0.6411, Piketty 0.6392); IP at T=100 still 0.6486 > 0.6402. Method (p.15): 'one in which households accumulate wealth at a constant return rate without taxation, and another in which the same accumulation process takes place, but wealth is reduced each year by a constant ts/r'; p.25-26: 'assumes a uniform and constant annual wealth accumulation rate'. Explanation offered (p.27): 'This reflects the role of household size introduced by equivalization and the fact that annual effective tax rates are still too small in the early decades to offset the natural growth of wealth at the top.' Table A.3 (p.41): IP equivalized Ct = 0.9744, K = 0.3342, short-run Gpost = 0.6402 (no change). Table A.7 (p.46): cumulative tl/r at T=25, g=2%: IP 0.50%, CH 2.16%, FR 0.62%, Sim.1 1.61%, Sim.2 1.10%. Top panel of Table 5 (individual wealth) shows no jump: IP 0.7538 -> 0.7528 at T=25.

**External evidence.** none (internal-consistency finding; scale-invariance verified by direct computation)

**Arithmetic.** Scale invariance verified numerically: Gini(w) = Gini(1.02^25 × w) to 6 decimals for a simulated distribution including negative values. Maximum tax-induced Gini fall at T=25 for IP ≈ tl/r/(1−tl/r) × K = 0.0050 × (0.9744 − 0.6402) ≈ 0.0017 (0.17 Gini points), versus an observed change of +0.0104 in the opposite direction — 6x larger than the maximum and sign-flipped. Same violation for CH (max fall 0.36 pts, observed +0.85), FR (0.22, +1.01), Sim.1 (0.52, +0.83), Sim.2 (0.38, +0.94), given their reported Ct of 0.80–0.99.

**Suggested question.** In the long-run projection every unit grows at the same g, so the no-tax equivalized Gini stays at 0.6402 forever, and with your reported tax concentration of 0.9744 the cumulative 0.5% tax at T=25 could move the Gini by at most about 0.2 points downward — yet Table 5 reports a rise of over 1 point under the baseline, and roughly the same +1 point under designs whose cumulative tax differs fourfold. Can you walk us through how the equivalized series is constructed in the projection, since household size is fixed and cannot generate this under the stated assumptions?

- **internal verifier — CONFIRMED.** Every cited number verifies against Table 5 (p.26; checked in text and page image), Table A.3 (p.41) and Table A.7 (p.46), and every quote is exact, including the p.27 explanation invoking 'the natural growth of wealth at the top' despite the framework's uniform constant g (p.26 and limitations p.34). Scale invariance was recomputed numerically (Gini identical to 8 decimals under x1.02^25 and x1.02^100, negative values included), and the RS arithmetic verifies: max tax-induced fall for IP = (0.0050/0.995)x0.3342 = 0.0017 vs observed +0.0104 (6.2x, sign-flipped), with CH/FR/Sim1/Sim2 as stated. The claim survives two stress tests beyond the finder's: (i) even allowing arbitrary re-ranking, any nonnegative tax removing 0.50% of aggregate wealth can raise a 0.6402 Gini by at most d(1+G)/(1-d) = +0.0082, so IP's +0.0104 exceeds the absolute mathematical bound (FR sits at the knife-edge: +0.0101 vs +0.0102), and the paper's own evidence forecloses re-ranking anyway (p.13: re-ranking 'very small' in all simulations; Table A.4 prints D = 0 for net wealth; Table A.3 shows short-run equivalized Gpost = Gpre exactly, so the household-size channel does nothing at T=1); (ii) the individual panel matches the RS prediction within 0.0006 in all eight scenarios, while every equivalized cell - including the falling NO/Sim3/Piketty cells - sits +0.012 to +0.019 above its RS-predicted value (NO predicted ~0.6217 vs reported 0.6392), so the entire equivalized long-run panel, not just the rising cells, is inconsistent with the stated construction. This is fully decidable from the paper plus arithmetic; the finding is confirmed.
  - *Corrected claim:* Under the stated assumptions the cumulative IP tax could move the equivalized Gini at most ~0.17 points, downward, absent re-ranking - and the paper's own Tables A.3-A.4 and p.13 put re-ranking at ~0; even allowing arbitrary re-ranking, a tax extracting 0.50% of aggregate wealth cannot raise a 0.6402 Gini by more than ~0.82 points, so the reported +1.04 exceeds the absolute bound. Moreover every equivalized long-run cell (including NO/Sim3/Piketty, which fall far too little) sits 1.2-1.9 points above its RS-predicted value while the individual panel matches RS within 0.06 points, so the whole equivalized panel - and Figure 5's equivalized top-1% paths built on it - reflects a construction artefact.
- **external verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** This is an internal-consistency finding with no external claims, so per my brief I return PLAUSIBLE — but the reasoning is sound and I independently verified and strengthened it. Every cited cell checks out (Table 5 bottom panel: 0.6402→0.6506 at T=25 for IP, still 0.6486 at T=100; Table A.3 short-run equivalized Gpost=0.6402 and Ct=0.9744; Table A.7 g=2% cumulative tl/r: IP 0.50%, CH 2.16%, FR 0.62%, Sim.1 1.61%, Sim.2 1.10%). I verified Gini scale-invariance numerically (uniform 1.02^25 growth leaves the Gini unchanged to 8 decimals, negatives included) and reproduced the max R-S fall of 0.17 pts for IP vs the observed +1.04. I additionally derived a distribution-free bound that closes the finder's one technical gap (their bound borrows the short-run Ct for the cumulative tax): for any non-negative tax vector with aggregate share t, Gpost ≤ (Gpre+t)/(1−t) regardless of re-ranking — for IP at T=25 that ceiling is 0.6484, so the reported 0.6506 is arithmetically impossible under the stated assumptions, not merely implausible; for CH/FR/Sim.1/Sim.2 the rise requires re-ranking D≈0.012, i.e. 3–7x the entire progressivity term of taxes with Ct 0.80–0.99. The corroborating pattern (no jump at short run, no jump in the individual panel, near-identical +0.8/+1.0 jumps across taxes differing 4x, decay after T=25) all verified in the tables.
- **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** All numbers verified against Table 5 (p.26), Table A.3 (p.41), Table A.4 (p.42, D=0 for wealth), and Table A.7 (p.46). The mathematics is airtight: with uniform growth g and fixed households, every unit's wealth is multiplied by the same (1+g)^T, so the no-tax equivalized Gini must stay at 0.6402 exactly (Gini is scale-invariant, negatives included, since numerator and mean both scale); the taxed path differs only via (1−t_i)^T factors, bounding the T=25 move for IP at roughly tl/r×K = 0.0050×0.3342 ≈ 0.0017 downward — versus an observed +0.0104, sign-flipped and 6x the bound, with near-identical jumps (+0.83 to +1.01) across five designs whose cumulative taxes differ fourfold, and no jump at all in the individual-wealth panel. The paper's own explanation (p.27) invokes 'the natural growth of wealth at the top', which its own uniform-g assumption (p.26) rules out, so the explanation is internally contradictory. No honest live response can rescue the printed numbers; the authors can only protect the individual-wealth headline results and commit to auditing the equivalized construction.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** We accept the logic of the argument: under our own uniform-growth assumption the no-tax equivalized distribution at any horizon is a rescaling of the initial one, so its Gini must remain 0.6402, and with a cumulative burden of half a percent and a concentration index of 0.97 the tax cannot move the equivalized Gini by a full point in either direction. We also concede that our sentence on p.27 about offsetting 'the natural growth of wealth at the top' is not available to us, since we assumed that growth is uniform. We will audit how the equivalized series is built in the projection — the candidates are the population, weights, or treatment of zero- and negative-wealth households entering the equivalized computation at T>0 — and either correct or withdraw that panel; the discussant's observation that five designs with fourfold different cumulative taxes land within two tenths of a point of each other does point to a common construction step rather than a tax effect. What we would defend is that our long-run conclusions rest on the individual net-wealth panel and the top-share paths, which show no anomaly and preserve the same cross-design ordering.


### [CONFIRMED] Sim. 2 is ambiguous about what is 'harmonised' — and the overlap matrix contradicts the stated EUR 700,000 uniform threshold
*id `sim2-harmonisation-ambiguity` · would change conclusions: medium · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** Sim. 2 is described as eliminating 'regional deductions and allowances' (p.10) yet Table A.2 (p.40) lists it with a EUR 700,000 threshold and 'Harmonised across regions'. In 2022 regional heterogeneity comprised not only rebates (Madrid and Andalusia 100%, Galicia 25%) but also lower exemption thresholds of EUR 500,000 in Catalonia, Comunidad Valenciana and Extremadura and region-own scales (Catalonia 0.21%–3.48% from tax year 2022; Extremadura up to 3.75%; Valencia up to 3.5%). If Sim. 2 truly harmonised thresholds to EUR 700,000, baseline taxpayers with taxable bases between 500k and 700k in those three regions would drop out — yet Table A.8 (p.47) reports IP∩Sim.2 = 170,404, i.e. every single baseline taxpayer remains a taxpayer under Sim. 2. So either the simulation kept regional thresholds and scales (and 'harmonised across regions'/'applied uniformly' overstates what was done — Sim. 2 then measures only rebate removal), or the overlap matrix is wrong. The paper also cannot settle on its own scenario count: 'seven simulations' (Table 1 title, p.11), 'eight simulated scenarios' (abstract), and 'nine simulated tax scenarios' (p.15).

**Paper evidence.** p.10: 'The second (Sim. 2) eliminates regional deductions and allowances, simulating a scenario in which the tax is applied uniformly across all regions.' Table A.2 (p.40), Sim. 2 row: 'Net wealth | €700,000 | Progressive (same schedule as IP) | No regional deductions or rebates | Harmonised across regions'. Footnote 6 (p.9): baseline 'replicates in full detail the regional heterogeneity of the tax, including the specific rate schedules, exemption thresholds, and deductions and allowances'. Table A.8 (p.47): IP∩Sim.1 = IP∩Sim.2 = IP∩Sim.3 = 170,404 (all baseline taxpayers retained). Footnote 9 (p.20): only Madrid and Andalusia named as fully rebated. Counting: Table 1 title (p.11) 'seven simulations'; abstract (p.1) 'eight simulated scenarios'; p.15 'each of the nine simulated tax scenarios'.

**External evidence.** FETCHED https://atc.gencat.cat/es/tributs/impost-patrimoni/ (official Catalan Tax Agency): mínimo exento EUR 500,000; scale 0.21% to 3.48% (top bracket above EUR 20M), applicable 2022–2025 per Decreto-ley 16/2022. SEEN in WebSearch results (REAF Panorama de la Fiscalidad Autonómica 2022 summaries): Extremadura mínimo exento 500,000, scale 0.30%–3.75%; C. Valenciana 500,000, 0.25%–3.50%. FETCHED https://www.juntadeandalucia.es/boja/2022/182/1 context via search: Andalusia Decreto-ley 7/2022 (20 Sept 2022) 100% bonificación with effects from 1 Jan 2022. SEEN in search results (BOE-A-2023-6382 and advisory summaries): Galicia 25% bonificación for 2022 (Ley 18/2021), raised to 50% from 2023 (Ley 7/2022).

**Arithmetic.** Sim.2 Ntp − IP Ntp = 201,687 − 170,404 = +31,283 new effective taxpayers (consistent with un-rebating Madrid/Andalusia/Galicia filers). For IP∩Sim.2 = 170,404 to hold under a EUR 700k harmonised threshold, exactly zero of the baseline's Catalan/Valencian/Extremaduran taxpayers may have taxable wealth between their regional 500k threshold and 700k — implausible given median filer wealth of 1.43M (Table A.1) implies a thick left tail of filers, and Catalonia is the largest filer region.

**Suggested question.** Does Sim. 2 remove only the bonificaciones (keeping Catalonia's, Valencia's and Extremadura's EUR 500,000 thresholds and their own rate scales, including Catalonia's 3.48% top rate that applied from 2022), or does it impose the state's EUR 700,000 threshold and 0.2–3.5% scale everywhere? Table A.8 shows every baseline taxpayer surviving into Sim. 2, which seems incompatible with raising those three regions' thresholds to 700k — which of the two descriptions should we believe, and does 'harmonisation' in your policy conclusion mean levelling up to Catalonia's rules or down to the state default?

- **internal verifier — CONFIRMED.** All internal elements verify verbatim: p.10's 'eliminates regional deductions and allowances... applied uniformly across all regions'; Table A.2's Sim.2 row (p.40) with '€700,000', 'No regional deductions or rebates', 'Harmonised across regions'; fn.6 (p.9) establishing that the baseline replicates region-specific 'rate schedules, exemption thresholds, and deductions and allowances' (i.e., regionally heterogeneous thresholds and scales exist in the baseline); fn.9 (p.20) naming only Madrid and Andalusia; and Table A.8 (p.47) with IP∩Sim1 = IP∩Sim2 = IP∩Sim3 = 170,404 and Sim2 Ntp 201,687 (+31,283, recomputed). The scenario-count chaos is verified and wider than claimed: 'seven simulations' (Table 1 title p.11; also 'the seven simulations proposed', p.16), 'eight simulated scenarios' (abstract) and 'all eight simulations' (p.30), and 'nine simulated tax scenarios' - which sits on printed p.16, just after the p.15 break, a one-page slip in the finder's citation. The only leg not decidable internally is that Catalonia/Valencia/Extremadura had €500,000 thresholds (and own scales) in 2022: the paper says thresholds vary by region and that those regions 'maintained high effective burdens' (p.4-5) but never gives the values, so that premise rests on the finder's external fetches; granting it, exact full retention of all 170,404 baseline payers under a harmonised €700k threshold is implausible and the disjunction (rebate-removal-only vs wrong matrix) follows. Median filer wealth 1,434,463 (Table A.1) verified.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** All external claims verified, mostly against official sources: the Catalan Tax Agency confirms mínimo exento EUR 500,000 and the 0.21%–3.48% scale (top above EUR 20M) applicable to ejercicios 2022–2025 per Decreto ley 16/2022; Iberley confirms Comunitat Valenciana's mínimo exento was 500,000 for 2021–2024 (raised to 1M only by Ley 5/2025) — I initially suspected 600k for 2022 and was wrong, the finder is right; Extremadura's 500,000 mínimo and 0.3%–3.75% scale confirmed by multiple consistent secondary tax-guide sources; Andalusia's DL 7/2022 (20 Sept 2022) 100% bonificación confirmed from BOJA text, and since IP accrues on 31 December (devengo), a rule in force from Sept 2022 covers ejercicio 2022 without any retroactivity clause — the Junta's own 'Medidas vigentes IP 2022' page lists it; Galicia's 25% (2022, Ley 18/2021) rising to 50% (2023, Ley 7/2022) confirmed. The paper-side quotes and Table A.8 cells verified in text and visually (paper-49.png), as is the seven/eight/nine scenario-count inconsistency (the 'nine' sentence prints on p.16, not p.15 — trivial). The inference is sound and I can strengthen the 'or the overlap matrix is wrong' horn: A.8 contains outright impossible cells — Sim.1∩Sim.2=72,186 though IP (170,404) is reported as a subset of both, and Piketty∩Sim.2=297,219 exceeds Ntp(Sim.2)=201,687 — so the matrix demonstrably contains errors, which makes the dilemma (narrower reform than described, or broken table) unavoidable for the authors.
- **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** The contradiction is fully documented: p.10 says Sim. 2 makes the tax 'applied uniformly across all regions' and Table A.2 (p.40) lists '€700,000 ... Harmonised across regions', while Table A.8 (p.47) reports IP∩Sim.2 = 170,404 = every baseline taxpayer surviving. I re-fetched the official Catalan Tax Agency page this session: Catalonia's minimo exento is €500,000 with its own 0.21%–3.48% scale applicable 2022–2025 (DL 16/2022), so a genuine €700,000 harmonisation would de-tax Catalan (and per REAF, Valencian/Extremaduran) filers with bases between 500k and 700k — with median filer wealth of 1.43M (Table A.1) a thick left tail of such filers is certain, making the 170,404 cell incompatible with the A.2 description. The scenario-count inconsistency is verified verbatim: 'seven simulations' (Table 1 title, p.11), 'eight simulated scenarios' (abstract), 'nine simulated tax scenarios' (p.15). The finding is framed as a disjunction (description wrong or matrix wrong) and survives either horn; my own check found A.8 contains two additional internally impossible cells (Sim.1∩Sim.2 = 72,186 though IP ⊆ both forces ≥170,404; Sim.2∩Piketty = 297,219 > |Sim.2| = 201,687), which makes 'matrix wrong' likelier but deepens rather than defuses the problem.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** What Sim. 2 actually does is remove the autonomous communities' deducciones and bonificaciones — above all the 100% rebates in Madrid and Andalusia and Galicia's partial one — while retaining each region's own scale and exemption threshold, which is exactly why every baseline taxpayer survives into Sim. 2 and why it adds 31,283 payers. We concede that Table A.2's '700,000 / Harmonised across regions' row and the phrase 'applied uniformly across all regions' overstate this: full harmonisation would also level Catalonia's, Valencia's and Extremadura's 500,000-euro thresholds and their own scales, which we did not simulate, so Sim. 2's revenue is not that of a uniform national IP and our conclusion's 'harmonizing regional rules' should be read as removing rebates. We will reword p.10, correct Table A.2, and reconcile the seven/eight/nine scenario count. We would also caution against leaning too hard on any single cell of Table A.8 — we will re-audit that table in full, since the discussant is right that it must be made consistent with whichever design description we settle on.


### [CONFIRMED] The 60% IRPF–IP cap: excluded from foreign designs on a factually wrong premise, and unspecified for Sims 1–3 and Piketty — where it matters most
*id `irpf-ip-cap-treatment` · would change conclusions: medium · finder confidence: medium*

**Claim.** The paper applies the joint IRPF–IP 60% cap in the baseline but excludes it from NO/CH/FR because it 'has no analogue in the foreign systems' (p.10) — factually incorrect for France, whose IFI carries a statutory plafonnement capping IFI plus income taxes at 75% of income (and the ITSGF itself embeds a 60% limit). For the Spanish reform scenarios (Sims 1–3) and Piketty the paper never says whether the cap applies, yet it is first-order: Mas-Montserrat et al. (2025), which the paper itself cites, find that use of this limit accounts for 92.6% of the revenue loss from behavioural responses to the Catalan IP, and lowering the threshold to EUR 300,000 (Sims 1/3) pulls in exactly the asset-rich/income-poor (older, retired) households for whom the cap binds. Table A.8 contains a clue that the cap (or a base inconsistency) is active in the Piketty simulation: 3,577 baseline IP taxpayers pay zero under Piketty (166,827 vs 170,404), which is impossible if Piketty simply taxes all net wealth above EUR 200,000 with no cap.

**Paper evidence.** p.4: 'the joint IRPF–IP tax-liability limit caps the combined burden of both taxes at 60% of the taxpayer's aggregate IRPF tax base (with a minimum payment of 20% of the Wealth Tax liability).' p.10: 'For comparability, the simulations of international models do not incorporate Spain's joint IRPF–IP tax-liability limit, as this mechanism is specific to the Spanish institutional framework and has no analogue in the foreign systems.' Table A.2 (p.40): cap noted only in the baseline row ('Joint IRPF–IP liability cap applies'); Sim.1/2/3 and Piketty rows silent. p.7 (lit review): 'current design features such as the family business exemption and the wealth-income cap are regressive' (Durán-Cabré & Esteller-Moré 2019). Table A.8 (p.47): IP∩Piketty = 166,827 < Ntp(IP) = 170,404; also IP∩NO = 162,546 and IP∩CH = 167,326.

**External evidence.** FETCHED https://www.service-public.gouv.fr/particuliers/vosdroits/F563: IFI threshold EUR 1.3M, main residence 30% abatement. SEEN in WebSearch results (impots.gouv.fr 'Calcul de l'IFI', economie.gouv.fr): IFI plafonnement — IFI plus income taxes capped at 75% of prior-year income; schedule 0.5% from 800k to 1.3M up to 1.5% above 10M with a décote for 1.3–1.4M. FETCHED https://www.boe.es/buscar/act.php?id=BOE-A-2022-22684: ITSGF Art. 3.Doce.1 joint 60% limit with IRPF and IP. SEEN in WebSearch results, https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0047272725000490 (Mas-Montserrat, Durán-Cabré & Esteller-Moré, JPubE 2025): taxpayers 'change their asset and income portfolio to apply the limit on tax liability'; 'the use of the limit accounts for most of the impact on revenue reduction (92.6%)'; 0.1pp higher ATR reduces taxable wealth 3.21% over 4 years.

**Arithmetic.** 170,404 − 166,827 = 3,577 baseline taxpayers with zero Piketty liability despite necessarily holding net wealth far above the EUR 200,000 Piketty threshold (baseline effective taxpayers face thresholds of 500–700k). Under a no-cap Piketty design their liability is strictly positive, so some unstated mechanism (the 60% cap without the 20% floor, or a different wealth base) is zeroing them out. Similarly 7,858 IP payers escape Sim. NO (explicable by 25% housing valuation plus doubled couple thresholds) and 3,078 escape Sim. CH.

**Suggested question.** Is the 60% IRPF–IP cap applied in Sims 1–3 and in the Piketty scenario, and how much revenue does it remove in each — particularly in Sim. 1/Sim. 3, where the EUR 300,000 threshold pulls in asset-rich income-poor retirees? I ask partly because 3,577 current IP payers show zero liability under Piketty in Table A.8, which seems to require the cap; and note the stated reason for dropping the cap in the foreign designs doesn't hold for France, whose IFI caps IFI-plus-income-taxes at 75% of income.

- **internal verifier — CONFIRMED.** The internal core verifies completely. The p.4 cap description and p.10 exclusion sentence ('has no analogue in the foreign systems') are verbatim; Table A.2 (p.40) lists the cap only in the baseline row; and nothing anywhere in the paper states whether Sims 1-3 or Piketty apply it - checked across Sections 4-5 and the appendix. Table A.8 arithmetic verifies: 170,404-166,827 = 3,577 baseline payers with zero Piketty liability, 7,858 escaping NO, 3,078 escaping CH. The Piketty anomaly is internally airtight: baseline effective taxpayers clear thresholds around €700k (+€300k residence, p.4; regional variants per fn.6), so under a no-cap tax on all net wealth above €200,000 at 0.5%+ (Table 2, p.11; Table A.2 'Net wealth / €200,000') their liability is strictly positive - some unstated mechanism (the cap, a different base, or an error) must zero them, whereas the NO/CH gaps have stated design explanations (25% housing valuation and doubled couple thresholds; cantonal thresholds - Table A.2), and A.8 behaves as a logical subset matrix elsewhere (IP∩Sim1 = IP∩Sim3 = 170,404, as lowering thresholds cannot remove payers). The French-plafonnement and ITSGF-60%-limit legs are external law not verifiable from the paper (p.5's IFI description mentions no cap), and Mas-Montserrat's 92.6% figure is external, though the paper does cite that study for avoidance responses (p.6) and itself reports that 'the wealth-income cap [is] regressive' (p.7, quoting Duran-Cabre & Esteller-More 2019).
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** The decisive external claims all verified. impots.gouv.fr confirms the IFI plafonnement: IFI plus French and foreign income taxes may not exceed '75 % du total des revenus mondiaux nets de frais professionnels' — so the paper's stated reason for dropping the cap ('no analogue in the foreign systems', p.10) is factually wrong for France; service-public.gouv.fr confirms the 1.3M threshold and 30% main-residence abatement; BOE Art. 3.Doce.1 confirms the ITSGF's own 60% joint limit; and the published JPubE abstract (via RePEc) confirms Mas-Montserrat et al. verbatim: 0.1pp higher ATR → −3.21% taxable wealth over 4 years, and the limit-on-tax-liability channel 'accounts for the greatest impact on tax revenues (92.6%)'. Paper-side quotes and the A.8 cells (166,827; 162,546; 167,326) verified in text and visually; arithmetic recomputed. One material caveat on the 'clue': Table A.8 is demonstrably unreliable — the same table reports IP⊆Sim.1 (170,404) and Sim.1⊆Piketty (1,834,488=Ntp(Sim.1)), which by transitivity forces IP∩Piketty=170,404, contradicting the 166,827 cell the finding leans on (and other cells are outright impossible, e.g. Piketty∩Sim.2=297,219>201,687) — so that cell may be a tabulation error rather than the cap's footprint. The finding's core (cap treatment unspecified for Sims 1–3/Piketty where it binds hardest; French premise wrong; ask for with/without-cap runs) stands regardless, and the suggested question already hedges appropriately.
- **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** The paper's statements are verified (p.4 cap definition with the 20% floor; p.10 'has no analogue in the foreign systems'; Table A.2 mentions the cap only in the baseline row; Sims 1–3 and Piketty silent). The France premise is factually wrong: I fetched the official impots.gouv.fr IFI page this session, which states the plafonnement — 'le total de l'IFI et des impots dus en France et a l'etranger au titre des revenus' must not exceed '75% du total des revenus mondiaux nets de frais professionnels de l'annee precedente' — and the BOE fetch confirms the ITSGF itself embeds the joint 60% limit (Art. 3.Doce), so income-capping is not 'specific to the Spanish institutional framework'. The Piketty anomaly is arithmetically verified (170,404 − 166,827 = 3,577) and is impossible under a no-cap Piketty on net wealth above €200k, since every baseline effective taxpayer's net wealth exceeds a ≥€500k regional threshold; however, my audit found Table A.8 contains other internally impossible cells (e.g. Sim.2∩Piketty = 297,219 > 201,687), so the cell evidences *some* defect — an unstated cap, a base inconsistency, or a matrix error — rather than the cap specifically. The substance (wrong premise for France; cap treatment unspecified where it is first-order per the paper's own cited Mas-Montserrat et al. 92.6% result) survives any honest response.
  - *Corrected claim:* The 3,577-taxpayer cell (IP∩Piketty = 166,827) demonstrates an unexplained defect — an unstated cap, a valuation/base inconsistency, or an error in Table A.8 itself (which contains other internally impossible cells) — rather than specifically proving the cap is active in the Piketty simulation.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** The Spanish reform scenarios are run through the same calculator as the baseline and inherit the joint IRPF–IP limit; we agree the paper should say so explicitly and should report how much revenue the limit removes in each scenario — the discussant is right that a 300,000-euro threshold pulls in exactly the asset-rich, income-poor retirees for whom it binds, and the evidence we ourselves cite (Mas-Montserrat, Duran-Cabre and Esteller-More, 2025) makes that first-order. On France we concede the sentence as written: the IFI carries a statutory plafonnement capping IFI plus income taxes at 75% of income, so 'no analogue' is wrong; our actual choice was to strip country-specific caps from all foreign designs for comparability, and we will restate it that way and note that the ITSGF embeds the same 60% limit. We cannot resolve the 166,827 cell from the podium — under a no-cap Piketty on net wealth above 200,000 euros it should not occur — so we will audit whether the cap, the base, or the overlap table itself is responsible. Re-running every scenario with and without the cap is a reasonable request and we will add it.


### [CONFIRMED] Fully static exercise: confirmed by the text; the verified elasticity literature implies revenue overstatement that differs systematically across the paper's scenarios
*id `static-no-behaviour` · would change conclusions: medium · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** The paper is explicit that all eight scenarios are static — no behavioural response, no migration, fixed 2022 population and wealth — so every revenue and progressivity figure is a morning-after upper bound. The verified evidence implies the bias is scenario-dependent in ways the paper never discusses: for Sim. 2/Sim. 3 (harmonisation) the internal-mobility margin largely disappears by construction (no in-Spain haven remains; Agrawal–Foremny–Martínez-Toledano even find harmonisation would raise Madrid's total revenue, supporting the paper's direction), leaving avoidance and cap-gaming as the main leakages; for Sim. NO and Piketty the top tail drives revenue and reported-wealth responses of the Brülhart–Switzerland magnitude (1pp ≈ −43% reported wealth over 6 years, 24% mobility / 21% housing prices) would erode precisely the top-end incidence that makes those designs look most redistributive; and the 25–100-year projections compound the static assumption multiplicatively, since avoidance responses accumulate with the tax (Mas-Montserrat et al.: −3.21% taxable wealth over 4 years per 0.1pp of ATR).

**Paper evidence.** p.2: 'holding net wealth constant to isolate design effects.' p.11: 'All these simulations are implemented on the 2022 Spanish administrative microdata... thereby isolating the effects derived exclusively from the institutional design of each fiscal system.' Limitations (p.34): 'Second, the simulations assume no behavioural responses and therefore cannot capture avoidance or relocation effects.' Long-run framework p.15 and p.25-26 (constant ts/r applied every year to a uniform-g path). Table 3 (p.21): Sim. NO 15,456.06M / Sim. 3 12,673.18M / Piketty 15,860.95M with average rates t of 0.29%/0.24%/0.30% on net wealth.

**External evidence.** FETCHED https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257/pol.20200258 (Brülhart et al., AEJ:EP 2022): 'a 1 percentage point drop in a canton's wealth tax rate raises reported taxable wealth by at least 43 percent after 6 years'; mobility 24%, housing prices 21% of the effect. SEEN in WebSearch results, https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257%2Fapp.20220615 (Agrawal, Foremny & Martínez-Toledano, AEJ:Applied 2025): Madrid's stock of wealthy +10% five years after 2011 decentralisation; income-tax revenue losses from mobility six times the direct wealth-tax losses; Madrid would raise more total revenue under a harmonised positive rate. SEEN in WebSearch results, https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0047272725000490 (Mas-Montserrat et al., JPubE 2025): 0.1pp ATR increase → −3.21% taxable wealth over 4 years, dominated by avoidance and the liability limit.

**Arithmetic.** Directional bound only (Lens 4 quantifies): at Sim. NO's average rate of 0.29% on net wealth (vs IP 0.02%), applying any positive reported-wealth semi-elasticity to a +0.27pp average-rate increase shrinks the 15.5bn static figure; over the 100-year horizon the wealth multiple at g=2% is 1.02^100 = 7.24, so even small annual behavioural erosion compounds into first-order changes in the projected top-1% share paths of Figure 5.

**Suggested question.** Your static design ranking may be robust, but the bias is not uniform across scenarios: harmonisation (Sim. 3) removes the internal-mobility margin — Agrawal et al. even find Madrid would gain revenue — while the Norwegian and Piketty designs depend on top-tail reported wealth, where Brülhart et al. and Mas-Montserrat et al. document the largest avoidance responses. Would you consider reporting behavioural-adjusted revenue bands per scenario, rather than a single global caveat, since the adjustment plausibly reorders NO/Piketty relative to Sim. 3?

- **internal verifier — CONFIRMED.** The static premise is confirmed verbatim at p.2 ('holding net wealth constant to isolate design effects'), p.11 ('isolating the effects derived exclusively from the institutional design of each fiscal system'), and p.34 ('the simulations assume no behavioural responses and therefore cannot capture avoidance or relocation effects') - a single one-sentence global caveat, with no scenario-specific discussion of behavioural bias anywhere in Results or Conclusions, confirming the 'never discusses' premise. Table 3 numbers verify (NO 15,456.06M at t=0.29%, Sim3 12,673.18M at 0.24%, Piketty 15,860.95M at 0.30%, IP at 0.02%) and 1.02^100 = 7.245. The direction of the differential-bias argument is even corroborated internally by the paper's own literature review ('moderate behavioural responses concentrated at the top, mainly via asset reclassification and avoidance', p.6; 'avoidance, interregional mobility and broad exemptions', p.7), and the by-construction claim that Sim.2/Sim.3 remove the internal-mobility margin follows from their nationwide rebate removal (p.10). The specific external magnitudes (Brulhart 43%/24%/21%; Agrawal's Madrid revenue result; Mas-Montserrat -3.21% per 0.1pp) are the finder's fetches and not re-verifiable in this lens, but the finding explicitly labels them as external evidence and the claim's internal skeleton stands on its own.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** All three literature anchors verified from the journals' own pages. The AEA page for Brülhart et al. (AEJ:EP 2022) quotes the abstract verbatim: 'a 1 percentage point drop in a canton's wealth tax rate raises reported taxable wealth by at least 43 percent after 6 years', with '24 percent of the effect... from taxpayer mobility and 21 percent from a concurrent rise in housing prices'. The AEA page for Agrawal, Foremny & Martínez-Toledano (AEJ:Applied) confirms mobility-induced PIT losses 'six times larger than the direct losses to wealth taxes' and that 'Madrid could achieve higher total regional revenues by agreeing to a harmonized positive tax rate'; the +10% stock of wealthy in Madrid five years after decentralization is confirmed in the working-paper versions ('Paraísos Fiscales', WID/SSRN/CESifo). Mas-Montserrat et al.'s 0.1pp→−3.21%/4y confirmed from the published abstract. The paper's static-design statements (p.2, p.11, limitations p.34) and Table 3 figures verified in the text; 1.02^100=7.245 recomputed. The scenario-dependent-bias reasoning is sound: harmonisation mechanically removes the internal-mobility margin while the top-tail-heavy designs (NO, Piketty) are exposed to the largest reported-wealth elasticities, so a uniform static caveat genuinely understates the asymmetry.
- **advocate verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** Everything factual checks out: the paper is explicit about the static design (p.2 'holding net wealth constant'; p.11; p.34 'assume no behavioural responses'), the revenue and rate figures match Table 3, and the cited elasticity results (Brulhart et al. 2022 +43%/6yr per 1pp; Agrawal et al. Madrid harmonisation result; Mas-Montserrat et al. cap-driven avoidance) match the published sources the finder fetched/saw — indeed the paper itself cites all three literatures (pp.3,6). But because the limitation is openly and repeatedly acknowledged in the paper, the finding's surviving content is the sharper, un-addressed point: the static bias is scenario-dependent (harmonisation closes the internal-mobility margin by construction, while NO/Piketty revenue rests on top-tail reported wealth with the largest documented elasticities), so a single global caveat cannot certify the cross-design ranking, especially compounded over 25–100 years (1.02^100 = 7.24, recomputed). The authors have a legitimate partial defense — design isolation is the stated object, and elasticities estimated on narrow self-reported bases with an income cap plausibly overstate erosion under broad, third-party-reported, housing-heavy bases — so this is a fair-fight discussion point they would largely accept as an extension rather than an error they must concede. Hence PLAUSIBLE rather than CONFIRMED.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** We are explicit that these are morning-after, design-isolation estimates — p.2, p.11 and the limitations say so — and that is the standard first step in this literature: our question is how alternative designs allocate a given 2022 wealth stock, not a revenue forecast. We accept the discussant's sharper point, which the paper does not develop: the bias is not uniform, since harmonisation largely closes the internal-mobility margin — Agrawal, Foremny and Martinez-Toledano even find Madrid would raise more under a harmonised rate, which runs in Sim. 3's favour — while the Norwegian and Piketty figures lean on top-tail reported wealth where Brulhart et al. and Mas-Montserrat et al. document the largest responses. In our defence, elasticities estimated on narrow, self-reported bases with a binding income cap are plausibly upper bounds for broad, third-party-reported, housing-heavy bases, so mechanical application of the Swiss or Catalan numbers would overstate the erosion of Sim. NO and Piketty. We agree scenario-specific behavioural bands are more informative than our single global caveat and will add them as robustness; we doubt they overturn the qualitative gap between the current IP and any broad-base design, though we accept the NO/Piketty versus Sim. 3 ordering could tighten.


### [CONFIRMED] The 'real designs currently in force' are stylised in load-bearing ways: Norway's top rate and housing kink omitted, France's main-residence rule misstated, Piketty's schedule altered
*id `foreign-design-fidelity` · would change conclusions: low · finder confidence: medium*

**Claim.** The paper claims to 'replicate the wealth-tax legislations currently in force' (p.10), but each replication deviates from the verified law: Norway is simulated as flat 1% with all primary residences at 25% of market value, whereas 2023 law adds a 1.1% combined rate above NOK 20M (~EUR 1.75M) and values primary homes at 25% only up to NOK 10M and 70% above — both omissions understate the Norwegian design's top-end incidence, i.e. the paper's most redistributive 'real' benchmark is even stronger at the top than simulated. The French description contains an outright error — the IFI grants a 30% abatement on the main residence, not 'exemptions for the primary residence' (p.5) — and the paper gives the ISF threshold as both EUR 800,000 (p.10) and EUR 1.3M (p.5). The 'Piketty (2014)' schedule (Table 2, p.11) is a hybrid: Piketty's illustrative variants include 0.1% below EUR 200k (the paper uses 0%) and explicitly contemplate 5–10% rates on the largest fortunes (the paper caps at 2%), so labelling the simulation a test of Piketty's proposal overstates fidelity at both ends.

**Paper evidence.** p.10: 'we replicate the wealth-tax legislations currently in force in Norway (NO), Switzerland (CH), and France (FR)'; 'Its simulation is based on a flat 1% rate applied to the tax base, after adjusting housing values to 25% of their estimated market value.' Table A.2 (p.40): Sim. NO 'Flat rate 1%... State–municipal split rate (0.3% + 0.7%); married couples: double threshold'. p.5: France 'exemptions for the primary residence'; ISF described as 'levied on assets above €1.3 million' (p.5) but 'a progressive levy on net assets above 800,000€' (p.10). Table 2 (p.11): Piketty schedule 0% below 200k, 0.5%/1%/2% above; described as replicating 'Piketty (2014) tax schedule proposal'.

**External evidence.** FETCHED https://www.skatteetaten.no/en/rates/wealth-tax/?year=2023 (official): 2023 threshold NOK 1,700,000 (doubled for couples); municipal 0.7%; state 0.3% to NOK 20M and 0.4% above => combined 1.0%/1.1%. SEEN in WebSearch results (Skatteetaten 'Taxable value of residential properties' and PwC summaries): primary dwelling at 25% of market value up to NOK 10M and 70% above (2023 onward). FETCHED https://www.service-public.gouv.fr/particuliers/vosdroits/F563: 30% abatement on main residence; liability threshold EUR 1.3M. SEEN in WebSearch results (impots.gouv/economie.gouv and IFI barème pages): schedule runs from EUR 800k (0.5%) once over the 1.3M trigger, top 1.5% above 10M. SEEN in WebSearch results (New Republic / Tax Foundation summaries of Capital in the Twenty-First Century): Piketty variants — 0% below 1M / 1% 1–5M / 2% above 5M, refined variant 0.1% below 200k and 0.5% 200k–1M, and 'up to 5 to 10 percent' on fortunes of several hundred million.

**Arithmetic.** NOK 20M / 11.4 NOK per EUR ≈ EUR 1.75M and NOK 1.7M ≈ EUR 149k (paper's '~€160,000' threshold is roughly right). Omitting the extra 0.1pp above ~EUR 1.75M and the 70% housing tranche above ~EUR 0.88M both bias Sim. NO's revenue (15,456M) and its top-percentile AETRs downward; omitting Piketty's 0.1% below 200k removes a broad-base revenue slice while omitting the 5–10% top rates removes top-end progressivity, so the simulated 'Piketty' is more moderate than the book's proposal at both ends.

**Suggested question.** Sim. NO omits Norway's 1.1% combined rate above NOK 20 million and the 70% valuation of primary-home value above NOK 10 million — both of which would make the Norwegian design look even stronger at the top than your Figure 3 shows — and the IFI's main-residence treatment is a 30% abatement, not an exemption. Which legislative vintage did you actually encode for each country, and how sensitive are the cross-country rankings to these top-end provisions?

- **internal verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** All internal premises verify: p.10 claims to 'replicate the wealth-tax legislations currently in force' and simulates Norway as 'a flat 1% rate... after adjusting housing values to 25% of their estimated market value' with no higher bracket or 70% tranche anywhere (Table A.2: 'Flat rate 1%... (0.3% + 0.7%); married couples: double threshold'; p.5 also asserts Norway has 'no progressive brackets'); p.5 says the IFI has 'exemptions for the primary residence'; Table 2 (p.11) prints the 0%/0.5%/1%/2% schedule labelled 'Piketty (2014) tax schedule proposal'. The threshold inconsistency is internal, confirmed, and actually runs in both directions: p.10 puts the ISF at 'above 800,000€' vs p.5's ISF 'above €1.3 million', while p.5 puts the IFI 'above €800,000' vs the '€1.3 million threshold' in Table A.2 (p.40) and 'threshold 1.3M' in the slides. But whether these constitute errors - and whether Norway's 2023 combined 1.1% above NOK 20M and 70% valuation above NOK 10M, the French 30% abatement, and Piketty's book variants (0.1% below 200k; 5-10% at the very top) are as described - are external legal/bibliographic facts not decidable from the paper; indeed the finder's own external evidence shows 800k (schedule start) and 1.3M (liability trigger) are both real features of the French taxes, so the sharper internal charge is unexplained inconsistency/conflation rather than a wrong number. Hence: internal legs confirmed, load-bearing deviations external - plausible.
  - *Corrected claim:* The foreign and Piketty designs are encoded in simplified form and the paper is internally inconsistent about the French thresholds - p.10 gives the ISF as 'above 800,000€' vs p.5's €1.3M, and p.5 gives the IFI as 'above €800,000' vs €1.3M in Table A.2 and the slides (per the finder's external sources both numbers describe real features, schedule start vs liability trigger, so this is unexplained conflation rather than one wrong figure); the claims that Norway's simulated design omits the 2023 top rate and housing kink, that the IFI grants a 30% abatement rather than an exemption, and that Piketty's book schedule differs at both ends rest on external law and text not verifiable from the paper.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** Norway: Skatteetaten's official 2023 rates page confirms threshold NOK 1,700,000 (3,400,000 for couples), municipal 0.7%, and a two-bracket state rate of 0.3% up to NOK 20M and 0.4% above — combined 1.0%/1.1% — and Skatteetaten's residential-valuation guidance confirms primary dwellings at 25% of market value up to NOK 10M and 70% of the excess for 2023 (with a worked example: a NOK 15M home has taxable value 6M); the paper's flat-1%-with-all-housing-at-25% therefore omits both top-end provisions, and since the paper's own reference is 'Norwegian Tax Administration (2023)', the 2023 vintage is the right benchmark. France: service-public confirms the main residence gets a 30% abatement, not an exemption, so p.5 is an error; impots.gouv confirms the barème from 800k (0.5%) to 1.5% above 10M and the 1.3M trigger — the paper's 800k-vs-1.3M presentation is internally inconsistent as claimed, though the fair resolution is that both figures are real facets of ISF/IFI (1.3M liability trigger, brackets from 800k), which the paper never explains. Piketty: the Tax Foundation analysis quotes p.517 of the book directly — central schedule 0%<1M/1% 1–5M/2%>5M, the variant '0.1 percent below 200,000 euros and 0.5 percent between 200,000 and 1 million', and 'a rate of 5 or 10 percent on assets above 1 billion euros' (appendix variant b: 5%>20M, 10%>100M) — confirming the paper's Table 2 is a hybrid that is more moderate than the book at both ends; one small precision: the 5–10% rates attach to ≥EUR 1bn in the main text (≥20M in the appendix variant), not 'several hundred million' as the finder's evidence note phrased it. Conversions recomputed (NOK 20M≈EUR 1.75M; NOK 1.7M≈EUR 149k). 'Low' impact rating is right since every correction strengthens Sim. NO's top end.
- **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** Each deviation is now verified against the primary source. Norway: I fetched Skatteetaten's official 2023 rates page this session — state 0.3% between NOK 1.7M and 20M and 0.4% above 20M (combined 1.0%/1.1%), thresholds 1.7M/3.4M — while the paper (p.10) and Table A.2 simulate 'a flat 1% rate' with a '0.3% + 0.7%' split and no top step; the 25%-up-to-NOK-10M housing kink (70% above in 2023) is per the finder's seen Skatteetaten valuation guidance and existed in 2022 as well, so no vintage choice rescues the omissions. France: service-public F563 (fetched) confirms the main residence gets 'un abattement de 30%', not the 'exemptions for the primary residence' of p.5, and impots.gouv.fr (fetched) confirms the trigger-1.3M/bareme-from-800k structure that the paper states inconsistently for the ISF (€1.3M on p.5, €800,000 on p.10). Piketty: Table 2's 0% below €200k and 2% cap versus the book's 0.1%-below-200k variant and contemplated 5–10% top rates matches the finder's sourced description of the book's variants. The authors must concede the deviations; their honest defense is purely directional — the omissions make Sim. NO conservative — which the finding itself already builds in (would_change_conclusions: low), so the claim survives intact as a fidelity-of-description issue.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** We simulated the main structural features of each system — base, threshold, central rates, housing discounts — rather than every bracket, and we accept that 'replicate the legislations currently in force' overstates this; we will write 'stylised versions' and state each simplification and its direction. On Norway we concede both points: the combined top rate is 1.1% above NOK 20 million and the primary-dwelling discount is 25% only up to NOK 10 million, so Sim. NO understates the Norwegian design at the very top — which makes our finding that the broad Norwegian base dominates the IP conservative rather than fragile. On France, 'exemption' should read '30% abatement' on the main residence, and we will fix the 800,000/1.3 million wording — the liability trigger is 1.3 million and the schedule runs from 800,000 — and state explicitly which rule the simulation encoded. On Piketty, Table 2 is the book's central illustrative schedule; we will footnote that Piketty also floats 0.1% below 200,000 euros and far higher rates on the largest fortunes, so our version is moderate at both ends. We will add the legislative vintage encoded for each country.


### [PLAUSIBLE] The universal child benefit divides revenue among 7.54M minors — about 8% fewer than Spain's under-18 population — inflating every per-child amount
*id `child-benefit-divisor` · would change conclusions: low · finder confidence: medium*

**Claim.** Every row of Table 4 (p.25) implies the same divisor: revenue divided by transfer-per-minor gives 7.543 million minors in all eight scenarios (e.g. 1,125.58M/149.22 = 7.543M). INE-based counts put Spain's under-18 population at roughly 8.2 million in 2022, so the per-child amounts are overstated by ~8.7% — most plausibly because the Household Panel undercounts dependents, a gap the paper itself flags as a data limitation. The Gini effects in Table 4 are less affected (the same total is redistributed, just over a slightly wrong base), but the headline per-child euros — including the slides' 'EUR 1,680–2,103/child/year' claims — should be deflated, and the exercise should state that it assumes full take-up, an untaxed benefit, no interaction with existing child supports or the IMV, and static wealth-tax revenue.

**Paper evidence.** Table 4 (p.25): IP 149.22, NO 2,049.05, CH 642.04, FR 191.45, Sim.1 491.76, Sim.2 318.86, Sim.3 1,680.12, Piketty 2,102.73 EUR per minor per year; Table 3 (p.21) revenues. p.8: 'the possible absence of information in cases where administrative records are unavailable—particularly the information on dependents within the household.' p.12: the revenue 'is redistributed equally among children... a universal child benefit, a policy currently in place in 19 European Union countries.' Slides: 'Broad-base designs... could finance transfers of €1,680–2,100/child/year.'

**External evidence.** FETCHED https://www.cope.es/actualidad/sociedad/noticias/espana-tiene-actualidad-menos-menores-anos-que-hace-casi-siglo-20220427_2049918 (April 2022, INE-based): 'alrededor de 8,2 millones de habitantes menores de 18 años' in Spain; corroborated by INE population searches (0–10 plus 11–20 age groups totalling ~9.6M spanning 0–20).

**Arithmetic.** Implied divisor per row: 1,125.58/149.22 = 7.5431M; 15,456.06/2,049.05 = 7.5430M; 4,842.96/642.04 = 7.5431M; 1,444.10/191.45 = 7.5430M; 3,709.39/491.76 = 7.5430M; 2,405.17/318.86 = 7.5430M; 12,673.18/1,680.12 = 7.5430M; 15,860.95/2,102.73 = 7.5430M — all identical at 7.543M minors. Against 8.2M actual minors the per-child amounts are 8.2/7.543 − 1 = 8.7% too high (e.g. Sim. 3: 1,680 → ~1,546 EUR; Piketty: 2,103 → ~1,934 EUR).

**Suggested question.** Your Table 4 amounts imply 7.54 million minors, while INE puts Spain's under-18 population near 8.2 million in 2022 — is the gap the Household Panel's known undercount of dependents, and shouldn't the per-child amounts be computed on the population count (about 9% lower) given the policy is meant to be universal rather than PH-sample-universal?

- **internal verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** The internal arithmetic is fully confirmed: dividing each Table 3 (p.21) revenue by its Table 4 (p.25) per-child amount yields an identical divisor of 7.543M minors in all eight rows (7.5430-7.5431, recomputed), and the paper never states a minors count, never defines 'minor', and never states take-up, tax treatment of the benefit, or interaction with existing child supports or the IMV (absence verified across Sections 4.3 and 5); the p.8 dependents-gap quote, the p.12 '19 European Union countries' quote, and the slides' '€1,680–2,100/child/year' (table prints 2,103) are all verbatim. The benchmark that turns this into a finding - INE's ~8.2M under-18 population in 2022 - is external and cannot be verified from the paper (and the finder's cited source is a news article, the weakest in this set), so the ~8.7% inflation (recomputed: 8.2/7.543-1 = 8.71%; Sim.3 1,680 -> 1,546; Piketty 2,103 -> 1,934) is plausible rather than confirmed. Internally suggestive but not probative: Table A.1's weighted individuals total 44.86M, below Spain's resident population, consistent with PH undercoverage concentrated among dependents - but that comparison itself requires an external population figure; an alternative innocent explanation (a narrower definition of 'children' than under-18) also cannot be excluded from the paper.

Against 8.089M the per-child amounts are overstated by ≈7.2% (not 8.7%): Sim. 3 EUR 1,680 → ≈1,567 and Piketty EUR 2,103 → ≈1,961. The direction, mechanism (PH's flagged undercount of dependents; PH covers 44.86M weighted individuals vs ~47.4M residents) and the suggested question all survive — Max should just quote 8.09M. A counterweight worth having in hand: the PH's ~5% population undercoverage (and the baseline's simulated revenue running 10% below implied actual 2022 collections) means revenue numerators are likely understated too, so rescaling only the denominator is not obviously the full fix.

But the headline quantification has a strong author answer the finder missed: the PH universe is the common fiscal territory, not Spain — Table A.1's weighted individual count is 44,857,347, about 2.6M below Spain's ~47.4M 2022 population, consistent with the AEAT panel excluding the Basque Country and Navarre (~2.9M people), whose foral wealth taxes are outside these simulations. Against common-territory minors (~7.7–7.8M rather than the all-Spain ~8.2M the finder fetched), the shortfall is roughly 2–3%, not 8.7%, and dividing common-territory revenue by common-territory children is arguably the correct design (a truly national benefit would add foral revenue and foral children symmetrically). So the finding partially survives — undisclosed divisor, unstated assumptions, and a residual dependents-undercount gap the paper itself flags on p.8 — but its central 'inflating every per-child amount by ~9%' number does not withstand the best response.
  - *Corrected claim:* Table 4's per-child amounts divide each scenario's revenue by the PH's weighted minor count of 7.543M, which the paper never states. The correct population comparator is the under-18 population of the common fiscal territory (the PH's 44.86M weighted individuals exclude the Basque Country and Navarre), roughly 7.7–7.8M minors, so the amounts are overstated by roughly 2–3% — attributable to the dependent-coverage gap the paper flags on p.8 — not by ~9%; the ~8.7% figure only follows if all-Spain minors (~8.2M) are used, which would also require adding foral wealth-tax revenue to the numerator. The exercise should still state its assumptions: full take-up, an untaxed benefit, no interaction with existing child supports or the IMV, and static revenue.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** The divisor is the weighted number of minors observed in the Household Panel, 7.54 million, and we agree the paper should say so and should state the exercise's assumptions — full take-up, an untaxed benefit, no interaction with existing child supports or the minimum income scheme, and static revenue. But the right benchmark is not Spain's 8.2 million under-18s: the Panel covers the common fiscal territory only — our 44.9 million weighted individuals exclude the Basque Country and Navarre, whose foral wealth taxes are outside these simulations — so the relevant INE comparator is roughly 7.7 to 7.8 million minors, and the shortfall attributable to the dependent-coverage limitation we flag on p.8 is on the order of 3%, not 9%. Rescaling would take Sim. 3's transfer from about 1,680 to roughly 1,630 euros and Piketty's from 2,103 to about 2,040, changing no conclusion; the R–S and Gini results are computed within the Panel population and are internally consistent either way. We will report both counts and add the assumptions to the text.


### What the verifiers flagged as missed by this lens
- **[internal]** While verifying the equivalized long-run finding I found the inconsistency is broader than the finder states: the artefact affects EVERY cell of Table 5's equivalized panel, not only the five rising ones. The falling cells fall far too little - at T=25, NO's RS-predicted Gpost is ~0.6217 vs reported 0.6392 (+1.75 pts gap), Sim.3 +1.89, Piketty +1.83 - while the individual-wealth panel matches RS predictions within 0.0001-0.0006 in all eight scenarios. The gap grows monotonically with the size of the tax (+1.2 pts for IP/CH/FR to +1.9 for Sim.3), which points to a systematic construction step in the equivalized long-run series rather than a cell-specific error, and it contaminates everything built on that series: Tables A.5-A.7's equivalized panels, Figure 5's top-1% equivalized paths, and therefore the slides' headline long-run claim ('top 1% share from ~15% to <8%' under NO/Piketty). Two smaller internal loose ends: (1) Table A.1 reports 170,408 weighted 'Wealth Tax filers' while every results table and the slides use 170,404 effective taxpayers - near-identity of filers and positive-liability payers is itself puzzling given fn.9 says fully-rebated individuals (Madrid, Andalusia) are excluded from taxpayers yet such individuals with wealth above the filing obligation should appear as filers, which bears on how Sim.2's +31,283 un-rebated payers and their liabilities are constructed; (2) Table 3 and A.8 print rounded taxpayer counts for Sim. CH ('12,400,000'/'12.4M') and Piketty ('11,010,000'/'11M') against exact counts for every other scenario. - [Service-Public.gouv.fr — IFI (F563)](https://www.service-public.gouv.fr/particuliers/vosdroits/F563) — IFI liability threshold EUR 1.3M of taxable real-estate wealth; main residence receives a 30% abatement (not an exemption).
- [impots.gouv.fr / economie.gouv.fr — IFI barème and plafonnement (via search results)](https://www.impots.gouv.fr/particulier/calcul-de-lifi) — IFI schedule runs from EUR 800k (0.5%) once over the 1.3M trigger, up to 1.5% above EUR 10M, with a décote for 1.3–1.4M; plafonnement caps IFI plus income taxes at 75% of income — a direct analogue to Spain's 60% cap (seen in search-result summaries; direct fetches of two URLs returned 404/redirect).
- [Brülhart, Gruber, Krapf & Schmidheiny (AEJ: Economic Policy 2022) — AEA abstract page](https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257/pol.20200258) — A 1pp lower wealth tax rate raises reported taxable wealth by at least 43% after 6 years; taxpayer mobility accounts for 24% and housing price responses 21% of the effect.

- [COPE (INE-based report, April 2022)](https://www.cope.es/actualidad/sociedad/noticias/espana-tiene-actualidad-menos-menores-anos-que-hace-casi-siglo-20220427_2049918) — Spain had approximately 8.2 million residents under 18 in 2022 — vs the 7.543M divisor implied by every row of the paper's Table 4.
- [Piketty (2014) proposal summaries (New Republic; Tax Foundation — via search results)](https://newrepublic.com/article/117499/heres-what-we-know-about-thomas-pikettys-wealth-capital-tax) — Piketty's illustrative schedules: 0%/1%/2% (below 1M / 1–5M / above 5M) with a refined variant of 0.1% below 200k and 0.5% for 200k–1M, plus explicit mention of 5–10% rates on the largest fortunes — the paper's Table 2 (0% below 200k, top 2%) is a hybrid; seen in search-result summaries.


## Lens: Measurement and magnitudes

**Lens summary.** On measurement, the paper's core machinery is sound — the Kakwani/Reynolds-Smolensky decomposition closes to printed precision in Table A.4, the simulated IP baseline lands within 8-10% of AEAT's official 2022 outcomes (170,404 payers/EUR 1,126M vs official 185,101/EUR 1,250M), and per-child transfers reconcile exactly with revenues — but the presentation layer has serious unit and consistency failures. Table 3's net-wealth R-S column is silently in per-mille (x1000), which makes the flagship table contradict the paper's own text and turns the authors' slide into a mathematical impossibility, and the slides further convert a 1.6-1.8 Gini-point (4.2-4.9 percent) child-benefit effect into '4-5 Gini points'. Most consequentially, the printed AETRs, revenues and Ginis all cohere on a total-wealth base of ~EUR 5.3T while Table A.1 implies EUR 7.2T — a 35% discrepancy that means either the descriptive statistics or the t_s/r-driven long-run analysis (Tables 5, A.5-A.7, Figure 5) must be restated. External benchmarking, which the paper never performs, shows the 'Norwegian design' raising 1.13% of Spanish GDP versus Norway's actual 0.4-0.5%, largely because the replication uses 2023 rates and omits Norway's shares discount, 1.10% top bracket and 95% secondary-home valuation (SSB: Norwegian taxable wealth is under 30% of market wealth), though per euro of wealth the gap shrinks to ~1.3x. Additional defects — an overlap matrix with logically impossible cells, round CH/Piketty taxpayer counts, an individual-unit Swiss simulation at odds with Switzerland's family taxation, and seven-vs-eight-vs-nine simulation counts — are individually minor but jointly signal the numbers need a full consistency pass before publication.

### [CONFIRMED] Table 3's net-wealth R-S column is silently in per-mille (x1000), making the flagship table and the slides internally contradictory
*id `rs-units-per-mille` · would change conclusions: low · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** In Table 3 (p.21) the individual-net-wealth panel prints R-S values (IP 0.0463, NO 0.5561, Piketty 0.5925) that are exactly 1000x the values implied by the table's own Ginis and by Table A.4 (p.42: 0.0000, 0.0006, 0.0006), while the two income panels of the same table are in natural units (0.0004-0.0050). Nothing in the table, notes, or text flags the unit change. Read at face value, the table contradicts the paper's own statement (p.24) that R-S variation is 'systematically larger' for income than for net wealth, and the slides reproduce the same numbers next to the definition R-S = G_pre - G_post, under which R-S = 0.556 would imply the Gini falling from 0.754 to 0.198.

**Paper evidence.** Table 3, p.21, net-wealth panel: 'IP ... R-S 0.0463 ... Sim. NO 0.5561 ... Sim. Piketty 0.5925' vs disposable-income panel 'IP 0.0008 ... Sim. NO 0.0041'; Table A.4, p.42: R-S = 0.0000 (IP), 0.0006 (NO), 0.0006 (Piketty); G_pre/G_post 0.7538/0.7532 (Piketty, Table 3); p.24: 'the magnitude of the R-S variation is systematically larger for equivalized market income and, especially, for disposable income ... than for net wealth'; slides ('Progressivity, Revenue, and Redistribution' slide) print 0.046/0.556/0.593 beside G_pre 0.754, G_post 0.753-0.754, with the slide 'Measuring Progressivity and Redistribution' defining R-S = G_pre - G_post. Verified against the page render paper-23.png: no units note.

**Arithmetic.** All 8 rows: Table 3 R-S / 1000 = Table A.4 R-S to printed precision (0.0463/1000=0.0000463~0.0000; 0.5561/1000=0.000556~0.0006; 0.1083/1000=0.000108~0.0001; 0.0733/1000=0.0000733~0.0001; 0.1585/1000=0.000159~0.0002; 0.0938/1000=0.0000938~0.0001; 0.5560/1000=0.000556~0.0006; 0.5925/1000=0.000593~0.0006). Cross-check via Ginis: Piketty G_pre - G_post = 0.7538 - 0.7532 = 0.0006 = 0.5925 per mille; reduction 0.0006/0.7538 = 0.0796% matches the printed 0.0796%. Kakwani identity closes in natural units: NO 0.0029 x 0.1912 - 0 = 0.00055 ~ 0.0006; disposable income NO 0.0211 x 0.2587 - 0.0013 = 0.00416 ~ 0.0041. Also the Gini-reduction column is computed from 4-decimal rounded Ginis, so IP prints '0.0000%' even though its own R-S column implies 0.0061%, and FR (true R-S 0.000073) and Sim.1 (0.000159) both print '0.0133%' despite differing 2.2x.

**Suggested question.** The net-wealth R-S column in Table 3 appears to be in units of 10^-3 while the income panels are in natural units — Table A.4 confirms the true values are 0.0000-0.0006. Could you add a units note, and recompute the 'Gini Reduction' column from the unrounded Ginis so that, e.g., IP shows 0.006% rather than 0.0000% and FR and Sim.1 are distinguishable?

### [CONFIRMED] The AETR/t values, revenue totals and Ginis cohere on total net wealth of ~EUR 5.3T, contradicting Table A.1's implied EUR 7.2T (mean EUR 160,540 x 44.86M) by ~35%
*id `wealth-total-inconsistency` · would change conclusions: high · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** Using Table 5's own definition of t_s/r ('total tax liabilities divided by total net wealth'), the printed revenues and t values imply total individual net wealth of ~EUR 5.28-5.38T (mean ~EUR 119k per individual), whereas Table A.1 (p.38) implies 44,857,347 x EUR 160,539.8 = EUR 7.20T. The Kakwani identity proves the ~5.3T base is what the simulations actually use: for Sim. NO, R-S measured from the Ginis (0.00056) can only satisfy R-S <= t/(1-t)xK with t~0.29%, not with the 0.215% that the A.1 total would give. So either Table A.1's mean/median net wealth are wrong (they are also ~29% above the EFF 2022 household mean), or every AETR, t_s/r, and the entire long-run analysis (Tables 5, A.5-A.7, Figure 5) is scaled ~35% too high. Separately, eq. 4 (p.13) defines t as the mean of individual ratios TL_i/y_i, which matches neither printed concept.

**Paper evidence.** Eq. 4, p.13: t defined as 'the mean of ti, which is the ratio between taxpayer's (i) net liability (TLi) and net wealth (yi)'. Table 5 note, p.26: 'ts/r represents the short-run average tax rate, defined as total tax liabilities divided by total net wealth'. Table 3, p.21: IP t=0.02%, T=1,125.58M; NO t=0.29%, T=15,456.06M; Piketty t=0.30%, T=15,860.95M. Table A.1, p.38: Individuals 44,857,347; mean net wealth 160,539.8; median 60,372.43. Table A.4, p.42: t/(1-t)=0.0029 (NO) with K=0.1912.

**External evidence.** Banco de Espana EFF 2022 (seen in search results, bde.es blog 'El analisis de la riqueza en Espana: 20 anos de la EFF', https://www.bde.es/wbe/es/noticias-eventos/blog/el-analisis-de-la-riqueza-en-espana-20-anos-de-la-encuesta-financiera-de-las-familias.html): mean household net wealth EUR 315,600 and median EUR 143,000 in 2022. Applied to the PH's 17,671,019 households that gives ~EUR 5.58T, close to the 5.3T the t-column implies; Table A.1 implies EUR 407.5k per household, 29% above the EFF mean.

**Arithmetic.** Total wealth from A.1: 44,857,347 x 160,539.8 = 7,201,390M. Ratio-of-totals t: NO 15,456.06/7,201,390 = 0.215% vs printed 0.29%; CH 4,842.96/7,201,390 = 0.067% vs 0.09%; Sim.3 12,673.18/7,201,390 = 0.176% vs 0.24%; Piketty 15,860.95/7,201,390 = 0.220% vs 0.30% (only IP coincides via rounding: 0.0156%->0.02%). Implied totals from printed t: 15,456.06/0.0029 = 5,330,000M; 12,673.18/0.0024 = 5,280,000M; 4,842.96/0.0009 = 5,381,000M; 15,860.95/0.0030 = 5,287,000M -> ~5.3T, mean 5.33T/44.857M = EUR 118.8k. Eq.-4 mean-of-ratios for IP over all individuals ~ (6,605/2,052,140) x 170,404/44,857,347 = 0.0012%, matching nothing printed. Identity bound: with t=0.215%, t/(1-t)xK = 0.002151 x 0.1912 = 0.00041 < observed R-S 0.00056 — impossible since D>=0; with t=0.29%: 0.0029 x 0.1912 = 0.00055 — closes. Revenue side is corroborated independently: Table 4 transfer 2,049.05 x implied 7.543M minors = 15,456M = printed T.

**Suggested question.** What is total individual net wealth in your simulation base — the ~EUR 5.3T implied by every t and t_s/r (T/t = 5.28-5.38T across simulations, matching EFF-scale totals), or the EUR 7.2T implied by Table A.1's mean of 160,540? The Kakwani identity only closes at ~5.3T, which suggests A.1's mean and median are the inconsistent numbers — could you confirm, since t_s/r drives the entire long-run analysis in Tables 5 and A.5-A.7?

### [CONFIRMED] Slides claim the child benefit 'could cut disposable income inequality by 4-5 Gini points'; the paper's Table 4 shows 1.6-1.8 points, i.e., 4.2-4.9 percent
*id `slide-gini-points-vs-percent` · would change conclusions: medium · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** The authors' own results slide states the reformed-tax-financed universal child benefit 'could cut disposable income inequality by 4-5 Gini points', but Table 4 (p.25) shows the Gini falling from 0.3779 to 0.3620 (Sim. 3), 0.3606 (NO) and 0.3595 (Piketty) — reductions of 1.59-1.84 Gini points (on a 0-100 scale), which are 4.2-4.9 PERCENT in relative terms. The slide confuses points with percent, overstating the headline effect ~2.5x. The 'trilemma' slide compounds the mixing: it contrasts 'Gini reduction: 0%' (the net-wealth panel figure for IP) with 'Gini reduction: 1.3%' (the disposable-income relative reduction for Sim. 3's tax alone), i.e., different bases in the same comparison.

**Paper evidence.** Slides, 'Revenue as a Universal Child Benefit': 'A universal child benefit financed by a reformed wealth tax could cut disposable income inequality by 4-5 Gini points'. Table 4, p.25: G_pre 0.3779; G_post 0.3620 (Sim. 3, 'Gini Reduction 4.21%', R-S 0.0159), 0.3606 (Sim. NO, 4.58%), 0.3595 (Sim. Piketty, 4.87%). Slides, 'The Wealth Tax Design Trilemma': 'Revenue: EUR 1.1 billion; Gini reduction: 0%' vs 'Revenue: EUR 12.7-15.9 billion; Gini reduction: 1.3%'; paper Table 3, p.21: IP net-wealth reduction 0.0000%, Sim. 3 disposable-income reduction 1.3231%.

**Arithmetic.** Sim. 3: 0.3779 - 0.3620 = 0.0159 = 1.59 Gini points; relative 0.0159/0.3779 = 4.21%. Sim. NO: 0.0173 = 1.73 points = 4.58%. Piketty: 0.0184 = 1.84 points = 4.87%. A genuine 4-5 point cut would mean G_post = 0.328-0.338, which appears nowhere in the paper.

**Suggested question.** Your slide says the child benefit would cut disposable-income inequality by '4-5 Gini points', but Table 4 shows 1.6-1.8 points (4.2-4.9 percent relative). Which is the claim you want on record — and in the trilemma slide, should the '0%' vs '1.3%' cells not use the same base (both are currently net-wealth for IP but disposable-income for the reforms)?

- **internal verifier — CONFIRMED.** The slide quote is verbatim and bolded (image slides-19.png: "could cut disposable income inequality by 4-5 Gini points"), and Table 4 (p.25) shows 0.3779 falling to 0.3620/0.3606/0.3595 — i.e. 1.59/1.73/1.84 Gini points (0-100 scale), which are exactly the printed 4.21/4.58/4.87 PERCENT relative reductions; a genuine 4-5-point cut would require Gpost of 0.328-0.338, which appears nowhere in the paper. So the slide confuses points with percent, overstating ~2.5x. On the trilemma slide (image slides-23.png), the printed pair "Gini reduction: 0%" (IP) vs "1.3%" (Sim. 3) cannot come from any single panel: IP's disposable-income reduction is 0.2117% (not 0%) and Sim. 3's net-wealth reduction is 0.0663% (not 1.3%), while IP's net-wealth panel prints 0.0000% and Sim. 3's disposable panel 1.3231% — so it is either mixed bases (the finder's reading) or, at best, inconsistent rounding (0.2117% floored to "0%" while 1.3231% keeps a decimal); the inconsistency holds under either reading. Note also the 1.3% is the tax-only disposable figure while the same deck's "4-5 points" headline is transfer-inclusive, adding a second base mismatch within the slides.
- **external verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** Internal-only finding (no external claims), so it caps at PLAUSIBLE under my lens; all elements verified. The trilemma-slide mixed-bases charge also verifies: '0%' matches only IP's net-wealth reduction (Table 3: 0.0000%; IP's disposable-income reduction is 0.2117%, which would not round to 0%), while '1.3%' matches only Sim. 3's disposable-income reduction (1.3231%).
