### [CONFIRMED] Simulated baseline is within ~8-10% of official AEAT 2022 outcomes — a validation the paper never reports — while the PH's top tail is thinner than the EFF's own 21%
*id `aeat-baseline-validation` · would change conclusions: low · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** AEAT's official statistics for ejercicio 2022 show 185,101 declarants with positive cuota a ingresar totalling EUR 1,250.0M (mean EUR 6,753); the paper's simulated baseline is 170,404 effective taxpayers and EUR 1,125.58M (mean EUR 6,605) — undershoots of 8.0% and 10.0%, an excellent match that the paper nowhere reports. Less favorably, the PH records only 170,408 weighted 'Wealth Tax filers' versus 230,365 official declarants (-26%), implying essentially zero of the ~45,264 official zero-quota filers (mostly Madrid/Andalusia >EUR 2M-gross filers) are identifiable in the data, and the PH baseline top-1% individual net-wealth share (18.76%, Table A.5) sits below the EFF-based 'just over 21%' the authors themselves cite (p.1) — so revenue from top-concentrated designs (Sim. 2, FR, Piketty's 2% band above EUR 5M) is likely understated.

**Paper evidence.** Table 3, p.21: Ntp 170,404, T = 1,125.58M. Table A.1, p.38: 'Wealth Tax filers 170,408'. Footnote 9, p.20: 'Individuals whose tax returns result in a positive effective tax liability after applying regional deductions and allowances are considered taxpayers. Those whose entire liability is fully rebated—such as in Madrid and Andalusia in the 2022 fiscal year—are excluded.' Footnote 2, p.5: ITSGF 'not modelled here'. p.1: 'the share of total net wealth held by the top 1% increased from 13.3% in 2002 to just over 21% in 2022' (EFF). Table A.5, p.44: baseline S top 1% = 18.76%, top 10% = 57.28% (individual); Tables A.6-A.7, p.45-46: equivalized top 1% = 14.67%.

**External evidence.** FETCHED — AEAT, Estadistica de los declarantes del IP 2022, resumen por CCAA (https://sede.agenciatributaria.gob.es/AEAT/Contenidos_Comunes/La_Agencia_Tributaria/Estadisticas/Publicaciones/sites/patrimonio/2022/jrubikf35674559c541e9fae2e571b2c13901892c339b84.html): declarantes 230,365; patrimonio total EUR 864,939,842,272; cuota integra EUR 5,346,231,310; resultado de la declaracion EUR 1,250,014,962. FETCHED — AEAT partidas, '055 Cuota a ingresar' (https://sede.agenciatributaria.gob.es/AEAT/Contenidos_Comunes/La_Agencia_Tributaria/Estadisticas/Publicaciones/sites/patrimonio/2022/jrubik1dc7edc50ff9f5c7989bb0b253649f3263a31d70.html): 185,101 declarants with amount due; EUR 1,250,014,962; media EUR 6,753. FETCHED — La Moncloa (https://www.lamoncloa.gob.es/serviciosdeprensa/notasprensa/hacienda/paginas/2023/200923-impuesto-solidaridad-grandes-fortunas.aspx): ITSGF 2023 EUR 623M from 12,010 taxpayers; combined IP+ITSGF 2023 'mas de 1.868 millones'.

**Arithmetic.** Taxpayers: 170,404/185,101 - 1 = -7.9%. Revenue: 1,125.58/1,250.01 - 1 = -10.0%. Mean liability: 1,125.58e6/170,404 = 6,605 vs official 6,753 (-2.2%). Zero-quota filers: official 230,365 - 185,101 = 45,264 vs PH 170,408 - 170,404 = 4 (weighted). Top-tail: at the EFF share, top-1% wealth would be 21/18.76 - 1 = +11.9% higher at equal totals, mechanically raising revenue of top-concentrated designs by a similar order.

**Suggested question.** Your simulated baseline lands within 8-10% of AEAT's official 2022 outcomes (185,101 payers, EUR 1,250M) — why not report that validation? And given the PH holds only 4 weighted zero-quota filers versus 45,264 in the official statistics and a top-1% share of 18.76% versus the EFF's 21%+, how much of Sim. 2's, FR's and Piketty's top-band revenue do you think is missing from the thinner top tail?

- **internal verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** Every paper-side premise checks out: Ntp 170,404 and T = 1,125.58M (Table 3, p.21; mean = 6,605 recomputed), filers 170,408 (Table A.1, p.38, image-verified — a weighted gap of only 4 vs effective taxpayers), footnote 9's Madrid/Andalusia exclusion and footnote 2's ITSGF exclusion quoted accurately, top-1% individual share 18.76% and top-10% 57.28% (Table A.5), equivalized 14.67% (A.6), and no comparison to official AEAT outcomes appears anywhere in the paper. However, the load-bearing external figures (AEAT 185,101 payers / EUR 1,250.0M / 230,365 declarants; La Moncloa ITSGF) cannot be re-verified under this internal-evidence pass, so the 8-10% match is conditional on the finder's fetches. One overstatement: the 18.76%-vs-21% comparison mixes units — the p.1 EFF figure comes from a household survey while 18.76% is the individual-level PH share (the paper's own equivalized share is 14.67%), so "thinner than the EFF's own 21%" needs a same-unit benchmark before quantifying understated top-band revenue.
  - *Corrected claim:* The paper's simulated baseline (170,404 payers, EUR 1,125.58M, mean EUR 6,605) is never benchmarked against official AEAT 2022 outcomes; if the finder's fetched AEAT figures (185,101 positive-quota declarants, EUR 1,250.0M) hold, it lands within 8-10% — a validation worth reporting — and the PH shows only 4 weighted zero-liability filers versus ~45k official ones. The top-tail point should be softened: 18.76% is the individual-level top-1% share while the EFF's 'just over 21%' (p.1) is a household-survey figure (the paper's equivalized share is 14.67%), so a same-unit comparison is needed before quantifying revenue understatement for top-concentrated designs.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** All external figures re-verified on primary sources. AEAT's IP 2022 statistics (re-fetched, both pages): 230,365 declarantes, patrimonio EUR 864,939,842,272, cuota integra EUR 5,346,231,310, resultado de la declaracion EUR 1,250,014,962; partida 055 'Cuota a ingresar': 185,101 declarants, EUR 1,250,014,962, media EUR 6,753 — so the paper's 170,404 payers / EUR 1,125.58M (Table 3, p.21) undershoot by 7.9% and 10.0%, and official zero-quota filers number 45,264 vs 4 weighted in the PH (170,408 - 170,404). La Moncloa confirms ITSGF 2023: EUR 623M from 12,010 taxpayers (10,302 in Madrid) and combined IP+ITSGF 'mas de 1.868 millones de euros', and BOE Ley 19/1991 Art. 37 confirms the >EUR 2M gross-wealth filing obligation ('...el valor de sus bienes o derechos...resulte superior a 2.000.000 euros') that generates zero-quota Madrid/Andalusia filers. I read the full paper: no validation against these official outcomes is reported anywhere. One caveat: the '21%+' EFF top-1% share (p.1) is household-based (via Conde-Ruiz & Garcia-Rodriguez 2025) while Table A.5's 18.76% is individual-level, so that particular comparison is indicative rather than exact; the PH's individual equivalized top-1% is 14.67% (Tables A.6-A.7).
- **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** I independently re-fetched both AEAT pages and confirmed every official figure: 230,365 declarants, patrimonio EUR 864,939,842,272, cuota integra EUR 5,346,231,310, resultado EUR 1,250,014,962, and partida 055 showing 185,101 declarants with cuota a ingresar at media EUR 6,753. The paper's baseline (170,404 payers, EUR 1,125.58M, mean EUR 6,605) is thus -7.9%/-10.0%/-2.2% against officials — a genuinely strong validation — and having read the full paper I confirm no comparison to AEAT outcomes appears anywhere. The zero-quota gap is also verified from the paper's own tables: A.1 reports 170,408 'filers' vs 170,404 payers (4 weighted zero-quota individuals) against 45,264 official zero-quota declarants, and Sim.2 adds only 31,283 payers when rebates are removed — below the official zero-quota population it should roughly activate, which matters for Sim.2's EUR 2,405M. The top-tail sub-claim is the softest part: 18.76% (Table A.5) is an individual-distribution share while the EFF's 21%+ (p.1) is a household share, so the comparison is not unit-clean, though the direction (thin top tail, business assets undervalued per the authors' own p.34 limitation) is plausible and the AEAT filer-wealth gap points the same way. The actionable substance — report the validation, explain the filer definition, bound the top-tail understatement — survives any response.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** We thank the discussant — landing within 8-10% of AEAT's official 2022 outcomes without targeting them is a validation we should have reported, and since the IP baseline is simulated on declared values (p.9), the comparison is legitimate; we will add a validation table. On the filer count: the PH wealth module identifies filers through returns with effective liability, so the roughly 45,000 zero-quota declarants — overwhelmingly Madrid and Andalusia — enter our data as ordinary non-filers; this does not bias the baseline, where they pay zero, but we agree it matters most for Sim. 2, whose EUR 2.4bn is precisely the reform that would activate them, so that figure is best read as a lower bound. On the top tail, the 18.76% is an individual-distribution share while the EFF's 21% is a household share, so they are not directly comparable; that said, we already flag business-asset undervaluation among high-wealth filers as a limitation (p.34), and we accept that revenue under the top-concentrated designs (Sim. 2, FR, Piketty's 2% band) is more likely understated than overstated.


### [CONFIRMED] The taxpayer overlap matrix (Table A.8) contains logically impossible cells
*id `overlap-matrix-impossible` · would change conclusions: low · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** Table A.8 (p.47) reports overlap(Sim. Piketty, Sim. 2) = 297,219, which exceeds Sim. 2's own total of 201,687 taxpayers — an intersection larger than one of its sets. It also reports overlap(IP, Sim. 1) = 170,404 and overlap(IP, Sim. 2) = 170,404 (i.e., IP's payers are subsets of both reforms), which forces overlap(Sim. 1, Sim. 2) >= 170,404, yet the printed cell is 72,186. At least two cells of the matrix cannot be correct, and the cell overlap(IP, Piketty) = 166,827 — implying 3,577 current IP payers would escape a EUR 200k-threshold Piketty tax — needs a mechanism (the IRPF-IP cap is excluded from foreign/Piketty designs per p.10).

**Paper evidence.** Table A.8, p.47: row 'Sim. Piketty', column 'Sim. 2' = 297,219; Np row: Sim. 2 = 201,687; row 'Sim. 1', column IP = 170,404; row 'Sim. 2', column IP = 170,404; row 'Sim. 2', column 'Sim. 1' = 72,186; row 'Sim. Piketty', column IP = 166,827. p.10: 'the simulations of international models do not incorporate Spain's joint IRPF-IP tax-liability limit'.

**Arithmetic.** 297,219 - 201,687 = 95,532 excess over the set's cardinality. Subset logic: IP (170,404) contained in Sim. 1 and in Sim. 2 implies |Sim.1 ∩ Sim.2| >= 170,404 > 72,186 (shortfall 98,218). Also |Sim.2| = 201,687 vs |IP| = 170,404 means removing rebates adds only 31,283 payers.

**Suggested question.** In Table A.8, the Piketty-Sim.2 overlap (297,219) exceeds Sim. 2's total taxpayer count (201,687), and the Sim.1-Sim.2 overlap (72,186) is below the 170,404 IP payers that both scenarios contain — could you re-generate the matrix, and explain how 3,577 current IP payers avoid liability under a EUR 200k Piketty threshold?

- **internal verifier — CONFIRMED.** All cells verified against the page image (paper-49.png) and the extracted text: overlap(Piketty, Sim. 2) = 297,219 exceeds Sim. 2's own Np of 201,687 by 95,532 — an intersection larger than one of its sets, which is logically impossible. Separately, overlap(IP, Sim. 1) = 170,404 = |IP| and overlap(IP, Sim. 2) = 170,404 = |IP| jointly force IP as a subset of both reforms, hence |Sim.1 ∩ Sim.2| >= 170,404, yet the printed cell is 72,186 (shortfall 98,218) — a second independent impossibility, so at least two entries cannot be correct. I swept every other cell for set-consistency and found no further violations, though overlap(IP, Piketty) = 166,827 does imply 3,577 current IP payers (taxable wealth above EUR 700k under IP rules) escaping a EUR 200k-threshold Piketty tax with no mechanism offered, and the p.10 quote about excluding the IRPF-IP cap from international designs is accurate (the cap could only reduce, not create, IP liability, deepening the puzzle). The arithmetic asides (201,687-170,404 = 31,283) also check.
- **external verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** Internal-only finding (no external claims), so it caps at PLAUSIBLE under my lens; the logic is verified and unassailable. Table A.8 (p.47) as printed: overlap(Sim. Piketty, Sim. 2) = 297,219 while Np(Sim. 2) = 201,687 — an intersection 95,532 larger than one of its sets, which is impossible; and overlap(IP, Sim. 1) = overlap(IP, Sim. 2) = 170,404 = |IP| makes IP a subset of both, forcing |Sim.1 ∩ Sim.2| >= 170,404 against the printed 72,186 — a second impossibility. Both are theorems of set arithmetic on the printed numbers, not judgment calls. The IP∩Piketty = 166,827 cell (3,577 current IP payers escaping a EUR 200k-threshold, no-cap Piketty tax) genuinely lacks a stated mechanism; p.10 confirms the IRPF-IP cap is excluded from international/Piketty designs, removing the most obvious escape route.
- **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** Verified directly in Table A.8 (p.47): the Piketty-Sim.2 cell reads 297,219 while Sim.2's own Np is 201,687 — an intersection 95,532 larger than one of its sets, which no data-generating process can produce. The subset contradiction also checks: overlap(IP, Sim.1) = overlap(IP, Sim.2) = 170,404 = |IP|, so IP is contained in both and |Sim.1 ∩ Sim.2| must be at least 170,404, yet the printed cell is 72,186 (shortfall 98,218). At least two cells are wrong and the matrix must be regenerated; the authors have no counter beyond conceding. The IP-Piketty cell (166,827, implying 3,577 IP payers escape a EUR 200k-threshold universal tax) is genuinely anomalous — a positive-liability IP payer has a taxable base above ~EUR 500-700k, and market-adjusted net wealth should exceed that, so a mechanism (or the same error) is owed; the finder is right to demand one rather than assert one. Purely descriptive table, so no headline result depends on it — consistent with the finding's 'low' impact rating.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** We concede the matrix as printed contains errors: an intersection cannot exceed the smaller set, and with the IP payers contained in both Sim. 1 and Sim. 2 the Sim.1-Sim.2 cell cannot be below 170,404 — the 297,219 and 72,186 cells are evidently transposition or paste errors from an earlier run, and we will regenerate Table A.8 from the final simulation output. The matrix is purely descriptive and no result in the body of the paper draws on it. On the IP-Piketty cell we will verify whether the 3,577 individuals are genuine — for instance filers whose IP base rests on acquisition values above their market-adjusted net wealth, or with debts secured against exempt assets — or whether that cell is part of the same error; we will not defend it until we have rerun the matrix.


### [CONFIRMED] 'Norwegian design' raises 1.13% of Spanish GDP — 2.4x Norway's own wealth-tax yield share — and replicates 2023 rates while omitting Norway's shares discount, top bracket and secondary-home valuation
*id `norway-design-magnitude` · would change conclusions: medium · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** Sim. NO's EUR 15,456M equals 1.13% of Spain's 2022 GDP (EUR 1,373,629M, INE), versus Norway's actual formuesskatt of ~0.4% of GDP pre-2022 (SSB) and ~NOK 26.5bn (~0.5% of GDP) in 2022 after rates nearly doubled. The paper's replication (flat 1% = the 2023 rate, EUR 160k threshold doubled for couples, housing at 25%) omits, relative to the actual 2022 Norwegian rules it claims to apply to 2022 data: the 0.95% combined 2022 rate, the 1.10% bracket above NOK 20M (~EUR 1.9M), the 50% primary-residence valuation above NOK 10M, the 95% valuation of secondary dwellings (the paper appears to discount ALL housing to 25%), and the 25% valuation discount on shares and operating assets — SSB shows these discounts leave Norwegian taxable wealth below 30% of market wealth. Much of the GDP-share gap is legitimately explained by Spain's higher household wealth/GDP ratio (per unit of wealth, Sim. NO's 0.29% bite is only ~1.3x Norway's actual ~0.22%), but the paper neither benchmarks its replication against Norway's own yield nor states which rules were dropped.

**Paper evidence.** p.5: 'primary residences are valued at 25% of market value, and a general deduction applies, followed by a flat 1% rate'. p.10: 'a flat 1% rate applied to the tax base, after adjusting housing values to 25% of their estimated market value'. Table A.2, p.40: 'Low threshold (EUR 160,000 individual) ... Flat rate 1% ... State-municipal split rate (0.3% + 0.7%); married couples: double threshold'. Table 3, p.21: Sim. NO T = 15,456.06M, Ntp 7,647,459, t = 0.29%. p.4: 'our baseline is the 2022 IP framework'. p.34 limitations: 'the simulations assume no behavioural responses'.

**External evidence.** FETCHED — Skatteetaten Forskuddsmeldingen 2022 (https://www.skatteetaten.no/en/rettskilder/type/skattedirektoratets-meldinger/forskuddsmeldingen-2022/): 2022 combined rate '0,95 prosent'; '1,10' above NOK 20M; bunnfradrag NOK 1.7M (3.4M jointly); primary residence 25% up to NOK 10M, 50% above; secondary dwellings 95%; shares/operating assets 75% (25% discount). FETCHED — SSB Discussion Paper 960, 'A wealth tax at work' (https://www.ssb.no/en/inntekt-og-forbruk/skatt-for-personer/artikler/a-wealth-tax-at-work/...DP960_web.pdf): revenue 'approximately 15 billion NOK per year, which corresponds to around 1.1 percent of the total tax revenue and 0.4 percent of GDP'; 'Total taxable net wealth in households amounted to roughly 1,800 billion NOK in 2018, whereas market wealth is estimated to 6,800 billion NOK'. FETCHED — Norsk Industri (https://www.norskindustri.no/dette-jobber-vi-med/okonomi/statsbudsjettet/formuesskatten-oker-kraftig-i-2023/): 'formuesskatten nesten ble fordoblet til 26,5 milliarder kroner' (2022), ~NOK 29.5-30bn in 2023, aksjerabatt 25%->20% in 2023. SEEN IN SEARCH — INE Contabilidad Nacional, Revision Estadistica 2024 (https://ine.es/dyngs/Prensa/CNA2023.htm): Spain 2022 GDP EUR 1,373,629M. SEEN IN SEARCH — SSB national accounts article (https://www.ssb.no/en/nasjonalregnskap-og-konjunkturer/nasjonalregnskap/statistikk/nasjonalregnskap/artikler/norwegian-economy-in-2022-high-growth-high-prices): 2022 nominal GDP rose 32.2%, an increase of NOK 1,358bn (implying ~NOK 5,575bn total).

**Arithmetic.** 15,456.06/1,373,629 = 1.125% of Spanish GDP (Sim. 3: 12,673.18/1,373,629 = 0.92%; Piketty: 15,860.95/1,373,629 = 1.15%; CH: 0.35%; IP: 0.08% vs official 1,250/1,373,629 = 0.09%). Norway 2022: 26.5/5,575 = 0.48% of GDP; pre-2022 SSB figure 0.4%. Ratio: 1.125/0.48 = 2.4x. Per unit of wealth: Sim. NO 15,456/5,330,000 = 0.29% of the coherent PH wealth total vs Norway 15,000/6,800,000 = 0.22% (2018) -> 1.3x, consistent with the omitted 25% shares discount; NOK 20M at ~10.6 NOK/EUR = EUR 1.89M for the omitted 1.10% bracket.

**Suggested question.** Norway's own wealth tax raised about 0.4-0.5% of GDP in 2022 (NOK 26.5bn), while your Norwegian design on Spanish data raises 1.13% of GDP; per euro of household wealth the two are much closer (0.29% vs 0.22%). Could you state explicitly which 2022 Norwegian rules you dropped — the 0.95% rate, the 1.10% bracket above NOK 20M, 95% secondary-home valuation, and the 25% shares/operating-asset discount — and show a sensitivity with the shares discount switched on, since SSB reports Norwegian taxable wealth is under 30% of market wealth?

- **internal verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** Everything checkable internally checks out: the paper describes Sim. NO as only a flat 1% on a EUR 160k threshold (doubled for couples, 0.3%+0.7% split) with housing at 25% (p.5 says "primary residences", p.10 says "housing values", supporting the finder's hedged "appears to discount ALL housing"), asserts Norway has "no progressive brackets" (p.10) and claims to "fully replicate the current Norwegian... wealth taxes" (p.2) while citing the 2023 Skatteloven — and it nowhere benchmarks Sim. NO's EUR 15,456M against Norway's own yield or lists dropped rules. The arithmetic is correct given the external inputs (15,456.06/1,373,629 = 1.125% of GDP; 26.5/5,575 = 0.48%; ratio 2.34x; per-wealth 0.29% vs 0.22% = 1.3x using the internally-coherent 5.33T base). However, the substantive premises — Norway's actual 2022 rate structure (0.95%, 1.10% bracket, 95% secondary dwellings, 25% shares discount), its NOK 26.5bn 2022 yield, Spanish GDP, and SSB's taxable-vs-market wealth ratio — are all external fetches I cannot re-verify in this internal-evidence pass, so the comparison, while fair-minded (it concedes wealth/GDP explains most of the GDP-share gap), rests on evidence outside the paper.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** Every external figure re-verified on primary sources. Skatteetaten Forskuddsmeldingen 2022: state 0.25% (0.40% above NOK 20M) + municipal 0.7% = 0.95%/1.10%, bunnfradrag NOK 1.7M/3.4M, primary residence 25% up to NOK 10M and 50% above, secondary dwellings 95%, shares/operating assets 75%; Skatteetaten's 2023 rates page: municipal 0.7% + state 0.3% (0.4% above 20M) = flat 1.0% — exactly the '0.3% + 0.7%' split in the paper's Table A.2 (p.40), proving the paper transplanted the 2023 standard rate while dropping the top bracket, the shares discount, and the secondary/luxury-home valuations. SSB DP960 verbatim: 'only approximately 15 billion NOK per year, which corresponds to around 1.1 percent of the total tax revenue and 0.4 percent of GDP' and 'Total taxable net wealth in households amounted to roughly 1,800 billion NOK in 2018, whereas market wealth is estimated to 6,800 billion NOK... less than 30 percent'. Norsk Industri: 'nesten ble fordoblet til 26,5 milliarder kroner' (2022), 29.5bn proposed 2023, aksjerabatt 25%->20%; INE: Spain 2022 GDP EUR 1,373,629M (exact); SSB: 2022 nominal GDP '+32.2 per cent, corresponding to 1 358 billion NOK' (implying ~5,575bn, so 26.5/5,575 = 0.48% and 15,456.06/1,373,629 = 1.13% — the 2.4x ratio holds). Two wording nuances: 'nearly doubled' in the source refers to the tax take, not the rates; and the paper claims to 'fully replicate' the legislation 'currently in force' (p.10) rather than 2022 rules specifically — which sharpens rather than weakens the criticism, since neither the 2022 nor 2023 law is a flat 1% on all wealth with all housing at 25% and no shares discount.
  - *Corrected claim:* Minor wording only: Norway's tax take (not its rates) nearly doubled to NOK 26.5bn in 2022 (rates rose 0.85%->0.95%/1.10% while valuation discounts were cut); and the paper claims to replicate Norwegian legislation 'currently in force' — its 0.3%+0.7% split matches 2023, not the 2022 rules contemporaneous with its data. All quantitative claims stand as stated.
- **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** The arithmetic is verified: 15,456.06/1,373,629 = 1.125% of Spain's 2022 GDP (and still 1.15% under the pre-revision GDP of ~1,346bn, so the conclusion is robust to the GDP vintage), versus Norway's own formuesskatt at ~0.4-0.5% of GDP — and the paper never reports this benchmark nor states which Norwegian rules were dropped (p.5/p.10 describe only 'flat 1%' and housing at 25%; Table A.2 adds the EUR 160k threshold, 0.3%+0.7% split and doubled couple threshold). The omissions are real under either legislative vintage: the 1.10% bracket above NOK 20M and the shares/operating-asset valuation discount (25% in 2022, 20% in 2023) exist in both years, and secondary dwellings were never at 25%; the finder's Skatteetaten and SSB quotes were not re-fetched by me but match the standard published 2022 parameters and the SSB DP960 figures. Critically, the intro claims the paper will 'fully replicate the current Norwegian, Swiss and French wealth taxes' (p.4), which converts these silent omissions from stylization into an accuracy gap the authors must concede. The finding already concedes the strongest author defense (per-euro-of-wealth the bite is 0.29% vs ~0.22%, so wealth/GDP explains most of the GDP-share gap), so what remains — no benchmark, undisclosed dropped rules, 'fully replicate' language — survives intact.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** Our Norwegian simulation is a stylized transplant intended to isolate the broad-base, flat-rate feature, and Table A.2 documents what we applied — the 1% rate is the 2023 combined state-municipal rate (0.3% + 0.7%), consistent with our stated benchmark of legislation currently in force. We concede we did not implement the 1.1% bracket above NOK 20 million, the higher valuation of secondary dwellings, or the 25% valuation discount on shares and operating assets, and that 'fully replicate' in the introduction overstates the exercise; we will write 'inspired by' and list the departures explicitly. On the benchmark, we would push back on GDP shares as the fairness test: as the discussant himself computes, per euro of household wealth our 0.29% is close to Norway's actual 0.22%, and the remainder reflects Spain's higher wealth-to-GDP ratio and our stated no-behavioural-response assumption (p.34). Adding the shares discount and the top bracket as a sensitivity is cheap and we are happy to include it — the shares discount would lower revenue and the top bracket raise it, so the headline is unlikely to move dramatically.


### [CONFIRMED] Swiss simulation applies a family-based tax to 44.9M individuals (12.4M 'taxpayers', 27.6% of all persons including minors), and the CH/Piketty counts are suspiciously round
*id `swiss-tax-unit-and-round-counts` · would change conclusions: medium · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** Switzerland taxes wealth at the family level — spouses' wealth is aggregated and minor children's wealth is added to the custodial parent (Art. 3(3) StHG, per the Swiss Federal Tax Administration) — with Zurich's tax-free allowance about CHF 80k single / 159k married (currently; ~77k/154k before the 2024 indexation, 2022-specific value UNVERIFIED). The paper instead applies the Zurich schedule to individuals in a base of 44,857,347 persons whose mean age (43.5) shows it includes minors, yielding 12,400,000 'taxpayers' = 27.6% of all persons; under Swiss law those minors could not be separate wealth-tax payers, and individual-level application of a progressive tariff on split wealth changes both counts and progressivity. Moreover, Sim. CH (12,400,000) and Sim. Piketty (11,010,000) are the only taxpayer counts printed as round numbers — Table A.8 even prints them as '12.4M' and '11M' — versus person-level precision elsewhere (7,647,459; 1,834,488).

**Paper evidence.** p.10: 'The simulation follows the structure of the Canton of Zurich, applying a progressive schedule with reduced rates and valuing real estate at 70% of its estimated market value'. Table A.2, p.40: 'Cantonal thresholds' (no figure given). Table 3, p.21: Sim. CH Ntp 12,400,000; Sim. Piketty 11,010,000. Table A.8, p.47: Np row '12.4M' and '11M'. Table A.1, p.38: Individuals 44,857,347, mean age 43.53; household types with children 42.23% combined; Table 4's transfers imply 7.543M minors in the data (see arithmetic). Figure 3, p.19: AETR x-axis is 'percentiles of the net wealth distribution' over these individuals; p.19 text: Swiss 'clear step around p67'.

**External evidence.** FETCHED — Swiss Federal Tax Administration, 'Vermogenssteuer naturlicher Personen' (https://www.estv2.admin.ch/stp/ds/d-vermoegenssteuer-np-de.pdf): 'Ehegatten und Minderjahrige sind jedoch in der Regel nicht selbststandig steuerpflichtig ... das Vermogen der in ungetrennter Ehe lebenden Ehegatten wird zusammengerechnet (Art. 3 Abs. 3 StHG)'; 'das Vermogen von minderjahrigen Kindern [wird] dem Inhaber der elterlichen Sorge zugerechnet'. FETCHED — neho.ch Zurich wealth-tax guide (https://neho.ch/de/blog/vermogenssteuer-zurich): tax-free CHF 80,000 single / 159,000 married; basic tariff 0.5 to 3 per mille, top bracket above CHF 3.262M.

**Arithmetic.** 12,400,000/44,857,347 = 27.6% of all persons; Spain's PH implies 7.543M minors (each simulation's revenue / per-child transfer: 15,456.06e6/2,049.05 = 7.543M; 1,125.58e6/149.22 = 7.543M), so excluding minors the share is 12.4M/37.3M = 33% of adults. Zurich CHF 80,000 ~ EUR 82k at ~0.97-1.03 CHF/EUR; with real estate at 70%, an all-housing individual needs ~EUR 117k market wealth to cross it, versus median individual net wealth EUR 60,372 (Table A.1) — internally consistent with the paper's own 'step around p67' (33% of persons), but only if wealth is genuinely individually attributed.

**Suggested question.** Swiss law taxes spouses jointly and attributes minors' wealth to parents; your Sim. CH reports 12.4 million individual taxpayers out of a base that includes roughly 7.5 million minors. How is household wealth attributed to individuals in the PH, what Zurich threshold and multiplier (Steuerfuss) did you apply, and why are the CH and Piketty counts (12,400,000 / 11,010,000) the only round numbers in Table 3?

- **internal verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** The internal skeleton is fully confirmed: the paper's only CH specification is the p.10 sentence plus Table A.2's "Cantonal thresholds" with no figure; the simulation base is 44,857,347 individuals with mean age 43.53 and ~7.543M implied minors (T/transfer is identical to 5-6 digits across all eight Table 4 rows — solid); Sim. CH's 12,400,000 and Piketty's 11,010,000 are the only round taxpayer counts in Table 3 (vs 170,404; 7,647,459; 117,585; 1,834,488; 201,687; 4,626,929), and Table A.8 literally prints "12.4M" and "11M" (image paper-49.png); 12.4M = 27.6% of persons and 33.2% of adults, both recomputed. The Swiss-law premise (spousal aggregation, minors' wealth attributed to parents, Zurich CHF 80k/159k allowances) is external and cannot be re-verified here — the finder itself marks the 2022 allowance UNVERIFIED — and whether individual-level application materially changes counts and progressivity is a judgment call not decidable from the paper. One arithmetic slip: the "(33% of persons)" gloss on the p67 step conflates adults with persons — 12.4M is the top 27.6% of the 44.86M-person x-axis, implying an onset near p72, so the paper's "clear step around p67" is in mild tension with its own count rather than cleanly consistent with it.
  - *Corrected claim:* Keep the question but fix one gloss: 12.4M taxpayers is 27.6% of the 44.86M individuals on the AETR x-axis (onset ~p72, so the paper's "step around p67" slightly overshoots its own count) and 33% of the ~37.3M adults. The core asks stand: how is household wealth attributed to individuals, what Zurich threshold/Steuerfuss was applied (none is disclosed), why a family-based tax is simulated on an individual base containing ~7.5M minors (Swiss-law premise external, pending verification), and why CH and Piketty are the only round counts.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** The Swiss-law core is confirmed verbatim from the primary source: the ESTV Dossier 'Vermogenssteuer naturlicher Personen' (September 2024 edition, fetched as PDF and text-extracted) states 'Ehegatten und Minderjahrige sind jedoch in der Regel nicht selbststandig steuerpflichtig', 'Das Vermogen der in ungetrennter Ehe lebenden Ehegatten wird zusammengerechnet (Art. 3 Abs. 3 StHG)', and 'Das Vermogen der minderjahrigen Kinder wird zum Vermogen des Inhabers der elterlichen Sorge [zugerechnet]' — family-unit taxation exactly as claimed. The official ESTV Kantonsblatt Zurich shows the § 47 StG wealth-tax tariff with a 0-per-mille band of CHF 81,000 single / 161,000 married (separate Verheiratetentarif; values valid from 2026, indexed every two years per the Dossier), and neho.ch gives CHF 80,000/159,000 for 2025 with the 0.5-3 per-mille Grundtarif — so the finder's ~CHF 80k magnitude is right and the 2022-specific 77k/154k remains formally unverified, exactly as the finder flagged. Internal numbers all check out: Sim. CH Ntp 12,400,000 = 27.6% of 44,857,347 (Table 3/A.1), 7.543M implied minors from Table 4's revenue/transfer ratios, and CH/Piketty are the only round counts (printed '12.4M'/'11M' in Table A.8).
- **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** Every paper-side fact checks out: Sim. CH applies the Zurich schedule to individuals (p.10) in a 44,857,347-person base whose ~7.543M minors I verified twice via Table 4's transfer arithmetic (15,456.06e6/2,049.05 = 1,125.58e6/149.22 = 7.543M), producing 12,400,000 'taxpayers' = 27.6% of all persons (33% of adults, matching the paper's own 'step around p67' only approximately); Table A.2 gives no Zurich threshold ('Cantonal thresholds'); and the CH/Piketty counts are the only rounded ones (Table 3: 12,400,000 and 11,010,000; A.8: '12.4M', '11M'). The Swiss family-unit rule (spousal aggregation, minors' wealth to parents, Art. 3(3) StHG) is standard law consistent with the finder's ESTV quote (not re-fetched by me; the 2022 Zurich allowance is honestly flagged UNVERIFIED). The sharpest point survives all defenses: Table A.2 shows the authors DID model the family unit for Norway (doubled couple threshold) but not for Switzerland, and the paper claims to 'fully replicate' foreign legislations — so the individual-unit CH simulation is a documented deviation, not just a framing choice, and applying a progressive tariff individually to split spousal wealth mechanically changes counts and liabilities. The authors' comparability defense (register wealth is individually attributed; Spain's IP is individual-based) is real but is a justification, not a refutation; they must still concede the deviation, the unstated threshold, and the rounded counts.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** The PH records wealth at the individual level directly from tax registers — property ownership shares, accounts, and securities are individually attributed — so no artificial splitting was performed, and we harmonized every design to the individual unit because Spain's IP is itself individual-based; minors hold essentially no wealth, so the 12.4 million effective taxpayers are in practice about a third of adults, which is what the step near p67 in Figure 3 reflects. We concede this is not the Swiss family unit — spouses are taxed jointly under Art. 3(3) StHG — and since we did apply the couple rule in the Norwegian simulation (doubled threshold, Table A.2), we accept the asymmetric treatment must at least be flagged: applying Zurich's progressive tariff to split individual wealth mechanically lowers liabilities and raises counts relative to joint taxation, so Sim. CH should be read as 'Zurich parameters on Spanish individual units' rather than a replication of Swiss law. We will state the exact Zurich threshold and multiplier we applied — 'cantonal thresholds' is too vague — and replace 12,400,000 and 11,010,000 with the exact counts, which are display roundings rather than generated figures.

Figure 5's equivalized top-1% shares show no such jump, isolating the anomaly to the equivalized Gini series. ## Lens: Literature and behavioural evidence

**Lens summary.** The paper's zero-behavioural-response assumption (stated only in the limitations, p.34) sits well outside the verified empirical literature, including the three quasi-experimental studies the paper itself cites: Brulhart et al. (2022) find reported wealth rises at least 43% per 1pp of wealth tax after 6 years, Jakobsen et al. (2020) imply long-run taxable-wealth elasticities up to 1.15 for the very wealthy, and Mas-Montserrat et al. (2025) find Spanish taxable wealth falls 3.21% per 0.1pp of average tax rate with avoidance costing 2.75x the 2011 revenue — yet the paper characterizes this literature as "moderate" responses. A transparent ready-reckoner using these verified semi-elasticities shrinks the headline static revenues (Sim. NO EUR 15.5bn, Sim. 3 EUR 12.7bn, Piketty EUR 15.9bn) by roughly 7-17% under the Advani-Tarrant design-based range, ~8-10% under the Spain-specific Mas-Montserrat coefficient applied conservatively, and up to ~43% under the Swiss upper bound — and the child-benefit and 100-year concentration results inherit this erosion one-for-one. Because erosion scales with the tax rate, ignoring behaviour is not ranking-neutral: it flatters high-top-rate designs (Sim. 3's 3.5% top bracket) relative to Norway's 1% flat tax, which ironically means the behavioural literature strengthens the paper's own qualitative conclusion (breadth over rates) while weakening its quantitative claims. The most consequential missing citation is Agrawal, Foremny and Martinez-Toledano (AEJ: Applied 2025) on Madrid's zero rate — directly on-point for Sims 2-3, showing wealth-tax mobility causes income-tax losses six times the direct wealth-tax losses and that a harmonized positive rate would raise Madrid's total revenue, a result that would actually arm the authors' harmonization case. Finally, the "reform" scenarios leave untouched the two state-level features (family-business exemption and the IRPF-IP cap) that the paper's own sources identify as the dominant avoidance and regressivity channels, and the Sec. 3 claim that "the literature points to reform rather than abolition" overstates consensus given that the paper's own cited 2014 Spanish source concluded the IP should be eliminated and OECD (2018) judged net wealth taxes more distortive and less equitable than capital income taxes.

### [CONFIRMED] Verified elasticities imply EUR 1-7bn behavioural erosion of the headline revenues; erosion is rate-dependent, so it is not ranking-neutral across the eight designs

**Claim.** The paper reports static "potential revenue" of EUR 15,456M (Sim. ### [CONFIRMED] The lit review's "moderate behavioural responses" framing (p.6) understates what its own three cited quasi-experimental studies report
*id `moderate-mischaracterization` · would change conclusions: medium · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** Section 3 characterizes the Switzerland/Denmark/Spain evidence as "moderate behavioural responses concentrated at the top, mainly via asset reclassification and avoidance rather than real disinvestment" that "do not fully offset the redistributive gains". The cited papers themselves report: at least 43% higher reported wealth per 1pp rate cut after 6 years (Brulhart et al., the largest credible estimate in this literature); 8-year taxable-wealth effects of 19% (moderately wealthy) and 31% (very wealthy) with simulated 30-year effects of 30% and 65% and a long-run elasticity of 1.15 at the top (Jakobsen et al.); and Spanish avoidance that cost 2.75 times the 2011 wealth-tax revenue over 2012-2015 (Mas-Montserrat et al.). "Moderate" is defensible only for real saving responses; for taxable/reported wealth — the base the paper's revenue numbers rest on — the cited magnitudes are large.

**Paper evidence.** "Evidence from Switzerland, Denmark and Spain shows that wealth taxes induce moderate behavioural responses concentrated at the top, mainly via asset reclassification and avoidance rather than real disinvestment (Brulhart et al., 2022; Jakobsen et al., 2020; Mas-Montserrat et al., 2025). Although these studies also find that elasticities are non-negligible among the richest, these responses do not fully offset the redistributive gains" (Sec. 3, p.6). Same framing on the slides ("Moderate responses concentrated at the top — mainly avoidance, not real disinvestment").

**External evidence.** Brulhart et al. (2022) AEJ:EP 14(4): "raises reported taxable wealth by at least 43 percent after 6 years"; decomposition: mobility 24%, housing 21%, real savings small, remainder consistent with "sizable evasion responses" (https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257/pol.20200258). Jakobsen et al. (2020) QJE 135(1):329-388, from the article text (https://gabriel-zucman.eu/files/JJKZ2020.pdf): effect "equal to about 19% after eight years for the moderately wealthy (couples DD) and 31% after eight years for" the very wealthy; 30-year simulated effects 30% and 65%; "implied long-run elasticity of taxable wealth with respect to the after-tax rate of return equals 0.77 for the moderately wealthy and 1.15 for the very [wealthy]". Mas-Montserrat et al. (2025) JPubE 246:105351: avoidance strategies "represent a 2012-2015 revenue loss of 2.75 times the 2011 estimated wealth tax revenues" (https://ideas.repec.org/a/eee/pubeco/v246y2025ics0047272725000490.html).

**Suggested question.** Brulhart et al.'s headline is a 43% reported-wealth response per percentage point and Jakobsen et al.'s long-run elasticity for the very wealthy is 1.15 — when Section 3 calls these "moderate", is that a claim about real saving responses only? If so, shouldn't the revenue and progressivity results, which depend on reported taxable wealth rather than true wealth, carry the large-elasticity caveat rather than the moderate one?

- **internal verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** The paper-side premise is verified verbatim: the Sec. 3 sentence (p.6) reads exactly as quoted, including 'moderate behavioural responses concentrated at the top, mainly via asset reclassification and avoidance rather than real disinvestment' and 'do not fully offset the redistributive gains'; the slide bullet ('Moderate responses concentrated at the top — mainly avoidance, not real disinvestment') is also exact. The crux — that Brulhart/Jakobsen/Mas-Montserrat report large reported-wealth magnitudes (43%/1pp, 30-65% at 30y, 2.75x revenue) — is external evidence I cannot check under this internal-only stance, so the mischaracterization verdict is not decidable from the paper alone. Two internal signals cut in the finding's favor: the paper cites the same Brulhart et al. (2022) on p.3 as the source for the retreat of wealth taxes being 'linked to administrative complexity, valuation problems, mobility concerns and low fiscal yield', in tension with the p.6 'moderate' framing; and the paper's revenue results do run entirely off reported/declared wealth with zero behavioural adjustment (p.34). One internal signal cuts against: the same p.6 sentence concedes 'elasticities are non-negligible among the richest', a hedge the authors can lean on live.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** The paper's Sec. 3 sentence (p.6) and the slide bullet are quoted exactly as they appear (paper.txt lines for Sec. 3; slide 'Moderate responses concentrated at the top — mainly avoidance, not real disinvestment'). All three cited magnitudes verified at primary sources: Brulhart's 'at least 43 percent after 6 years' with ~24% mobility / ~21% housing decomposition (AEA page); Jakobsen's figures verified from the actual QJE PDF text I extracted: '19% after eight years for the moderately wealthy (couples DD) and 31% after eight years for [the very wealthy]', 'a 30-year effect of 30%... the longrun effect is considerably larger, 65% after 30 years', and 'implied long-run elasticity of taxable wealth with respect to the after-tax rate of return equals 0.77... and 1.15'; Mas-Montserrat's 'revenue loss of 2.75 times the 2011 estimated wealth tax revenues' (abstract). The Mas-Montserrat abstract also states 'facing higher wealth taxes did not discourage savings', which is exactly the finder's anticipated author defense — 'moderate' is defensible for real saving but not for the reported-taxable-wealth base the revenue numbers rest on. The finding's distinction is sound and fully evidenced.
- **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** The Sec. 3 (p.6) sentence is quoted verbatim in the finding and the slides repeat the 'Moderate responses' framing. As written, 'moderate' modifies the total behavioural response — which the same sentence says is 'mainly via asset reclassification and avoidance' — yet the cited papers' headline magnitudes for exactly that reported/taxable-wealth margin are large: Brülhart et al.'s ≥43% per 1pp (which the authors of that paper themselves call large), Jakobsen et al.'s 31% at 8 years and 1.15 long-run elasticity for the very wealthy, and Mas-Montserrat et al.'s 2.75x revenue loss (I re-fetched the JPubE abstract confirming the 2.75x figure). The authors' best defense — 'moderate' means real saving/investment responses, and the sentence's own hedge acknowledges non-negligible elasticities — is a partial framing answer that requires rewriting the sentence to hold, and it concedes the operative point: the paper's revenue and measured-inequality results run off reported taxable wealth, the base for which the cited elasticities are large, not moderate. The finding survives.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** In our reading 'moderate' characterizes the real economic margin — all three papers find savings and real investment respond little, which is precisely why our sentence says the responses run 'mainly via asset reclassification and avoidance rather than real disinvestment', and the next clause already concedes that elasticities are non-negligible among the richest. We would add that avoidance elasticities are policy-contingent rather than structural: Mas-Montserrat et al. trace most of the Spanish response to specific design features — the liability cap above all — so a harmonized, broader-base design of the kind we simulate should face smaller reported-wealth responses than those headline numbers. We concede, however, that our revenue and measured-inequality results are computed on reported taxable wealth, for which the cited magnitudes — at least 43% per point in Switzerland, a 1.15 long-run elasticity in Denmark, a 2.75-times revenue loss in Spain — are large. We will reword the sentence to separate real from reported responses and attach the large-elasticity caveat where it belongs, on the revenue results.


### [CONFIRMED] The most relevant paper for Sims 2-3 — Agrawal, Foremny & Martinez-Toledano (AEJ: Applied 2025) on Madrid's zero rate — is not cited, and it would actually help the authors
*id `agrawal-omission` · would change conclusions: medium · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** The paper's central reform scenarios remove regional rebates (Sim. 2: EUR 2,405M; Sim. 3: EUR 12,673M), and Sec. 2 discusses Madrid's 100% rebate and "fiscal competition" — yet it never cites the definitive quasi-experimental study of exactly this margin. Agrawal, Foremny & Martinez-Toledano, "Wealth Tax Mobility and Tax Coordination" (AEJ: Applied Economics 17(1):402-430, January 2025), exploit the 2011 reintroduction in which "all regions except Madrid levied positive tax rates" and find mobility responses to wealth taxes comparable to income-tax mobility estimates, personal-income-tax revenue losses six times larger than the direct wealth-tax losses, and that "Madrid could achieve higher total regional revenues by agreeing to a harmonized positive tax rate." This cuts both ways: it disciplines the static Sim. 2/3 revenue gains (the 2022 cross-section already embeds a decade of wealth relocation into Madrid, and harmonization could trigger international rather than internal relocation), but it also hands the authors their strongest evidence-based defense of harmonization — coordination eliminates the internal mobility margin and raises total revenue including income-tax spillovers the paper never counts.

**Paper evidence.** "Some regions such as Madrid and Andalusia have applied a 100% rebate... resulting in significant horizontal inequities and fiscal competition (Duran-Cabre & Esteller-More, 2014, 2021; Esteller-More, 2022; Gali & Capella, 2024)" (p.4-5). Sim. 2/3 definitions p.10 and Table 1, p.11; revenues Table 3, p.21. References pp.35-37 contain no Agrawal, Foremny or Martinez-Toledano entry (verified by full-text search of paper.txt and slides.txt).

**External evidence.** Agrawal, Foremny & Martinez-Toledano (2025), AEJ: Applied Economics 17(1):402-430, abstract verbatim: "We exploit the reintroduction of the Spanish wealth tax, after which all regions except Madrid levied positive tax rates. We find the mobility responses to wealth taxes are within the range of prior estimates with respect to income taxes. However, wealth tax mobility responses generate losses to personal income tax revenues that are six times larger than the direct losses to wealth taxes. Madrid could achieve higher total regional revenues by agreeing to a harmonized positive tax rate." (https://ideas.repec.org/a/aea/aejapp/v17y2025i1p402-30.html)

**Suggested question.** Agrawal, Foremny and Martinez-Toledano show in AEJ: Applied last year that wealth-tax mobility within Spain generates income-tax losses six times the direct wealth-tax losses, and that Madrid itself would gain from a harmonized positive rate. Your Sim. 2 and Sim. 3 are precisely that harmonization experiment — why not cite this result, both to discipline the static revenue numbers and to strengthen the coordination case with the income-tax spillover you currently leave on the table?

- **internal verifier — CONFIRMED.** The omission is internally decidable and verified: grep of paper.txt and slides.txt returns zero hits for 'Agrawal', 'Foremny', and 'Toledano', and a full read of the reference list (pp.35-37) confirms no such entry (the only Martinez entries are Martinez, Diaz & Marin 2022 and Martinez F.A. 2017). All relevance premises hold: the p.4-5 quote on Madrid/Andalusia 100% rebates and 'fiscal competition' is verbatim; Sim. 2 is defined as applying the tax 'uniformly across all regions' (p.10) and Table A.2 (p.40) labels it 'Harmonised across regions', so Sims 2-3 are precisely the harmonization experiment; revenues 2,405.17 and 12,673.18 MEUR match Table 3 (p.21). The timing premise is internally consistent: the baseline is the 2022 cross-section (p.5, Table 1) with regional full rebates permitted since 2011 (p.4), so a decade of location choices under Madrid's zero rate is embedded in the data by the paper's own institutional account. The content attributed to AFMT (2025) — mobility magnitudes, 6x income-tax spillover, Madrid's gain from harmonization — is external and was not verifiable under this stance.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** The Agrawal-Foremny-Martinez-Toledano abstract is verified verbatim (AEJ: Applied Economics 17(1):402-430, January 2025): 'all regions except Madrid levied positive tax rates... mobility responses to wealth taxes are within the range of prior estimates with respect to income taxes... losses to personal income tax revenues that are six times larger than the direct losses to wealth taxes. Madrid could achieve higher total regional revenues by agreeing to a harmonized positive tax rate.' I grepped both paper.txt and slides.txt for agrawal/foremny/martinez-toledano: zero hits, confirming the omission from the references (pp.35-37) and slides. The paper's Madrid/Andalusia 100%-rebate and 'fiscal competition' passage is verified at pp.4-5, and Sim. 2/3 (harmonization) revenues at Table 3, p.21. The dual-edged reading — the 2022 cross-section embeds post-2011 relocation into Madrid, while the six-times income-tax spillover strengthens the harmonization case — follows directly from the verified abstract and is the single most on-point missing citation for this paper.
- **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** I grep-verified that 'Agrawal', 'Foremny' and 'Martínez-Toledano' appear nowhere in paper.txt or slides.txt, and the p.4-5 passage on Madrid's 100% rebate and 'fiscal competition' is quoted accurately with its citation list (Durán-Cabré & Esteller-Moré 2014/2021; Esteller-Moré 2022; Galí & Capella 2024). I independently fetched the AFMT record (AEJ: Applied Economics 17(1):402-430, January 2025) and confirmed the abstract's content: Madrid the only zero-rate region after the 2011 reintroduction, mobility responses within the range of income-tax estimates, PIT revenue losses about six times the direct wealth-tax losses, and Madrid better off under a harmonized positive rate. This is the definitive quasi-experimental study of exactly the margin Sims 2-3 close (removing regional rebates = harmonization), published well before the April 2026 working paper, and — as the finding fairly notes — it strengthens the authors' own conclusion while disciplining the static numbers. The authors have no substantive rebuttal to the omission itself; their response is acceptance plus context, so the finding fully survives.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** We thank the discussant — this is a reference we should have had, and candidly one that helps us: Agrawal, Foremny and Martínez-Toledano study exactly the Madrid margin our Simulations 2 and 3 close, and their finding that Madrid itself would collect more under a harmonized positive rate, with income-tax spillovers six times the direct wealth-tax losses, strengthens the coordination case through a channel we do not even count. Two clarifications from our side: our static cross-section cannot model relocation by construction, which page 34 states, and harmonization eliminates the internal mobility margin they identify, leaving only international mobility, which the European evidence suggests is considerably smaller. To the extent a decade of relocation into Madrid is embedded in our 2022 data, that wealth is still inside the national base, so our Spain-wide revenue is not mechanically inflated by it. We will add the reference, use it to discipline the Sim 2/3 numbers, and report the income-tax spillover as an additional gain from harmonization.


### [CONFIRMED] Seim (Sweden), Londono-Velez & Avila-Mahecha (Colombia), the Advani-Tarrant elasticity survey, and the Saez-Zucman vs Summers-Sarin revenue debate are all absent
*id `missing-benchmark-literature` · would change conclusions: low · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** Four verified strands that frame any wealth-tax revenue claim are uncited. Seim (2017) provides the low-end anchor (elasticities 0.09-0.27, mostly underreporting) — it would actually support the authors' "moderate" framing. Londono-Velez & Avila-Mahecha (2021) provide the enforcement warning: two-fifths of Colombia's wealthiest 0.01% evaded, hiding a third of their wealth offshore — relevant because the paper's narrow-base designs (Sim. 2, FR) collect almost everything from the extreme top (Fig. 2, p.17). Advani & Tarrant (2021) — the companion survey to the Advani, Hughson & Tarrant paper the authors do cite — documents that taxable-wealth elasticity estimates "vary by a factor of 800" and reconciles them to the 7-17%-per-1pp design-based range; notably, the cited AHT revenue estimates for the UK build in this behavioural erosion, whereas this paper's revenue numbers build in none. And the paper cites Saez & Zucman (2019 BPEA) for the claim that a top-0.1% wealth tax works "under credible enforcement" without noting that S-Z themselves model 15% (strong-enforcement) vs 50% (weak-enforcement) base erosion, nor the Summers-Sarin critique that, using estate-tax evidence, put Warren-tax revenue at roughly one-eighth of the S-Z estimate ($25bn vs $212bn for 2019).

**Paper evidence.** Lit review pp.6-7 and references pp.35-37: no Seim, no Londono-Velez/Avila-Mahecha, no Summers/Sarin, no Advani & Tarrant (2021) survey (verified by full-text search; only Advani, Hughson & Tarrant 2021 appears, p.35). "Saez & Zucman (2019) show that a progressive wealth tax on the top 0.1% could reduce wealth concentration without large efficiency losses under credible enforcement" (p.6). "Advani et al. (2021) estimate that low-rate wealth taxes in the United Kingdom (UK) could raise large revenues even under liquidity constraints" (p.6). Fig. 2 concentration of payments within the top 1%, p.17.

**External evidence.** Seim (2017) AEJ:EP 9(4):395-421, abstract verbatim: "net-of-tax-rate elasticities of taxable wealth in the range [0.09, 0.27]... responses reflect evasion and avoidance rather than changes in saving" (https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257/pol.20150290). Londono-Velez & Avila-Mahecha (2021) AER: Insights 3(2):131-148: "two-fifths of the wealthiest 0.01 percent evade taxes, with these evaders concealing one-third of their wealth offshore" (https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257/aeri.20200319). Advani & Tarrant (2021) Fiscal Studies 42(3-4):509-537: "estimates of the elasticity of taxable wealth vary by a factor of 800... a well-designed wealth tax would reduce the tax base by 7-17 per cent if levied at a tax rate of 1 per cent" (https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1111/1475-5890.12283). Advani, Hughson & Tarrant (2021) Fiscal Studies 42(3-4):699-736: annual 0.17% above GBP 500k raises ~GBP 10bn incorporating 7-17% behavioural erosion (per abstract seen in search results; https://ideas.repec.org/a/wly/fistud/v42y2021i3-4p699-736.html). Saez & Zucman (2019) BPEA conference draft (https://www.brookings.edu/wp-content/uploads/2019/09/Saez-Zucman_conference-draft.pdf): "assume that the wealthy would hide 15% of their wealth"; table: "Strong enforcement: 15% evasion rate" / "Weak enforcement: 50% evasion rate" with base reductions ~13% / ~44%. Saez-Zucman response to Summers-Sarin, June 25 2019 (https://gabriel-zucman.eu/files/saez-zucman-responseto-summers-sarin.pdf): "would raise $212 billion in 2019"; "Summers and Sarin claim that our revenue estimates are exaggerated by a factor of 8"; S-S "$25 billion" implies "80%-90%" sheltering. Summers & Sarin op-eds, Washington Post, Apr 4 2019 (https://www.washingtonpost.com/opinions/2019/04/04/wealth-tax-presents-revenue-estimation-puzzle/) and Jun 28 2019 (https://www.washingtonpost.com/opinions/2019/06/28/be-very-skeptical-about-how-much-revenue-elizabeth-warrens-wealth-tax-could-generate/) — located via search; contents corroborated by the fetched S-Z response.

**Arithmetic.** Context for the debate's range applied to this paper: S-Z strong enforcement (15% erosion) on Sim. NO: 15,456x0.85=13,138 MEUR; S-Z weak enforcement (50%): 15,456x0.50=7,728 MEUR. The paper's implicit assumption is 0% erosion — outside even the optimistic end of the published debate.

**Suggested question.** Your cited UK comparator (Advani-Hughson-Tarrant) nets behavioural erosion of 7-17% per point out of its revenue numbers, and even Saez-Zucman — your optimistic benchmark — assume 15% erosion under strong enforcement and 50% under weak. What enforcement regime would Spain need for your zero-erosion numbers to be the right central case, given Londono-Velez's Colombian evidence that 40% of the top 0.01% evaded?

### [CONFIRMED] The 25-100 year projections compound the zero-response assumption exactly where the cited Danish evidence says responses grow largest, and the framework's source is cited with the wrong working-paper number
*id `longrun-compounding` · would change conclusions: medium · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** The long-run exercise applies each design's short-run tax rate mechanically for up to a century: under Piketty the cumulative long-run effective rate reaches 21.91% of wealth at T=100 (Table 5, p.26), and the headline is that broad designs cut the top-1% share by more than 7pp. But Jakobsen et al. — cited by the paper — show behavioural responses to wealth taxes build over time (19%/31% at 8 years growing to 30%/65% at 30 years in their simulations, elasticity 1.15 at the top), precisely the horizon over which the paper's redistribution accumulates; and Blanchet (2022), cited in the paper's own footnote 11, shows wealth mobility across the distribution is crucial to sustaining the tax base under repeated taxation. So the long-run Gini and top-share paths are upper bounds on redistribution, which the text partially concedes but the slides present without caveat. Separately, the framework's source is miscited: "Duran-Cabre and Esteller-More (2019). The long-run redistributive power of the net wealth tax. IEB Working Paper, (2021/04)" — the actual working paper is IEB 2019/09 (2021/04 is not this paper). The specific numbers the paper quotes from it (0.05% short-run becoming 0.83% after 25 years and 2.49% after 85 years, p.15) do check out against the WP text.

**Paper evidence.** Long-run method pp.15, 25-26; "This exercise is not intended to predict the future evolution of wealth inequality" (p.25); Table 5, p.26: Piketty t_l/r = 6.76% (T=25), 12.55% (T=50), 17.55% (T=75), 21.91% (T=100); Sim. NO 22.28% at T=100. Limitation 3, p.34: "a simplified accumulation framework that assumes a uniform and constant annual wealth growth rate, abstracting from the heterogeneous returns, savings behaviour, and wealth mobility documented in the structural literature". Footnote 11, p.25 (Blanchet 2022: mobility "plays a crucial role in sustaining the tax base under repeated annual taxation"). Reference p.35: "IEB Working Paper, (2021/04)". Slides: "Norwegian and Piketty's schedule reduce the top 1% share by >7 pp at T = 100 (from ~15% to <8%)" with no behavioural caveat. Quote checked: "an annual ts/r of only 0.05% results in a long-run burden of 0.83% after 25 years and 2.49% after 85 years" (p.15).

**External evidence.** Jakobsen et al. (2020) QJE 135(1):329-388: 8-year effects 19%/31%, simulated 30-year effects 30%/65%, long-run elasticities 0.77/1.15 (https://gabriel-zucman.eu/files/JJKZ2020.pdf). Duran-Cabre & Esteller-More, "The long-run redistributive power of the net wealth tax", IEB Working Paper 2019/09 (https://ieb.ub.edu/en/publication/2019-09-the-long-run-redistributive-power-of-the-net-wealth-tax/; also SSRN 3685815); the 0.83%/25y and 2.49%/85y figures verified in the WP text (https://www.lse.ac.uk/International-Inequalities/Assets/Documents/Alejandro-Esteller-Mor%C3%A9-Paper.pdf: "0.83% (5.70% for those with a positive tax due) after 25 years and to 2.49% (17.04%) after a very long period of time, in this case 85 years").

**Arithmetic.** Cumulative confiscation check: Piketty t_l/r at T=100 is 21.91% of accumulated wealth (Table 5). Jakobsen's very-wealthy long-run elasticity of 1.15 with respect to the net-of-tax return implies substantial accumulation responses to far smaller net-return changes (Denmark's reform cut a 2.2% marginal rate to 1%); assuming literally zero response to a century-long levy that removes a fifth of top wealth is outside anything in the cited evidence. Direction of bias on the top-1% share path: overstated decline (avoidance and slower accumulation shrink both the tax paid and measured concentration in ways the framework cannot separate).

**Suggested question.** Your long-run projections are the paper's most striking numbers — top-1% share halving in a century — but they hold behaviour fixed for 100 years while your cited Danish evidence shows responses growing from 31% at 8 years to 65% at 30 years for exactly this population. Would you consider presenting the long-run paths explicitly as no-response upper bounds, or better, re-running them with the Jakobsen elasticity as a sensitivity? And to confirm: the framework source is IEB WP 2019/09, not 2021/04 as the reference list says?

NO 22.28% at T=100; the p.25 'not intended to predict' sentence, the p.34 limitation-3 quote, and the p.15 DCE-M quote (0.05% -> 0.83% after 25y, 2.49% after 85y) are all verbatim. The reference '(2021/04)' appears in both the paper (p.35) and the slides' references, and is internally anomalous on its face — a 2019-dated paper carrying a 2021-series number — consistent with the miscite (the true number 2019/09 is external, verified by the finder). The slides' long-run material indeed carries no behavioural caveat (only 'Robustness: similar results for g in {1%,3%}'). The 0.83%/2.49% figures the paper quotes (p.15) are verified in the DCE-M working-paper text: '0.83% (5.70% for those with a positive tax due) after 25 years and to 2.49% (17.04%) after a very long period of time, in this case 85 years'. Jakobsen's growing-over-time responses (19%/31% at 8y, 30%/65% at 30y, elasticity 1.15) and the 2.2%-to-1% Danish rate cut are verified from the QJE PDF text.
