NO 22.28% at T=100; the p.25 disclaimer and p.34 limitation 3 read as quoted; and the slides state '>7 pp at T = 100 (from ~15% to <8%)' with no behavioural caveat. I independently confirmed the miscite: the IEB publication page (fetched) lists the paper as Working Paper 2019/09, while the reference list (p.35) and the slides both print '(2021/04)'. The authors' best response — the exercise is explicitly illustrative, the limitation is listed, dynamics are future work — is real but already priced into the finding, and it does not neutralize the core point: the paper cites Jakobsen et al., whose central result is that responses to wealth taxes grow with horizon (31% at 8y → 65% at 30y simulated, elasticity 1.15 at the top), yet never connects that evidence to the century-long zero-response projections that produce its most striking numbers, and the slides present those numbers as design properties. Their fallback (avoidance shrinks reported not real wealth) concedes that measured long-run inequality declines cannot be read as real either. One extra wrinkle I verified: the slide's '>7pp' for NO and Piketty does not even match the paper's own Table A.7 (equivalized, g=2%: 14.67% → 9.13% and 7.96%, i.e. −5.5pp and −6.7pp; only Sim. 3 at −8.1pp exceeds 7pp, and NO ends above 8%).

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** We flag exactly this in the paper: page 25 states the exercise 'is not intended to predict the future evolution of wealth inequality' but to illustrate the mechanical compounding of a stock tax, and limitation 3 on page 34 names the constant-growth abstraction from heterogeneous returns, savings behaviour and mobility, with behavioural elasticities listed as future work. The framework is Durán-Cabré and Esteller-Moré's, chosen for transparency — a structural model with Danish-style accumulation responses is a different paper. We concede the presentation point: the slides carry the >7pp headline without that caveat, and we will relabel the long-run paths explicitly as no-behavioural-response upper bounds; re-running them with the Jakobsen top-wealth elasticity as a sensitivity is feasible and worth doing. And yes, the reference should read IEB Working Paper 2019/09 rather than 2021/04 — a typo we will fix, and we thank you for checking the underlying numbers, which do hold.


### [CONFIRMED] The "reform" simulations leave intact the two state-level features — the family-business exemption and the IRPF-IP cap — that the paper's own sources identify as the dominant avoidance and regressivity channels
*id `retained-avoidance-channels` · would change conclusions: medium · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** Sims 1-3 modify only the general threshold and regional deductions; the state-level family-business exemption and the joint IRPF-IP 60% liability cap remain in place (the cap is removed only from the foreign-design simulations, p.10). Yet the paper itself reports that "current design features such as the family business exemption and the wealth-income cap are regressive" (p.7, citing Duran-Cabre & Esteller-More 2019) and that removing business-asset exemptions plus harmonization "would markedly raise both revenue and redistribution" (p.6, citing their 2021 CESifo paper) — and Mas-Montserrat et al. (2025) show the cap is the single largest avoidance channel, accounting for 92.6% of the revenue impact of avoidance after the 2011 reintroduction. Consequence: Sim. 3 multiplies the number of taxpayers exposed to this machinery by 27 (170,404 to 4.63M, Table 3) while keeping the escape hatches open, so its static EUR 12,673M is vulnerable on a margin the paper's own citations quantify; and the reform menu omits the one reform its cited Spanish literature says matters most. The paper's stated reason (PH records business assets only for wealth-tax filers, p.8) explains why non-filer coverage is hard, but a filer-level bound was feasible as in Duran-Cabre & Esteller-More (2021).

**Paper evidence.** Sim definitions: Table 1, p.11 and pp.10; Table A.2, p.40 (Sim. 1/2/3 rows change only threshold and regional rules; baseline note "Joint IRPF-IP liability cap applies"). "For comparability, the simulations of international models do not incorporate Spain's joint IRPF-IP tax-liability limit" (p.10) — implying Spanish sims retain it. "Simulation results confirm that current design features such as the family business exemption and the wealth-income cap are regressive" (p.7). "Duran-Cabre & Esteller-More (2021) show that removing business-asset exemptions and harmonizing thresholds would markedly raise both revenue and redistribution" (p.6). Data gap: "only those who file the Wealth Tax have their business assets from self-employment recorded in the PH" (p.8); limitation 1, p.34. Taxpayer counts: Table 3, p.21 (IP 170,404; Sim. 3 4,626,929).

**External evidence.** Mas-Montserrat et al. (2025) JPubE 246:105351, abstract verbatim: "Taxpayers also take advantage of the limit on tax liability through a change in their asset and income composition. By far, this latter source of avoidance accounts for the greatest impact on tax revenues (92.6%)" (https://ideas.repec.org/a/eee/pubeco/v246y2025ics0047272725000490.html). Duran-Cabre & Esteller-More (2021), CESifo Economic Studies 67(4):488-510, "A quantitative assessment of the net wealth tax: The Spanish experience": "the family business exemption and the common ceilings are highly regressive", evaluates reforms with broader bases (https://ideas.repec.org/a/oup/cesifo/v67y2021i4p488-510..html).

**Arithmetic.** Taxpayer exposure: 4,626,929 / 170,404 = 27.2x more effective taxpayers under Sim. 3 than baseline (Table 3, p.21), all facing an unchanged cap-and-exemption structure that Mas-Montserrat et al. show accounted for 92.6% of avoidance-driven revenue loss after 2011.

**Suggested question.** Your own Section 3 says the business exemption and the income-wealth cap are the regressive features, and Mas-Montserrat et al. show the cap alone drives 92.6% of avoidance revenue loss — yet Sims 1-3 keep both and instead move the threshold and regional rules. Was this a data constraint from the PH's business-asset coverage, and could you at least bound the filer-level effect of removing the exemption and cap, as Duran-Cabre and Esteller-More did with SIMPA?

- **internal verifier — CONFIRMED.** All quotes are verbatim: the p.7 sentence calling 'the family business exemption and the wealth-income cap' regressive (citing DCE-M 2019); the p.6 DCE-M 2021 sentence on removing business-asset exemptions; the p.8 data-gap sentence; the p.10 sentence dropping the joint IRPF-IP limit only from 'the simulations of international models'; the 60% cap and 20% minimum (p.4); and limitation 1 (p.34). The retention premise for Sims 1-3 is an inference but a strong one: Table 1 (p.11) and Table A.2 (p.40) define Sims 1-3 as changing only the threshold and regional rules ('same schedule as IP'), the cap-removal is justified specifically and only for international models, and the cap is a state-level rule that Sim. 2's removal of 'regional deductions and allowances' does not touch — though the authors never state cap retention explicitly (Table A.2's Notes flag the cap only in the baseline row), which is itself a fair documentation question. Counts and arithmetic exact: 4,626,929/170,404 = 27.15 (Table 3, p.21). Two external dependencies flagged: the MM 92.6% cap-avoidance share and the feasibility of a DCE-M-2021-style filer-level bound (SIMPA is never mentioned in this paper). One nuance: '27x more taxpayers facing the escape hatches' is literally true of the structure, but the cap channel binds mainly for high-wealth/low-income filers, so the at-risk share of the 12,673M is concentrated at the top rather than spread over all 4.6M new taxpayers.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** Both external quotes verified verbatim: the Mas-Montserrat abstract states 'Taxpayers also take advantage of the limit on tax liability through a change in their asset and income composition. By far, this latter source of avoidance accounts for the greatest impact on tax revenues (92.6%)', and the DCE-M 2021 CESifo abstract states 'the family business exemption and the common ceilings are highly regressive' and describes 'a tax simulator we have developed' assessing 'alternative reforms with more comprehensive tax bases'; the companion working-paper text names the simulator SIMPA, so the filer-level-bound suggestion is grounded. Paper-side verified: p.10 exempts only the international models from the cap ('the simulations of international models do not incorporate Spain's joint IRPF–IP tax-liability limit'), Table A.2's baseline notes 'Joint IRPF–IP liability cap applies' and the Sim. 1-3 rows change only threshold/regional rules; the p.7 regressive-features sentence and the p.8 business-asset data gap are verbatim; 4,626,929/170,404 = 27.15x recomputed. One small ambiguity the authors could exploit: p.10's wording leaves unstated whether the Piketty simulation (a normative proposal, not a foreign legislation) retains the cap — the finding's core about Sims 1-3 is unaffected.
- **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** The textual basis all verifies: p.10 exempts only the international simulations from the joint IRPF-IP cap ('For comparability, the simulations of international models do not incorporate Spain's joint IRPF-IP tax-liability limit'), Table A.2 (p.40) confirms Sims 1-3 change only threshold and regional rules with the baseline note 'Joint IRPF-IP liability cap applies', and the paper's own Section 3 states both that 'the family business exemption and the wealth-income cap are regressive' (p.7) and that removing business-asset exemptions 'would markedly raise both revenue and redistribution' (p.6). I recomputed 4,626,929/170,404 = 27.2 and re-fetched the Mas-Montserrat abstract confirming the cap channel 'accounts for the greatest impact on tax revenues (92.6%)'. The authors have genuine partial defenses — the cap is a legally load-bearing anti-confiscation rule, the PH records business assets only for wealth-tax filers (p.8, limitation 1), and Sim. NO already shows a cap-free broad base — but none rebuts the substance: the reform menu omits the two features their own cited Spanish literature identifies as most decisive, a filer-level bound (as DCE-M did with SIMPA) was feasible, and Sim. 3's static revenue is exposed at precisely the avoidance margin their own citation quantifies at 92.6%. The finding survives with the scope/data context as mitigation, not refutation.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** This was partly deliberate scope and partly data. Simulations 1–3 isolate the two parameters at the centre of the Spanish political debate — the exemption threshold and the regional rebates — while the joint IRPF–IP cap is a legally load-bearing protection against confiscatory taxation, so deleting it is a different and contentious counterfactual; note that our international simulations already drop the cap, and Sim. NO shows what a cap-free broad base delivers. On the family-business exemption, the Household Panel records business assets only for wealth-tax filers, as we state on page 8, so removing the exemption for the millions of new taxpayers in Sim. 3 is not identifiable in our data. We accept the substantive point: our own review flags both features as regressive, Mas-Montserrat and our colleagues show the cap is the dominant avoidance channel, and a filer-level bound of removing the cap and the exemption — in the spirit of Durán-Cabré and Esteller-Moré's SIMPA exercise — is feasible and would be a valuable addition. We also accept that Sim. 3's static revenue is exposed where taxpayers can re-optimize their asset and income composition against the cap.


### [PLAUSIBLE] "The literature points to reform rather than abolition" (p.7) overstates consensus — the paper's own cited 2014 Spanish source concluded the IP should be eliminated, and OECD (2018) recommends capital-income taxation instead
*id `reform-vs-abolition-framing` · would change conclusions: low · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** The lit review closes with "Overall, the literature points to reform rather than abolition, aligning wealth-tax design with broader objectives of fairness and coherence." But Duran-Cabre & Esteller-More (2014) — cited three times in the paper for institutional background — states in its conclusions "El impuesto sobre el patrimonio debe eliminarse" (the wealth tax should be eliminated), recommending instead a reinforced inheritance tax and real-estate taxation; and OECD (2018), also cited, concludes net wealth taxes "tend to be more distortive and less equitable than personal capital income taxes" and notes repeals were justified by efficiency, administrative and redistributive failures. The honest placement is that the literature is split between harmonize-and-broaden (DCE-M 2019/2021, the 2022 Libro Blanco co-authored by Canto, Saez-Zucman) and replace-with-better-instruments (DCE-M 2014, OECD 2018, Bastani-Waldenstrom's wealth-vs-capital-income framing) — and the paper's design comparison is actually well positioned to inform that split rather than assume it away.

**Paper evidence.** "Overall, the literature points to reform rather than abolition, aligning wealth-tax design with broader objectives of fairness and coherence" (Sec. 3, p.7). The paper cites Duran-Cabre & Esteller-More (2014) at pp.3, 5, 7 (institutional history, regional disparities, Spanish empirical conclusions) and OECD (2018) at pp.5, 6. Reference list p.35-36.

**External evidence.** Duran-Cabre & Esteller-More (2014), "La imposicion sobre la riqueza en Espana", Papeles de Economia Espanola 139:70-85, conclusions section verbatim: "El impuesto sobre el patrimonio debe eliminarse por la imposibilidad de solventar los problemas..." and earlier "reafirmar (con importantes cambios) el ISD en nuestro sistema fiscal y eliminar definitivamente el IP" (https://www.funcas.es/wp-content/uploads/Migracion/Articulos/FUNCAS_PEE/139art06.pdf, fetched PDF). OECD (2018), The Role and Design of Net Wealth Taxes in the OECD: net wealth taxes "tend to be more distortive and less equitable than personal capital income taxes, largely because they are imposed irrespective of the actual returns" (conclusions text seen in oecd.org search results; report page https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/the-role-and-design-of-net-wealth-taxes-in-the-oecd_9789264290303-en.html).

**Suggested question.** Your Section 3 says the literature points to reform rather than abolition — but the Duran-Cabre and Esteller-More piece you cite for the Spanish institutional record concluded in 2014 that the IP should be eliminated outright, and OECD 2018 favors capital-income taxation over net wealth taxes. Doesn't your own comparison — where the existing IP achieves essentially zero redistribution — inform that reform-vs-replace debate rather than settle it, and would you frame it that way?

- **internal verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** The paper-side premises verify: the closing sentence of Sec. 3 (p.7) is verbatim; DCE-M (2014) is cited exactly three times, at pp.3, 5 and 7, all for institutional/empirical background; both DCE-M 2014 and OECD 2018 are in the reference list (pp.35-36). One page-cite error: OECD (2018) is cited at pp.4 and 6 (the property-tax-statistics cite sits on printed p.4), not pp.5-6. The decisive premise — that DCE-M 2014 concluded the IP should be eliminated and OECD 2018 favors capital-income taxation — is external evidence I cannot check under this stance, so the 'overstates consensus' verdict is not decidable from the paper alone. Internal signals are consistent with a live split inside the paper's own reference list: it cites Boadway & Pestieau's 'The tenuous case for an annual wealth tax' and Bastani & Waldenstrom's wealth-vs-capital-income-choice paper, and pp.3-4 recount the near-universal retreat of wealth taxes 'typically linked to administrative complexity, valuation problems, mobility concerns and low fiscal yield'. Also internally confirmed for the anticipated exchange: Canto is listed among the authors of Ruiz-Huerta et al. (2022, Libro Blanco) in the reference list (p.37), and the paper reports IP net-wealth Gini reduction of exactly 0.0000% (Table 3), supporting the suggested question's 'essentially zero redistribution'.
  - *Corrected claim:* As written, except OECD (2018) is cited at pp.4 and 6, not pp.5-6; and the attribution of an explicit abolition conclusion to DCE-M (2014) rests on external evidence (the paper itself only cites it for institutional history and Spanish empirical conclusions).
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** I fetched the actual funcas PDF of Duran-Cabre & Esteller-More (2014, Papeles de Economia Espanola 139) and extracted its text: the conclusions state verbatim 'El impuesto sobre el patrimonio debe eliminarse por la imposibilidad de solventar los problemas derivados de la elevada elusion fiscal, un bajo cumplimiento fiscal y unas normas de valoracion...', and earlier 'nuestra propuesta va en la linea de reafirmar (con importantes cambios) el ISD en nuestro sistema fiscal y eliminar definitivamente el IP', plus strengthening the IBI — exactly the eliminate-IP, replace-with-ISD/IBI position claimed. The OECD 2018 report PDF states 'net wealth taxes tend to be more distortive and less equitable. This is largely because they are imposed irrespective of the actual returns that taxpayers earn on their assets' (the comparison to personal capital income taxes is set by the preceding sentence, so the finder's splice is faithful), plus 'limited arguments for having a net wealth tax in addition to broad-based personal capital income taxes' and repeal factors (efficiency costs/capital flight, failed redistributive goals, high admin costs). The paper's 'points to reform rather than abolition' sentence (p.7) and its three DCE-M 2014 citations (pp.3, 5, 7) are verified; one trivial slip in the finder's evidence: OECD 2018 is cited at printed pp.4 and 6, not pp.5-6.
- **advocate verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** The factual core is verified beyond dispute: the p.7 sentence is verbatim, DCE-M (2014) is cited on pp.3, 5 and 7, OECD (2018) on pp.5-6, and I extracted the funcas PDF myself — its abstract says 'se aboga por la eliminación definitiva del impuesto sobre el patrimonio' ('we propose the definite abolishment of the net wealth tax'), the text says 'eliminar definitivamente el IP', and a section heading reads 'El impuesto sobre el patrimonio debe eliminarse', recommending a reinforced ISD and real-estate taxation instead. OECD (2018)'s preference for capital-income taxation over net wealth taxes is that report's well-known conclusion. But the authors hold a genuinely strong partial answer that keeps this at framing rather than error: the Spanish debate demonstrably moved after 2014 — the same two economists' later work (2019, 2021) analyzes reform designs, and the 2022 Libro Blanco (Ruiz-Huerta et al., with Cantó on the committee, per the p.36 reference listing 'Cantó, O.') recommended harmonization — so 'points to reform' is defensible as a claim about the current centre of gravity even though, as written, it flattens a live reform-vs-replace split their own reference list documents. Partial survival: the sentence overstates consensus, but the fix is a one-sentence reframe, and the finder itself grades conclusion-impact low.
  - *Corrected claim:* The p.7 sentence overstates consensus as a description of the full cited literature — DCE-M (2014) argued for eliminating the IP and OECD (2018) prefers capital-income taxation — but it is defensible as a characterization of the post-2019 Spanish policy debate (DCE-M 2019/2021, Libro Blanco 2022), so the accurate charge is that the sentence erases a live reform-vs-replace split rather than that it misreports the field's direction.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** We would defend the sentence as a statement about where the debate now stands: since 2014 Durán-Cabré and Esteller-Moré themselves have produced the reform-oriented analyses we cite from 2019 and 2021, the 2022 Libro Blanco — on whose committee one of us served — recommended harmonizing the tax rather than abolishing it, and the recent quasi-experimental literature is about design margins, not abolition. We recognize, though, that the 2014 piece we cite for the institutional record concluded the IP should be definitively eliminated in favour of a reinforced inheritance tax and real-estate taxation, and that the OECD's 2018 report prefers well-designed capital-income taxation to net wealth taxes — so there is a live reform-versus-replace debate in our own reference list, not a settled consensus. We are comfortable reframing the sentence accordingly: our comparison informs that debate rather than presupposing its answer, and indeed our finding that the current IP achieves essentially zero redistribution is evidence both sides will want to use.


### What the verifiers flagged as missed by this lens
- **[internal]** Internal-consistency issues noticed while verifying, all usable by this lens: (1) The slides' long-run headline 'Norwegian and Piketty's schedule reduce the top 1% share by >7 pp at T=100 (from ~15% to <8%)' is contradicted by the paper's own tables: on the equivalized basis the slide figure uses (Table A.7, g=2%), Norway falls 14.67%->9.13% (-5.5pp, ending above 8%) and Piketty -6.7pp; only Sim. 3 (-8.1pp, to 6.58%) clears 8pp; on the individual basis (Table A.5) Norway is -6.9pp. (2) Paper text p.27-28 says broad systems bring the top 1% share 'to less than half its initial level after a century' — at g=2% only Sim. 3 achieves that (equivalized 6.58/14.67=44.9%; Piketty 54.3%, Norway 62.2%). (3) The paper cites Brulhart et al. (2022) on p.3 as the source for wealth-tax retreat being 'linked to administrative complexity, valuation problems, mobility concerns and low fiscal yield' — the same study framed as finding 'moderate' responses on p.6; a usable internal tension for the moderate-mischaracterization question. (4) Smaller internal errata a discussant can bundle: methodology p.9 says 'the benchmark is the legislation applicable to fiscal year 2023' while the baseline everywhere else is 2022 (p.5, fn.6, Table 1); the simulation count is variously 'seven simulations' (Table 1 caption, p.16), 'eight simulated scenarios' (abstract, slides), and 'nine simulated tax scenarios' (p.15); the cadastral-to-market ratio is 3.0838 in Eq. A.1 but 3.1125 in the bullet directly beneath it (p.39, slides say x3.08); and Table A.2 flags the IRPF-IP cap only in the baseline row, leaving cap treatment in Sims 1-3 and Piketty formally undocumented (the p.10 sentence removes it only for 'international models').
- **[external]** Two additional slide-vs-paper numerical inconsistencies in the same family as the '4-5 Gini points' error, both checkable live: (1) The slides claim 'Norwegian and Piketty's schedule reduce the top 1% share by >7 pp at T = 100 (from ~15% to <8%)', but the paper's own Figure 5 / Table A.7 (equivalized net wealth, g=2%) give declines of only 5.54pp for Norway (14.67% to 9.13%) and 6.71pp for Piketty (to 7.96%); only Sim. 3 exceeds 7pp (8.09pp, to 6.58%). Relatedly, the paper text (p.27) says these systems bring the top 1% share 'to less than half its initial level after a century' — true only of Sim. 3 (44.9% of initial); Piketty retains 54% and Norway 62%. (2) The trilemma slide compares 'Gini reduction: 0%' for the current IP against '1.3%' for the reformed design, but these are different bases: 0% is the IP's individual net wealth Gini reduction (Table 3: 0.0000%) while 1.3% is Sim. 3's equivalized disposable income Gini reduction (1.3231%); on a common disposable-income basis the comparison is 0.21% vs 1.32%, and on a common wealth basis 0.000% vs 0.066%.
- **[advocate]** Two items surfaced during verification. (1) The slides' long-run headline is inconsistent with the paper's own appendix: the slide says 'Norwegian and Piketty's schedule reduce the top 1% share by >7 pp at T = 100 (from ~15% to <8%)', but Table A.7 (equivalized net wealth, g=2%, p.46) shows the top-1% share going from 14.67% to 9.13% under Sim. NO (−5.5pp, ending above 8%) and to 7.96% under Piketty (−6.7pp); only Sim. 3 exceeds a 7pp decline (14.67% → 6.58%, −8.1pp). So the flagship long-run slide overstates NO and Piketty relative to the authors' own numbers, independent of any behavioural caveat — an easy, checkable point for the discussion. (2) Outside this lens but material: the appendix's cadastral-to-market adjustment (Eq. A.1, p.39) states R = 3.0838 in the equation and 'a value of 3.1125' in the explanatory bullet directly below it (slides say x3.08); since every wealth level, threshold crossing, and revenue figure runs through this single uniform national ratio, the internal inconsistency and the strong homogeneity assumption (no regional or price-segment variation in cadastral-to-market gaps, in tension with the housing-capitalization channel in their cited Brülhart et al.) deserve a question. Also trivia: p.9 says the benchmark is 'fiscal year 2023' legislation while footnote 6 and everything else says 2022.

### Sources this lens fetched or saw
- [Brulhart, Gruber, Krapf & Schmidheiny (2022), Behavioral Responses to Wealth Taxes: Evidence from Switzerland, AEJ: Economic Policy 14(4):111-150](https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257/pol.20200258) — A 1pp drop in a canton's wealth tax rate raises reported taxable wealth by at least 43% after 6 years; mobility accounts for 24% of the effect, housing 21%, real savings little — consistent with sizable evasion. Cited by the paper.
- [Jakobsen, Jakobsen, Kleven & Zucman (2020), Wealth Taxation and Wealth Accumulation: Theory and Evidence from Denmark, QJE 135(1):329-388](https://gabriel-zucman.eu/files/JJKZ2020.pdf) — 1989 Danish reform effects on taxable wealth: ~19% after 8 years (moderately wealthy) and ~31% (very wealthy); simulated 30-year effects 30% and 65%; implied long-run elasticities of taxable wealth wrt net-of-tax return 0.77 and 1.15. Cited by the paper.
- [Seim (2017), Behavioral Responses to Wealth Taxes: Evidence from Sweden, AEJ: Economic Policy 9(4):395-421](https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257/pol.20150290) — Net-of-tax-rate elasticities of taxable wealth of 0.09-0.27, about a third from underreporting; evasion/avoidance rather than saving. NOT cited by the paper — the low-end anchor.
- [Londono-Velez & Avila-Mahecha (2021), Enforcing Wealth Taxes in the Developing World, AER: Insights 3(2):131-148](https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257/aeri.20200319) — Two-fifths of Colombia's wealthiest 0.01% evade the wealth tax, concealing one-third of wealth offshore; enforcement strongly affects compliance and progressivity. NOT cited by the paper.
- [Mas-Montserrat, Duran-Cabre & Esteller-More (2025), Avoidance Responses to the Wealth Tax, Journal of Public Economics 246:105351](https://ideas.repec.org/a/eee/pubeco/v246y2025ics0047272725000490.html) — Verbatim abstract: 0.1pp higher average tax rate reduces taxable wealth 3.21% over 4 years; the tax-liability limit channel accounts for 92.6% of the revenue impact; 2012-2015 revenue losses equal 2.75x the 2011 estimated wealth-tax revenue. Cited by the paper (venue and article number match its reference).
- [Agrawal, Foremny & Martinez-Toledano (2025), Wealth Tax Mobility and Tax Coordination, AEJ: Applied Economics 17(1):402-430](https://ideas.repec.org/a/aea/aejapp/v17y2025i1p402-30.html) — Madrid's zero rate after 2011: mobility responses comparable to income-tax mobility estimates; wealth-tax mobility causes PIT revenue losses 6x direct wealth-tax losses; Madrid would gain from a harmonized positive rate. NOT cited by the paper despite Sims 2-3 being harmonization experiments.
- [Duran-Cabre & Esteller-More (2019), The Long-Run Redistributive Power of the Net Wealth Tax, IEB Working Paper 2019/09](https://ieb.ub.edu/en/publication/2019-09-the-long-run-redistributive-power-of-the-net-wealth-tax/) — The paper's long-run framework source. Correct WP number is 2019/09 — the paper's reference says (2021/04). The quoted figures (0.05% short-run rate -> 0.83% after 25y, 2.49% after 85y) verified in the WP text at https://www.lse.ac.uk/International-Inequalities/Assets/Documents/Alejandro-Esteller-Mor%C3%A9-Paper.pdf.
- [Duran-Cabre & Esteller-More (2021), A Quantitative Assessment of the Net Wealth Tax: The Spanish Experience, CESifo Economic Studies 67(4):488-510](https://ideas.repec.org/a/oup/cesifo/v67y2021i4p488-510..html) — Exists as cited; finds the family business exemption and the common (IRPF-IP) ceilings highly regressive and evaluates broader-base reforms via the SIMPA simulator.
- [Duran-Cabre & Esteller-More (2014), La imposicion sobre la riqueza en Espana, Papeles de Economia Espanola 139:70-85](https://www.funcas.es/wp-content/uploads/Migracion/Articulos/FUNCAS_PEE/139art06.pdf) — Exists as cited; its conclusions state verbatim 'El impuesto sobre el patrimonio debe eliminarse' — the source recommends eliminating the IP, contradicting the paper's 'literature points to reform rather than abolition' framing.
- [Saez & Zucman (2019), Progressive Wealth Taxation, Brookings Papers on Economic Activity Fall 2019](https://www.brookings.edu/wp-content/uploads/2019/09/Saez-Zucman_conference-draft.pdf) — Cited by the paper. Models base erosion explicitly: 'assume that the wealthy would hide 15% of their wealth'; scenarios 'Strong enforcement: 15% evasion rate' vs 'Weak enforcement: 50% evasion rate' (base reductions ~13% vs ~44%). Landing page: https://www.brookings.edu/articles/progressive-wealth-taxation/

NOT cited by the paper.

NOT cited by the paper (only the companion AHT modelling paper is).
- [Advani, Hughson & Tarrant (2021), Revenue and distributional modelling for a UK wealth tax, Fiscal Studies 42(3-4):699-736](https://ideas.repec.org/a/wly/fistud/v42y2021i3-4p699-736.html) — Cited by the paper. Its UK revenue estimates (e.g. annual 0.17% above GBP 500k raising ~GBP 10bn; one-off 4.8% raising ~GBP 250bn) already net out 7-17% behavioural erosion — unlike the paper's zero-erosion revenues. Details from abstract seen in search results (Wiley page returned 403).

Concludes net wealth taxes 'tend to be more distortive and less equitable than personal capital income taxes' and that repeals reflected efficiency, administrative and redistributive failures (conclusions text seen in oecd.org search results; direct fetch blocked).
- [Scheuer & Slemrod (2021), Taxing Our Wealth, Journal of Economic Perspectives 35(1):207-230](https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257/jep.35.1.207) — Exists as cited; abstract verbatim: studies 'sometimes, but not always, find a substantial behavioral response, including of saving, portfolio change, avoidance, and evasion, and the impact depends crucially on design features, especially the broadness of the base and enforcement provisions.'


## Lens: Framing: claims vs evidence

**Lens summary.** The paper's tables are honest; its abstract and slides are not always faithful to them. The body correctly concludes that redistributive gains come from higher incidence on a broader base ("level effect"), yet the abstract claims the alternative scenarios "increase tax progressivity" when the paper's own Kakwani/Suits indices FALL under every high-revenue design it champions (NO, Sim. 3, Piketty) and rise across the board only under Sim. 2 — the paper's actual, more interesting result is that progressivity and redistribution move in opposite directions across designs. The abstract's promises of "robust empirical evidence" and insight on "efficiency" are not earned: the only robustness exercise is a growth-rate sweep that is nearly vacuous under the stated accumulation method, and there is no efficiency analysis of any kind. The slides then amplify: "4–5 Gini points" (actually 4–5 percent, i.e. 1.6–1.8 points), a ">7 pp in 100 years" top-1% claim that fails for Norway on either wealth basis, a "trilemma" with only two horns, a mixed-basis 0%-vs-1.3% Gini comparison, and omission of the paper's own equivalized panel in which five of eight designs leave the Gini ABOVE its starting value even after a century (a rise that is itself mechanically inconsistent with the paper's uniform-growth assumption). Finally, the "current design" framing ignores that a state surcharge neutralizing regional rebates above ~€3.7M (ITSGF) has been in force since the paper's own valuation date (31 Dec 2022), was extended indefinitely, and raised €623M against a ~€1.5bn forecast — a live demonstration of the behavioral erosion (43% per 1pp in Switzerland; 3.21% per 0.1pp ATR in Spain, per the authors' own citations) that the paper's €12.7–15.9bn headline revenue and 100-year projections entirely exclude.

### [CONFIRMED] Abstract claims reforms 'increase tax progressivity'; the paper's own K and Suits indices fall under every design it champions
*id `progressivity-vs-incidence` · would change conclusions: medium · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** The abstract asserts that alternative scenarios "have the potential to increase tax progressivity, redistributive capacity and the decrease of long-run net wealth inequality," but Table 3 shows measured progressivity FALLING relative to the current IP under NO, CH, Sim. 1 and Piketty, flat under Sim. 3, and rising on all three outcome bases only under Sim. 2 — the design with among the smallest redistributive gains. The paper's own decomposition (Figure 4) and conclusions attribute all gains to the level (incidence) effect, so the abstract contradicts the body. The paper's true headline — redistribution and measured progressivity move in opposite directions across wealth-tax designs — is obscured by its own abstract. (A related hazard: Table 3's net-wealth R–S column is silently scaled ×1000 relative to its income panels — 0.5561 for NO cannot be a Gini change of 0.0005 — and the slides copy the mixed units into one table.)

**Paper evidence.** Abstract (p.1): "Results show that various alternative scenarios have the potential to increase tax progressivity, redistributive capacity and the decrease of long-run net wealth inequality." Table 3 (p.21), individual net wealth: K = 0.2313 (IP) vs 0.1912 (NO), 0.1202 (CH), 0.2262 (Sim.1), 0.2311 (Sim.3), 0.1969 (Piketty); rises only for FR (0.2442) and Sim.2 (0.2343). Suits: 0.8055 (IP) vs 0.5221 (NO), 0.2758 (CH), 0.7792 (Sim.3), 0.5701 (Piketty). Equivalized disposable income panel (p.21): K = 0.5363 (IP) vs 0.2587 (NO), 0.4060 (Sim.3), 0.2903 (Piketty); only Sim.2 rises (0.5655); FR falls (0.4925). p.22: "In every scenario, improvements are driven primarily by the Level Effect." Conclusions (p.33): "these gains stem mainly from higher incidence rather than changes in rate differentiation." Slides ("Where Do Redistributive Gains Come From?"): "driven primarily by the Level Effect (higher tax incidence) rather than changes in progressivity."

**Arithmetic.** K checks: Ct − Gpre = 0.9851 − 0.7538 = 0.2313 (IP) ✓; 0.945 − 0.7538 = 0.1912 (NO) ✓. K change vs IP: NO −17.3%, CH −48.0%, Piketty −14.9%, Sim.3 −0.1%, Sim.1 −2.2%; FR +5.6%, Sim.2 +1.3%. Disposable-income K change: NO −51.8%, Sim.3 −24.3%, Piketty −45.9%, Sim.2 +5.4%. R–S units: Table A.4 gives NO net-wealth R–S = t/(1−t)·K = 0.0029 × 0.1912 = 0.00055; Table 3 prints 0.5561 = ×1000; income panels print natural units (0.0016 × 0.5363 = 0.00086 ≈ 0.0008 printed). Which claims survive: 'redistributive capacity' rises in all 8 sims (trivially, more revenue); 'increase progressivity' survives only for Sim.2 (all bases) and FR (wealth base only, fails on income); long-run wealth-inequality decline survives on the individual basis for all sims but on the equivalized basis only for NO/Sim.3/Piketty within 100 years.

**Suggested question.** Your own Figure 4 and conclusions say the gains come from incidence, not progressivity — and Table 3 shows Kakwani and Suits falling under Norway, Sim. 3 and Piketty. Would you consider rewording the abstract to state the trade-off directly — broader bases buy redistribution at the cost of measured progressivity — since that inverse relationship seems to be your most interesting finding? And could Table 3 state the ×1000 scaling of the net-wealth R–S column?

- **internal verifier — CONFIRMED.** Abstract quote is verbatim (p.1). Table 3 (p.21, image-checked) confirms every K and S value: vs IP's K=0.2313, K falls under NO (0.1912, -17.3%), CH (0.1202, -48.0%), Sim.1 (0.2262), Piketty (0.1969), is flat under Sim.3 (0.2311), and rises only for FR (0.2442) and Sim.2 (0.2343); on disposable income only Sim.2's K rises (0.5655 vs 0.5363). The p.22 and p.33 quotes are verbatim ('primarily by the Level Effect'; 'mainly from higher incidence' -- note the paper says 'primarily'/'virtually all', not literally 'all'). The x1000 scaling is decisively established: every wealth-panel R-S equals 1000 x [t/(1-t) x K] from Table A.4 (IP 0.0463 = 1000 x 0.0002 x 0.2313; FR 0.0733 = 1000 x 0.0003 x 0.2442; NO 0.5561 vs Gpre-Gpost = 0.0005) with no scaling note, while income panels are natural units (IP 0.0016 x 0.5363 = 0.00086 = printed 0.0008), and the slides table (lines 279-296) mixes both scales in one table. Only softening: 'various alternative scenarios' in the abstract could be read distributively (FR/Sim.2 do raise K), so 'contradicts' is at the strong end -- but the designs the paper recommends (NO, Sim.3, Piketty) all show flat-to-falling K and S on the paper's own declared metrics (Sec. 4.3), so the substantive tension stands.
- **external verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** No external claims to test; every internal citation verified against the paper. The abstract quote (p.1), Table 3 values (p.21), and the incidence attributions (p.22 'driven primarily by the Level Effect'; p.33 'stem mainly from higher incidence'; slides 'rather than changes in progressivity') all match verbatim. Recomputed K = Ct − Gpre (0.9851−0.7538=0.2313 IP; 0.945−0.7538=0.1912 NO) and the direction claims: K and Suits fall vs IP for NO/CH/Sim.1/Piketty, Sim.3 is flat on the wealth base (0.2311 vs 0.2313) and falls on both income bases, and only Sim.2 rises on all three bases (FR rises on wealth only, 0.2442, but falls on disposable income, 0.4925 vs 0.5363). The ×1000 R–S scaling is arithmetically airtight via Table A.4: t/(1−t)·K = 0.0002×0.2313 = 0.0000463 vs Table 3's printed 0.0463 (exact ×1000) and 0.0029×0.1912 = 0.00055 vs printed 0.5561, while the income panels print natural units (0.0016×0.5363 − D 0.000041 = 0.0008 as printed); the slide table mixes both scalings in one table (0.556 next to 0.004). Rubric assigns PLAUSIBLE to no-external-claim findings; on internal evidence this one is fully accurate.
- **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** Every number checks against Table 3 (p.21): individual net-wealth K falls from 0.2313 (IP) to 0.1912 (NO), 0.1202 (CH), 0.2262 (Sim.1), 0.1969 (Piketty), is flat at 0.2311 (Sim.3), and rises only for FR (0.2442) and Sim.2 (0.2343); on disposable income only Sim.2 rises (0.5655 vs 0.5363). The body itself states gains are 'driven primarily by the Level Effect' (p.22) and 'stem mainly from higher incidence rather than changes in rate differentiation' (p.33), so the abstract's 'increase tax progressivity' contradicts the paper's own account for the designs it champions. The R-S scaling is verified: t/(1-t)*K = 0.0029*0.1912 = 0.00055 for NO vs printed 0.5561 (x1000), while the income panels print natural units (0.0016*0.5363 = 0.00086 vs printed 0.0008) — mixed units in one table, copied into the slides. The authors' only outs (the hedges 'various' and 'have the potential'; steeper AETR profiles as local progressivity) are exactly the ambiguity the finding criticizes, so the substance survives.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** We accept the substance: our own decomposition attributes the gains to the level effect (p.22, p.33), and Table 3 shows Kakwani and Suits falling under the Norwegian, Swiss, Sim. 1 and Piketty designs — that inverse movement between measured progressivity and redistribution is the paper's central mechanism, and we will reword the abstract to state the trade-off explicitly rather than let 'increase tax progressivity' stand unqualified. In our defence, the sentence says 'various alternative scenarios' and lists three distinct outcomes: Sim. 2 raises K and S on all three bases, the French design raises them on the wealth base, and every design raises redistributive capacity. We would also point to Figure 3: Sim. 3 and Piketty generate the steepest effective-rate profiles, so progressivity in the local AETR sense does rise even where the global indices fall mechanically as the base extends down the distribution — a distinction we should make explicit. And yes, the net-wealth R-S column in Table 3 is printed in units of 10^-3 while the income panels are in natural units; we will state the scaling in the table note and harmonize the slides.


### [CONFIRMED] 'Robust empirical evidence' rests on one cross-section, no standard errors, one uniform valuation ratio (reported with two different values), and a growth-rate sweep that is nearly vacuous by construction
*id `robust-evidence` · would change conclusions: low · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** The abstract's "our findings offer robust empirical evidence" is supported by exactly one robustness exercise: rerunning the long-run accumulation at g ∈ {1%, 2%, 3%} (Tables A.5–A.7). Under the paper's own stated method — wealth grows at uniform g and is reduced each year by a constant short-run rate — g cancels exactly from any scale-invariant inequality index (w_i(T) = w_i(0)·[(1+g)(1−t_i)]^T, so ratios are g-free), making this check close to tautological; the small printed g-differences actually suggest the implementation deviates from the stated method. Meanwhile no standard errors or confidence intervals accompany any Gini/K/S/R–S estimate, all simulations use the single 2022 wave despite the abstract advertising 2016–2023 data, behavioural responses are assumed away, and the single most consequential parameter — the uniform cadastral-to-market ratio applied to all real estate — is reported as 3.0838 in Equation A.1 but 3.1125 in the accompanying bullet on the same page (slides say ×3.08).

**Paper evidence.** Abstract (p.1): "our findings offer robust empirical evidence to inform policy discussions." Long-run method (p.15): "the same accumulation process takes place, but wealth is reduced each year by a constant ts/r." p.26 note and Tables A.5–A.7 (pp.44–46): the only sensitivity, g ∈ {1,2,3}%. Appendix p.39, Eq. A.1: "R = ... = 3.0838" vs the bullet immediately below: "resulting in a value of 3.1125." Abstract (p.2): data "for the 2016-2023 period"; Sec. 4.1 (p.7): "for the year 2022." Limitations (p.34): "First, the valuation of business assets may understate the taxable base... Second, the simulations assume no behavioural responses... Third, the long-run analysis relies on a simplified accumulation framework."

**Arithmetic.** g-invariance: w_i(T) = w_i(0)·[(1+g)(1−t_i)]^T ⇒ w_i(T)/w_j(T) = (w_i(0)/w_j(0))·[(1−t_i)/(1−t_j)]^T, independent of g; hence Gini(T) and the aggregate long-run rate 1 − Σw_i(0)(1−t_i)^T/Σw_i(0) are g-free under the stated method. Yet Table A.5/A.6 print NO t_l/r at T=100 as 22.45% (g=1%), 22.28% (g=2%), 22.11% (g=3%) — nonzero g-dependence that the stated formula cannot produce. Valuation ratio discrepancy: 3.1125/3.0838 − 1 = 0.93% divergence in the parameter that rescales the dominant asset class of Spanish household wealth (and 100% of the FR tax base).

**Threshold-path evidence (official sources).** The actual systems do not share one indexation rule. Spain's Ley 19/1991 fixes the state €300,000 main-residence exemption, €700,000 fallback minimum, and fallback schedule in nominal euros; its final provision 2 authorizes later legislative changes rather than automatic CPI indexing ([BOE, arts. 4, 28, 30 and DF2](https://www.boe.es/buscar/act.php?id=BOE-A-1991-14392)). Autonomous communities may vary the general minimum, schedule, deductions, and rebates—not the state-law home exemption—under [Ley 22/2009 art. 47](https://www.boe.es/buscar/act.php?id=BOE-A-2009-20375). Norway's annual Storting resolution sets state thresholds/rates and the municipal deduction and maximum rate; municipalities may adopt a lower rate, and there is no statutory CPI formula ([Tax Act §§15-1–15-2](https://lovdata.no/dokument/NL/lov/1999-03-26-14/KAPITTEL_15); [2022 resolution §§2-1 and 2-3](https://www.stortinget.no/no/Saker-og-publikasjoner/Vedtak/Vedtak/Sak/?did=40028923&p=86046)). Zurich instead automatically adjusts the §47 wealth-tax zero-rate band and bracket amounts to the Swiss CPI at the start of each two-year tax-coefficient (Steuerfuss) period ([Zurich StG §48](https://www.zh.ch/content/dam/zhweb/bilder-dokumente/themen/steuern-finanzen/steuern/vertreter/steuerbuch/stg-zh.pdf)); the 2022 schedule remained at its 2012 values because the relevant index had not risen, then adjustments took effect in 2024 and 2026 ([2022 decision](https://www.zh.ch/de/news-uebersicht/medienmitteilungen/2021/06/auch-per-1-januar-2022-kein-ausgleich-der-kalten-progression.html); [2026 adjustment](https://www.zh.ch/de/news-uebersicht/medienmitteilungen/2025/07/kanton-gleicht-kalte-progression-aus.html)). These Zurich rules are cantonal, not a Switzerland-wide wealth-tax rule.

**Suggested question.** The only robustness dimension tested is g — but under your stated constant-t_s/r accumulation, g cancels exactly from any scale-invariant index, so similarity across g is guaranteed rather than informative (and the small g-differences in A.5–A.7 suggest the code does something slightly different from the text). Would you consider robustness on the dimensions that can actually move results — the 3.08 valuation ratio (which appears as both 3.0838 and 3.1125 on p.39), regional heterogeneity in that ratio, and behavioural erosion — and toning down 'robust' until then?

- **internal verifier — CONFIRMED.** The 'robust empirical evidence' quote is verbatim (p.1), and the g-sweep is the paper's only robustness exercise (Table 5 note p.26: 'For robustness, alternative values of g... are also tested'; Tables A.5-A.7; slides: 'Robustness: similar results for g in {1%, 3%}'); no standard errors or CIs appear anywhere. The g-cancellation algebra is correct: under p.15's stated method (uniform growth, wealth 'reduced each year by a constant ts/r'), w_i(T)/w_j(T) = (w_i(0)/w_j(0)) x [(1-t_i)/(1-t_j)]^T is g-free, so Gini, top shares, and aggregate cumulative loss are exactly g-invariant -- yet A.5/A.6 print NO T=100 tl/r as 22.45/22.28/22.11% across g=1/2/3% and Ginis shift monotonically (Sim.3: 0.7147/0.7150/0.7153), a systematic pattern rounding cannot produce, so the implementation deviates from the stated method. I additionally verified the paper's tl/r definitions are mutually inconsistent: the p.15 definition matches the tables approximately (IP: 1-(1-0.0002)^25 = 0.499% vs printed 0.51%) while Table 5's note ('cumulative tax liabilities to accumulated wealth') would give ~0.40% at g=2%. The 3.0838 (Eq. A.1) vs 3.1125 (bullet directly below) discrepancy is confirmed, both on printed p.39; slides say 'x3.08'. One attribution error: the '2016-2023 period' phrase is in the introduction (p.2), not the abstract -- the abstract names no period; Sec. 4.1 (p.7) confirms 'for the year 2022'.
  - *Corrected claim:* Same claim with one fix: the 2016-2023 data window is advertised in the introduction (p.2), not the abstract (which names no period); the simulations use only the 2022 wave (Sec. 4.1, p.7). All other elements stand as written.
- **external verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** No external claims. Internals all verified: 'robust empirical evidence' (abstract p.1); the only sensitivity exercise is g ∈ {1,2,3}% (p.26 text plus Tables A.5–A.7 and the Table 5 note); no standard errors accompany any inequality/progressivity estimate anywhere in the paper; the data claim mismatch is real ('2016-2023 period' p.2 vs 'for the year 2022' p.7 and 'implemented on the 2022 Spanish administrative microdata' p.11). The g-cancellation algebra is correct: under p.15's stated method ('wealth is reduced each year by a constant ts/r'), w_i(T)=w_i(0)[(1+g)(1−t_i)]^T, so every scale-invariant index and t_l/r = 1 − Σw_i(0)(1−t_i)^T/Σw_i(0) are exactly g-free — yet the printed NO T=100 t_l/r is 22.45%/22.28%/22.11% at g=1/2/3% (Tables A.5–A.6) and Gpost also varies (0.7064/0.7068/0.7072), so the implementation demonstrably deviates from the stated method, exactly as claimed. The valuation-ratio discrepancy is verbatim on p.39: Eq. A.1 prints '= 3.0838' while the bullet directly below says 'resulting in a value of 3.1125' (0.93% apart); slides say '×3.08'.

The 3.0838 (Eq. A.1) vs 3.1125 (bullet below it) discrepancy on p.39 is verified, no SE or CI accompanies any inequality estimate anywhere, and Sec. 4.1 confirms only the 2022 wave is simulated (the '2016-2023 period' sentence is in the introduction on printed p.2, not the abstract proper — a minor citation slip by the finder). **Likely author response (advocate verifier).** 'Robust' was meant to describe the evidentiary base — full-population administrative registers rather than surveys subject to top-coding and nonresponse — not statistical inference, and we will say so; register-based microsimulation conventionally reports point estimates without sampling error. ### [CONFIRMED] The abstract promises evidence on 'efficiency'; the paper contains no efficiency analysis
*id `efficiency-claim` · would change conclusions: low · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** The abstract closes by claiming the findings "inform policy discussions on the role of wealth taxation in promoting equity, efficiency, and fiscal sufficiency in Spain," but no efficiency concept is measured anywhere: there is no deadweight-loss, behavioural-margin, savings/investment, or excess-burden analysis, and the limitations section concedes that behavioural responses are excluded entirely. Every mention of efficiency in the paper is either scene-setting or a citation of other studies' findings. Equity and revenue are genuinely analysed; efficiency is name-checked.

**Paper evidence.** Abstract (p.1): "...promoting equity, efficiency, and fiscal sufficiency in Spain." All in-text uses of 'efficiency' are literature or framing: p.1-2: "how alternative configurations... perform along the equity–efficiency frontier"; p.4: "persistent controversy over legitimacy and efficiency"; p.6 (citing others): "well-designed wealth taxes can enhance equity without strong efficiency losses"; p.6: Moore & Pecoraro and Saez & Zucman citations. Limitations (p.34): "the simulations assume no behavioural responses and therefore cannot capture avoidance or relocation effects." No table, figure, or equation in the paper computes any efficiency object.

**Suggested question.** The abstract offers evidence on equity, efficiency and fiscal sufficiency — I could find the first and third in the tables, but no efficiency analysis. Is the efficiency claim inherited from the broad-base/low-rate design literature you cite, and if so, would you either drop the word from the abstract or add even a stylized efficiency discussion (e.g., applying published elasticities to your designs)?

- **internal verifier — CONFIRMED.** The abstract quote is verbatim (p.1). An exhaustive scan of the full text finds 'efficiency' only in framing -- p.1 'equity-efficiency frontier'; p.2 'persistent controversy over legitimacy and efficiency' (note: this quote is on printed p.2, not p.4 as the finder cites -- quote itself accurate) -- and in literature citations on p.6 (Brulhart et al.-adjacent 'enhance equity without strong efficiency losses'; Moore & Pecoraro; Saez & Zucman). No table (1-6, A.1-A.8), figure (1-7, A.1), or equation (1-9, A.1) computes any deadweight-loss, elasticity, excess-burden, or savings/investment object, and p.34 states the simulations 'assume no behavioural responses.' The claim follows: efficiency is name-checked in the abstract but never analysed.
- **external verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** No external claims. I read the full paper: 'efficiency' appears only as framing or literature citation — pp.1-2 ('equity–efficiency frontier'), p.4 ('persistent controversy over legitimacy and efficiency'), p.6 (citing Brülhart/Jakobsen/Mas-Montserrat 'without strong efficiency losses', Moore & Pecoraro, Saez & Zucman) — and no table, figure, or equation among Tables 1–6/A.1–A.8 and Figures 1–7/A.1 computes any efficiency object (no deadweight loss, elasticity application, savings margin, or excess burden). The limitations section (p.34) explicitly concedes 'the simulations assume no behavioural responses.' So the abstract's promise of evidence on 'equity, efficiency, and fiscal sufficiency' (p.1) is unsupported on the efficiency dimension, exactly as the finding states.
- **advocate verifier — CONFIRMED.** Verified by a full read of the paper: no table, figure, or equation computes any efficiency object (no deadweight loss, elasticity application, savings/investment margin, or excess burden). Every occurrence of 'efficiency' is scene-setting or citation — 'equity-efficiency frontier' (p.2), 'controversy over legitimacy and efficiency' (p.4), 'without strong efficiency losses' citing Brülhart/Jakobsen/Mas-Montserrat (p.6), Moore & Pecoraro and Saez & Zucman summaries (p.6) — and the limitations concede 'the simulations assume no behavioural responses' (p.34). The abstract nonetheless promises evidence 'to inform policy discussions on the role of wealth taxation in promoting equity, efficiency, and fiscal sufficiency.' The authors' best response is a concession plus a design-relevance argument, which supports rewording, not the claim as written.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** We concede this directly: the paper computes no efficiency object — no deadweight loss, no behavioural margin — and the limitations section says exactly that (p.34). The word in the abstract carries over the design lesson of the literature we build on (Brülhart et al., 2022; Scheuer and Slemrod, 2021; Jakobsen et al., 2020): broad bases at moderate rates are the configurations that literature finds least distortive, so evidence on which designs deliver redistribution at moderate rates is relevant to the efficiency debate even though we do not measure it. As written, 'evidence to inform... efficiency' overstates our contribution, and in revision we would either drop the term or add a stylized exercise applying published elasticities to our simulated bases.


### [CONFIRMED] Long-run slide claims outrun the long-run tables: Norway's '>7 pp' decline is not in any table, the paper's own 'not a prediction' caveat disappears, and the adverse equivalized panel is omitted — and is itself internally anomalous
*id `longrun-framing` · would change conclusions: medium · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** The slides state "Norwegian and Piketty's schedule reduce the top 1% share by >7 pp at T = 100 (from ∼15% to <8%)" and "Long-run effects dominate," and the trilemma slide repeats ">7 pp in 100 years" for Sim.3/Piketty. On the equivalized basis matching the slide's own figure (start 14.67%), Norway's decline is 5.54 pp (to 9.13%) and Piketty's is 6.71 pp (to 7.96%) — only Sim. 3 exceeds 7 pp (−8.09); on the individual basis (start 18.76%, not ~15%) Norway is −6.85 pp. Norway satisfies '>7 pp' on no basis in the paper. The slides also drop the paper's own caveat that the exercise "is not intended to predict the future evolution of wealth inequality" and show only the individual-wealth long-run Gini table, omitting the equivalized panel in which the Gini RISES for decades and, under five of eight designs (IP, CH, FR, Sim.1, Sim.2), remains above its pre-tax starting value even at T = 100. That rise is itself mechanically suspect: with uniform growth (scale-invariant) and taxes the paper reports as progressive with respect to equivalized wealth (K = 0.3342, Table A.3), a 25-year cumulative levy of 0.50% of wealth cannot raise the Gini by +1.04 points, so the paper's stated explanation cannot be the mechanism.

**Paper evidence.** Slides, 'Long-Run Dynamics of Wealth Concentration': "Norwegian and Piketty's schedule reduce the top 1% share by >7 pp at T = 100 (from ∼15% to <8%)"; slides, Conclusions: "Long-run effects dominate"; trilemma slide: "Reduces top 1% wealth share by >7 pp in 100 years." Paper caveat (p.25): "This exercise is not intended to predict the future evolution of wealth inequality, but rather to illustrate a fundamental mechanism." Table A.7 (p.46, equivalized, g=2%): top 1% 14.67% → T=100: NO 9.13%, Piketty 7.96%, Sim.3 6.58%. Table A.5 (p.44, individual, g=2%): 18.76% → NO 11.91%, Piketty 10.76%, Sim.3 9.37%. Table 5 (p.26, equivalized panel): IP Gpre 0.6402 → Gpost 0.6506 at T=25, 0.6486 at T=100; CH 0.6409, FR 0.6474, Sim.1 0.6424, Sim.2 0.6459 at T=100 — all above 0.6402. Paper's explanation (p.27): "This reflects the role of household size introduced by equivalization and the fact that annual effective tax rates are still too small in the early decades to offset the natural growth of wealth at the top." Slides' long-run Gini table shows the individual panel only.

**Arithmetic.** Equivalized declines at T=100 (g=2%): NO 14.67 − 9.13 = 5.54 pp; Piketty 14.67 − 7.96 = 6.71 pp; Sim.3 14.67 − 6.58 = 8.09 pp. Individual: NO 18.76 − 11.91 = 6.85 pp; Piketty 18.76 − 10.76 = 8.00 pp; Sim.3 18.76 − 9.37 = 9.39 pp. So '>7 pp' holds for: Sim.3 (both bases), Piketty (individual only), NO (neither). 'From ~15% to <8%': start matches only the equivalized basis, on which NO ends at 9.13% (not <8%). Anomaly bound: IP's cumulative 25-year long-run rate is 0.51% of wealth; removing ≤0.51% of total wealth cannot move a Gini by +1.04 points in either direction (order-of-magnitude bound ~0.1 point), and uniform growth leaves any scale-invariant index unchanged, so 0.6402 → 0.6506 is unexplained by the stated model.

**Suggested question.** Two questions on the long-run exercise. First, on the equivalized basis of your own Figure 5, Norway's top-1% decline is 5.5 pp and Piketty's 6.7 pp — could you align the '>7 pp, to below 8%' slide claim with Tables A.5/A.7, and restore the paper's 'not a prediction' caveat on the slide? Second, in Table 5's equivalized panel the Gini rises from 0.6402 to 0.6506 by T=25 under the current IP, whose cumulative levy is only 0.5% of wealth — since uniform growth is distribution-neutral and Table A.3 says the tax is progressive on the equivalized basis, what in the computation generates that rise, and should five of eight designs really show century-long inequality above baseline?

Every decline recomputes exactly from A.5/A.7: equivalized (the basis of the slide's own figure, caption verified and Fig. 5 image-checked with start ~14.7%) NO -5.54pp, Piketty -6.71pp, Sim.3 -8.09pp; individual NO -6.85pp, Piketty -8.00pp -- so '>7pp' holds for NO on no basis and the 'from ~15% to <8%' start matches only the equivalized basis, where NO ends at 9.13%. The p.25 'not intended to predict' caveat is verbatim in the paper and absent from the slides, which also show only the individual-wealth long-run Gini table. Table 5's equivalized panel is verified: IP 0.6402 -> 0.6506 at T=25, and five of eight designs (IP, CH, FR, Sim.1, Sim.2) sit above 0.6402 at T=100. The anomaly is real and sharper than the finder states: even the theoretical worst case |dG| <= t/(1-t) x (1+Gpre) = 0.0050 x 1.64 = 0.83 points for a 0.50% cumulative levy is below the printed +1.04, the paper's own K=0.3342 (Table A.3) implies the sign should be negative (~-0.17pt), and A.7 shows the top-1% share FALLING (14.67 -> 14.36) in the same cells where the Gini rises -- the p.27 household-size explanation cannot generate this under uniform (scale-invariant) growth.
- **external verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** No external claims; every number verified against Tables 5/A.5/A.7 and the slide text, plus visual inspection of the paper's Figure 5 (p.28) and the trilemma slide. The p.25 caveat ('not intended to predict...') is verified in the paper and absent from the slides, which instead assert 'Long-run effects dominate'; the slides' long-run Gini table is individual-basis only, omitting the equivalized panel (Table 5) where Gpost exceeds 0.6402 at T=100 under IP (0.6486), CH (0.6409), FR (0.6474), Sim.1 (0.6424) and Sim.2 (0.6459) — verified. The authors would concede the slide corrections and would have to take the computational question away.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** On the slide we accept the corrections: on the equivalized basis of our own figure the T=100 declines are 5.5 pp (Norway), 6.7 pp (Piketty) and 8.1 pp (Sim. 3), so the '>7 pp, to below 8%' formulation is exact only for Sim. 3 and mixes bases for the others; we will restate the numbers basis by basis and restore the 'illustrates a mechanism, not a prediction' caveat from p.25. ### [CONFIRMED] Headline slide numbers: '4–5 Gini points' is actually 4–5 percent (1.6–1.8 points), the trilemma's 0%-vs-1.3% compares different Ginis, and the 'trilemma' has only two horns
*id `gini-points-percent-trilemma` · would change conclusions: low · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** The slides' 'Key result' box claims a wealth-tax-financed child benefit "could cut disposable income inequality by 4–5 Gini points"; Table 4 shows reductions of 0.0159–0.0184 in the Gini — i.e., 1.6–1.8 points on the conventional 0–100 scale, or 4.2–4.9 PERCENT — a ~2.6× overstatement in the deck's flagship number. The trilemma slide compares "Gini reduction: 0%" (current IP, individual net wealth) against "Gini reduction: 1.3%" (Sim.3/Piketty, equivalized disposable income): on a like-for-like basis the comparison is 0.00% vs 0.07% (wealth) or 0.21% vs 1.32% (income). The slide is titled "The Wealth Tax Design Trilemma" but displays two boxes and a two-way trade-off; no three horns are ever named. It also discloses the taxpayer share for the current design ("0.4% of population") but not for the reformed designs, whose 4.6–11 million taxpayers are 10.3–24.5% of the same population — the politically decisive number.

**Paper evidence.** Slides, 'Revenue as a Universal Child Benefit', Key result box: "A universal child benefit financed by a reformed wealth tax could cut disposable income inequality by 4–5 Gini points." Table 4 (p.25): Gpre 0.3779 → Gpost 0.3606 (NO), 0.3620 (Sim.3), 0.3595 (Piketty); printed reductions 4.58%, 4.21%, 4.87%. Trilemma slide (slides-23.png, verified visually): two boxes — "Current design (IP)... Only 170,404 taxpayers (0.4% of population); Revenue: €1.1 billion; Gini reduction: 0%" vs "Reformed design (Sim. 3 / Piketty)... 4.6–11 million taxpayers; Revenue: €12.7–15.9 billion; Gini reduction: 1.3%" — no third element. Table 3 (p.21): IP wealth Gini reduction 0.0000%, IP disposable-income reduction 0.2117%; Sim.3 wealth 0.0663%, disposable income 1.3231%; Piketty disposable 1.2966%. Table A.1 (p.38): 44,857,347 weighted individuals.

Trilemma shares: 170,404/44,857,347 = 0.380% ('0.4%' ✓); reformed: 4,626,929/44,857,347 = 10.31%; 11,010,000/44,857,347 = 24.54% — not shown on the slide. Revenue figures check: €1,125.58M ≈ €1.1bn ✓; €12,673.18M–€15,860.95M ≈ €12.7–15.9bn ✓. Like-for-like Gini comparison: wealth 0.0000% vs 0.0663%; income 0.2117% vs 1.3231%.

**Suggested question.** Three slide-level fixes I'd suggest before Friday: (1) the 'Key result' box — Table 4 shows 1.6–1.8 Gini points, i.e. 4–5 percent, not '4–5 Gini points'; (2) the trilemma's '0% vs 1.3%' uses the wealth Gini on the left and the disposable-income Gini on the right — like-for-like it is 0.21% vs 1.32%; (3) what exactly are the trilemma's three horns? If it is 'high measured progressivity, meaningful redistribution, taxing only the very rich — pick two,' that is a genuinely nice result worth stating explicitly, alongside the 10–25% of the population who would pay under the reformed designs.

- **internal verifier — CONFIRMED.** The 'Key result' box quote is verbatim, and Table 4 (p.25) confirms the misreading: absolute Gini reductions are 0.0159-0.0184 (1.6-1.8 points on the 0-100 scale) while the printed 4.21-4.87% column is a relative reduction (recomputed: 0.3779-0.3606=0.0173; 0.0173/0.3779=4.58%), so '4-5 Gini points' overstates by ~2.6x. The trilemma slide was image-checked: exactly two boxes under a 'Trilemma' title with no third horn named anywhere; its '0%' matches only IP's individual-net-wealth row (0.0000%, Table 3) while '1.3%' matches Sim.3/Piketty's equivalized-disposable-income rows (1.3231%/1.2966%) -- a cross-basis comparison, since like-for-like is 0.00% vs 0.07% (wealth) or 0.21% vs 1.32% (income). Population shares recompute exactly from the slide's own implied denominator (170,404/44,857,347 = 0.38% ~ '0.4%'): the reformed designs' 4,626,929 and 11,010,000 taxpayers are 10.31% and 24.55%, and the slide indeed omits these shares, printing only absolute counts.
