### [PLAUSIBLE] The 'current design delivers no redistribution' framing ignores the ITSGF, in force since the paper's own valuation date, extended indefinitely, and already delivering part of Sim. 2
*id `itsgf-baseline` · would change conclusions: medium · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** The paper frames Sim. 2 (removing regional rebates) as a hypothetical reform against a 2022 baseline where Madrid and Andalusia rebate 100%, and the slides describe the current system as "low revenue and no redistribution." But Ley 38/2022 (in force 29 Dec 2022) created the ITSGF, a state surcharge that neutralizes regional rebates above ~€3.7M net wealth (0% to €3M over a €700k exemption, then 1.7%/2.1%/3.5%, crediting IP paid); its first accrual fell on 31 December 2022 — the very date on which the paper's wealth data are valued — and it was extended indefinitely by RDL 8/2023. Its first campaign (July 2023) raised €623M from 12,010 taxpayers, 89% from Madrid, so actual current-law revenue (IP + ITSGF ≈ €1.87bn) exceeds the paper's simulated "current design" baseline (€1.13bn) by ~66%. Footnote 2's description ("introduced in December 2022 for 2023–2024") both misstates the applicable years and omits the indefinite extension — by the paper's writing date (March 2026), 'temporary' had been inoperative as a description for over two years.

**Paper evidence.** p.5: "in December 2022, the central Government approved an additional Wealth Tax... (ITSGF) applicable on 2023 as a state-level surcharge to offset regional rebates above €3.7 million net wealth, crediting any IP paid. Note that our baseline is the 2022 IP framework while the state solidarity surcharge (ITSGF) is not included in our simulations." Footnote 2 (p.5): "The ITSGF, introduced in December 2022 for 2023–2024... is not modelled here to preserve comparability with foreign systems and pre-reform incidence." Sim. 2 description (p.10): "eliminates regional deductions and allowances, simulating a scenario in which the tax is applied uniformly across all regions." Slides, Conclusions: "Current IP severely limited: narrow base + regional rebates ⇒ low revenue and no redistribution." Table 3 (p.21): IP revenue €1,125.58M. p.34: "Future research could incorporate the new state-level solidarity tax."

**External evidence.** BOE consolidated text of Ley 38/2022 (https://www.boe.es/buscar/act.php?id=BOE-A-2022-22684): Art. 3 creates the ITSGF; mínimo exento €700,000; brackets 0% to €3,000,000, 1.7% to €5,347,998.03, 2.1% to €10,695,996.06, 3.5% above; "aplicable en los dos primeros ejercicios en los que se devengue a partir de la fecha de su entrada en vigor" (in force 29 Dec 2022; devengo each 31 December); IP paid is credited; extended by RDL 8/2023 (DA 5.2): "Se prorroga la aplicación del impuesto... en tanto no se produzca la revisión de la tributación patrimonial." Hacienda/Moncloa press release, 20 Sept 2023 (https://www.lamoncloa.gob.es/serviciosdeprensa/notasprensa/hacienda/paginas/2023/200923-impuesto-solidaridad-grandes-fortunas.aspx): €623M collected in the July 2023 campaign from 12,010 taxpayers (~0.1% of filers); Madrid: 10,302 taxpayers, €555M; average ~€52,000; IP+ITSGF combined "más de 1.868 millones de euros." Newtral fact-check (https://www.newtral.es/impuesto-solidaridad-grandes-fortunas/20230921/, seen in search results): the 2023 collection corresponds to "ejercicio 2022, primer ejercicio de devengo," ~60% below the government's ~€1.5bn forecast.

**Arithmetic.** First devengo: in force 29 Dec 2022 + accrual every 31 December ⇒ first accrual 31 Dec 2022 (confirmed by the July 2023 filing campaign — a 31 Dec 2023 accrual could not be declared in July 2023). Madrid share: 555/623 = 89.1%. Actual current-law revenue vs simulated baseline: (623 + ~1,245 IP) / 1,126 ≈ 1.66 — about 66% more than the paper's 'current design.' Effective floor: €700k exemption + €3M zero bracket ≈ €3.7M ✓ (matches the paper's own description). ITSGF taxpayers (12,010) ≈ 7% of the paper's 170,404 effective IP taxpayers, but concentrated exactly in the rebate regions whose 'horizontal inequity' motivates Sim. 2.

**Suggested question.** Since your wealth data are valued at 31 December 2022 — the exact date of the ITSGF's first accrual — the 'current design' your baseline simulates was already superseded on your own reference date, and actual current-law revenue (€1.87bn IP+ITSGF in 2023, 89% of the surcharge from Madrid) exceeds your €1.13bn baseline by two-thirds. Would you consider (a) correcting footnote 2 (first ejercicios are 2022–2023, extended indefinitely by RDL 8/2023), and (b) reframing Sim. 2 as 'extending the ITSGF's existing rebate-neutralization from the >€3.7M segment to the whole schedule,' with the incremental revenue and Gini effects measured against an IP+ITSGF baseline rather than a pre-ITSGF one?

- **internal verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** Every internal premise verifies: the p.5 and footnote-2 quotes are verbatim (including 'applicable on 2023' and 'introduced in December 2022 for 2023-2024'), the baseline explicitly excludes the ITSGF, Sim.2's description (p.10), the slides' 'low revenue and no redistribution', Table 3's EUR 1,125.58M, and p.34's future-research sentence all check, and the finding's EUR 3.7M-floor arithmetic matches the paper's own description. But the load-bearing facts -- first accrual 31-Dec-2022, the footnote's years being wrong, indefinite extension via RDL 8/2023, EUR 623M from 12,010 taxpayers, 89% Madrid, EUR 1.87bn combined -- are all external (BOE/Moncloa/Newtral) and cannot be verified from the paper, slides, or arithmetic under this lens; the paper's own text asserts the contrary dating. One internal fact strengthens the finding if the external record holds: the paper is internally inconsistent about its baseline year -- Sec. 4.2 (p.9) says 'the benchmark is the legislation applicable to fiscal year 2023' while footnote 6 and p.5 say 2022 -- and 2023 legislation would include the ITSGF by the paper's own account.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** All external claims verified on primary sources I fetched. BOE consolidated Ley 38/2022: 'Entrada en vigor: 29/12/2022'; 'El impuesto se devengará el 31 de diciembre de cada año'; applicable 'en los dos primeros ejercicios en los que se devengue a partir de la fecha de su entrada en vigor' — so the first accrual was 31-Dec-2022 (the paper's own valuation date) and the two 'temporary' ejercicios are 2022–2023, making footnote 2's 'for 2023–2024' wrong; brackets 0% to €3M / 1.7% to €5,347,998.03 / 2.1% to €10,695,996.06 / 3.5% above, €700k mínimo exento (≈€3.7M effective floor, matching the paper's own description) and IP-quota credit; the consolidated text carries the RDL 8/2023 prórroga verbatim: 'Se prorroga la aplicación del impuesto regulado en este artículo, en tanto no se produzca la revisión de la tributación patrimonial' (I confirmed the substance and attribution to RDL 8/2023, though not the finder's specific 'DA 5.2' provision number). The Moncloa/Hacienda release (fetched) confirms €623M, 12,010 taxpayers (0.1% of contribuyentes), Madrid 10,302/€555M (=89.1%), average €52,000, and combined 'La recaudación total por el Impuesto de Solidaridad ... y por el Impuesto sobre el Patrimonio asciende en 2023 a más de 1.868 millones de euros' → +66% vs the paper's simulated €1,125.58M baseline; press coverage (Newtral et al., via search) confirms the €623M corresponds to ejercicio 2022, the first devengo, and 2025–26 sources (Garrigues, OCU, AEAT sede) confirm the indefinite extension remains in force.
- **advocate verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** The factual components are confirmed at primary sources this session: BOE consolidated Ley 38/2022 shows entry into force 29 Dec 2022, devengo 'el 31 de diciembre de cada año', applicability 'en los dos primeros ejercicios en los que se devengue a partir de la fecha de su entrada en vigor' (hence first ejercicios 2022-2023, not fn.2's '2023-2024'), minimo exento 700k with 0% to 3M then 1.7/2.1/3.5% (floor ~3.7M as the paper says), IP creditable, and indefinite prorroga via RDL 8/2023 DA 5.2; Moncloa's 20-9-2023 release confirms 623M from 12,010 taxpayers, Madrid 555M/10,302 (89.1%), and combined IP+ITSGF 'mas de 1.868 millones' vs the simulated 1,126M baseline (+66%). So the footnote's dates are wrong, the first accrual coincided with the paper's 31-Dec-2022 valuation date, and the trilemma's 'current design... 1.1 billion' describes superseded law. However, the exclusion itself is explicitly disclosed with a rationale (p.5 and fn.2: comparability with foreign systems, pre-reform incidence), incorporation is flagged as future work (p.34), and the slides' 'Current IP severely limited' sentence is literally about the IP, which the separate crediting surcharge does not change — a genuine partial answer that blunts 'ignores'. The finding therefore partially survives: the corrections (dates, indefinite extension, qualifying 'current design', reporting Sim.2/Sim.3 increments against an IP+ITSGF baseline) would have to be accepted, but the deliberate-baseline defense stands for the simulations themselves.
  - *Corrected claim:* The paper does not ignore the ITSGF — it describes it on p.5, discloses its exclusion in fn.2 with a comparability rationale, and lists it as future work (p.34) — but it misdates it (first ejercicios were 2022-2023, with the first accrual on the paper's own 31-Dec-2022 valuation date, and RDL 8/2023 extended it indefinitely), and the slides present the pre-ITSGF IP alone as 'the current design' with 1.1bn revenue and 'no redistribution', although actual 2023 current-law collections (IP+ITSGF) were ~1.87bn, ~66% above the simulated baseline, with the surcharge already delivering rebate neutralization above ~3.7M.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** The exclusion of the ITSGF is deliberate and disclosed — p.5 states our baseline is the 2022 IP framework and footnote 2 explains the comparability rationale — and incorporating the surcharge is already flagged as future research on p.34; Sim. 2 also answers a broader question than the ITSGF, neutralizing rebates across the entire schedule rather than only above 3.7 million euros. We concede the dating: the law entered into force on 29 December 2022 with accrual each 31 December, so its first two ejercicios were 2022-2023, its first accrual coincided with our valuation date, and RDL 8/2023 has extended it indefinitely; we will correct footnote 2 and the 'applicable on 2023' sentence. We also accept that the deck should not present the IP alone as 'the current design' without qualification, given that IP plus ITSGF collected over 1.8 billion euros in 2023 against our simulated 1.1 billion baseline — though the IP-specific statements remain accurate, since the surcharge is a separate state tax that credits IP paid. Re-basing all simulations on an IP+ITSGF baseline would compromise the clean cross-design comparison we are after, but reporting the incremental revenue and inequality effects of Sim. 2 and Sim. 3 against that baseline is a reasonable addition we can provide.


### [CONFIRMED] 'Women significantly less likely to be taxpayers' rests on marginal effects of 0.005–0.48 pp; the paper's own Figure 6b shows a roughly 50/50 split
*id `gender-magnitudes` · would change conclusions: low · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** Section 5 reports "statistically robust gender differences: women are significantly less likely to be effective taxpayers in every scenario," and juxtaposes UK evidence that ~68% of potential wealth-tax payers would be male. But the estimated female marginal effects are tiny in absolute terms — e.g. −0.00015 under the current IP against a taxpayer base rate of 0.38% — and translate to roughly 1–4% lower relative probability across designs; the paper's own descriptive Figure 6b shows women as approximately half of effective taxpayers under every design, and p.28 itself says "Gender patterns remain balanced across scenarios." With N of 2.1–2.8 million, statistical significance is nearly automatic; the narrative conflates statistical with substantive significance, and the UK juxtaposition (68% male) suggests a gap an order of magnitude larger than the one estimated here.

**Paper evidence.** p.30: "The results show statistically robust gender differences: women are significantly less likely to be effective taxpayers in every scenario. The marginal effect is negative and significant across all eight simulations, ranging from –0.00005 in the French model to –0.0048 in the Swiss one"; p.30: "around 68% of potential wealth-tax payers in the United Kingdom would be male under high thresholds (Advani et al., 2021)." Table 6 (p.31): Female ME −0.00015 (IP), −0.00351 (NO), −0.00476 (CH), −0.00005 (FR), −0.00174 (Sim.1), −0.00013 (Sim.2), −0.00181 (Sim.3), −0.00291 (Piketty); N = 2,055,252–2,769,593. p.28: "Gender patterns remain balanced across scenarios, though women represent a slightly smaller share as the tax base expands." Figure 6b (p.29, inspected visually): women ≈ 50–53% of effective taxpayers in every simulation. Table A.1 (p.38): women 50.93% of the population.

**Arithmetic.** Relative effects = ME / weighted base rate (Ntp/44,857,347, Table 3): IP −0.00015/0.00380 = −3.9%; NO −0.00351/0.1705 = −2.1%; CH −0.00476/0.2764 = −1.7%; FR −0.00005/0.00262 = −1.9%; Sim.1 −0.00174/0.0409 = −4.3%; Sim.2 −0.00013/0.00450 = −2.9%; Sim.3 −0.00181/0.1032 = −1.8%; Piketty −0.00291/0.2455 = −1.2%. (If the logit is unweighted, sample base rates are higher — e.g. ~1.4% for IP — making relative effects smaller still.) Contrast: UK comparator implies men overrepresented ~2.1:1 (68/32); the Spanish estimates imply ~1.01–1.05:1 conditional on covariates, and Figure 6b shows near-parity unconditionally.

**Suggested question.** Your Figure 6b shows women are about half of effective taxpayers under every design, and the female marginal effects are 1–4% in relative terms — far from the UK's 68%-male profile you cite. Would you report the marginal effects relative to each scenario's base rate (or as odds ratios), and reconcile the 'significantly less likely' narrative with the 'patterns remain balanced' description on p.28? It may be worth noting whether Spain's community-property regime, which splits marital wealth between spouses for IP filing, is what mutes the gap relative to the UK.

- **internal verifier — CONFIRMED.** The p.30 and p.28 quotes are verbatim, including the '68% male' UK juxtaposition, and Table 6's female marginal effects all match (-0.00015 IP to -0.00476 CH). The relative-effect arithmetic recomputes exactly using Table 3's Ntp over 44,857,347: -3.9% (IP), -2.1% (NO), -1.7% (CH), -1.9% (FR), -4.3% (Sim.1), -2.9% (Sim.2), -1.8% (Sim.3), -1.2% (Piketty) -- roughly 1-4% lower relative probability vs the UK's implied 2.1:1 male overrepresentation. Figure 6b (printed p.29, image-checked) shows the % women bars at roughly 50-53% in all eight designs -- at or slightly above the 50.93% population share (Table A.1) -- so unconditional near-parity coexists with the 'significantly less likely' narrative, exactly as claimed. One error in the finder's evidence: Table 6's N range is 1,566,511 (Sim.1) to 2,769,593, not 2,055,252-2,769,593, so 'N of 2.1-2.8 million' should read ~1.6-2.8 million -- immaterial to the point that significance is nearly automatic at these sample sizes.
  - *Corrected claim:* Same claim with the sample sizes corrected: Table 6's N ranges from 1,566,511 (Sim.1) to 2,769,593 -- i.e., ~1.6-2.8 million, not 2.1-2.8 million; the statistical-vs-substantive significance point is unaffected.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** The finding's one external claim checks out: Advani, Hughson & Tarrant (2021), Fiscal Studies 42(3–4):699–736, report the gender imbalance is 'most pronounced for a wealth tax starting at £5 million, under which 68 per cent of taxpayers are male' — matching the paper's p.30 citation and the finder's implied ~2.1:1 UK contrast. All internals verified: Table 6 female marginal effects and Ns (p.31) match the quoted values; the p.30 'statistically robust gender differences ... ranging from –0.00005 in the French model to –0.0048 in the Swiss one' and p.28 'Gender patterns remain balanced across scenarios' quotes are verbatim; Figure 6b (paper-31.png, inspected) shows women at roughly 50–53% of effective taxpayers in every simulation against a 50.93% population share (Table A.1). Recomputed relative effects ME/weighted base rate: IP −3.9%, NO −2.1%, CH −1.7%, FR −1.9%, Sim.1 −4.3%, Sim.2 −2.9%, Sim.3 −1.8%, Piketty −1.2% — an order of magnitude below the UK comparator — and the statistical-vs-substantive point is sound at N = 2.06–2.77M. The community-property suggestion is appropriately posed as a question, not asserted.
- **advocate verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** All figures verified: Table 6 (p.31) female marginal effects -0.00005 to -0.00476; base rates from Table 3 Ntp over 44,857,347 give relative effects of -1.2% (Piketty) to -4.3% (Sim.1), confirming the finder's arithmetic; Figure 6b (p.29, inspected visually) shows women at roughly half of effective taxpayers in every simulation; p.28 states 'Gender patterns remain balanced across scenarios' while p.30 states 'women are significantly less likely to be effective taxpayers in every scenario' and juxtaposes the UK's 68%-male profile (a ~2.1:1 ratio vs Spain's ~1.01-1.05:1 conditional). The tension and the misreading risk are real, and with N of 2.1-2.8 million significance is nearly automatic. But the paper itself already reports the balanced descriptive pattern, 'significantly' is literally accurate as a statistical claim, and the Advani citation is framed as directional evidence of a gender wealth gap rather than a magnitude benchmark — a genuine partial answer. The finding survives as a presentation defect with a cheap fix (relative effects/odds ratios, reconciling pp.28 and 30), not as an overstated-results charge the authors must fully concede.

**Likely author response (advocate verifier).** The marginal effects are small in absolute terms because being an effective taxpayer is rare, which is exactly why Figure 6 and p.28 describe the composition as balanced; the regression finding is that the female gap is uniformly negative, precisely estimated, and robust to controls — which is all that 'statistically robust' claims. We did not assert magnitudes comparable to the UK: Advani et al. (2021) is cited as directional evidence of a gender wealth gap under a different institution, and Spain's individual filing of largely community-property marital wealth mechanically splits couples' wealth between spouses and mutes the gap — a point we are happy to make explicit. We accept the constructive fix: we will report effects relative to each scenario's base rate (women are roughly 1-4% less likely than comparable men) or as odds ratios, and align the wording of pages 28 and 30 so the statistical and substantive statements cannot be read against each other.


### [PLAUSIBLE] The policy conclusion rests on a 14× revenue counterfactual with zero behavioural response — while the authors' own citations, Norway's actual yield, and Spain's own ITSGF experience all indicate substantial erosion
*id `static-policy-counterfactual` · would change conclusions: high · finder confidence: high*

**Claim.** The conclusions urge "broadening its base, harmonizing regional rules, and increasing effective incidence," quantified by static estimates — most prominently the Norwegian design raising €15.5bn (13.7× the simulated current €1.13bn, 1.13% of Spain's 2022 GDP) from 7.6M taxpayers. Three verified benchmarks say the static number is an upper bound, not a central estimate: (1) Brülhart et al. (2022), which the paper cites, finds reported taxable wealth responds by at least 43% to a 1 pp rate change over 6 years — roughly the rate increase the Norwegian design imposes on the newly taxed — implying the €15.5bn could shrink toward ~€9bn; (2) Mas-Montserrat et al. (2025), also cited, finds a 0.1 pp increase in Spain's average wealth-tax rate reduced taxable wealth 3.21% within 4 years, with 92.6% of the revenue loss flowing through the IRPF–IP liability cap — a cap the paper removes from all foreign-design simulations, mechanically inflating their revenue relative to any implementable Spanish version; (3) Norway's actual wealth tax yields ~0.58% of GDP (NOK 31.1bn assessed for 2024) — about half the simulated Spanish yield share — and Spain's own ITSGF delivered €623M against a ~€1.5bn government forecast. The 100-year projections compound the zero-response assumption over a century on a frozen 2022 population (mean age 43.5). None of this is quantified anywhere in the paper; the limitation is one sentence on p.34.

**Paper evidence.** Conclusions (p.33): "enhancing the redistributive capacity of the Spanish Wealth Tax requires broadening its base, harmonizing regional rules, and increasing effective incidence rather than adjusting marginal rate schedules"; slides: "Policy implication: Trying to redistribute wealth requires harmonizing regional rules and increasing tax incidence." Table 3 (p.21): Sim. NO revenue €15,456.06M, t = 0.29%, Ntp 7,647,459 vs IP €1,125.58M, t = 0.02%. p.10: "the simulations of international models do not incorporate Spain's joint IRPF–IP tax-liability limit." p.6 (the paper's own reading of the literature): "these responses do not fully offset the redistributive gains." p.34: "the simulations assume no behavioural responses and therefore cannot capture avoidance or relocation effects." Table A.1 (p.38): mean age 43.53.

**External evidence.** Brülhart et al. 2022, AEJ:Economic Policy (https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257/pol.20200258, fetched): "a 1 percentage point drop in a canton's wealth tax rate raises reported taxable wealth by at least 43 percent after 6 years" (≈24% mobility, ≈21% housing prices, remainder consistent with evasion). Mas-Montserrat et al. 2025, J. Public Economics 246:105351 (ScienceDirect abstract seen via search, https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0047272725000490): a 0.1 pp increase in the average tax rate reduces taxable wealth 3.21% over 4 years; use of the liability limit accounts for 92.6% of the revenue reduction. SSB tax statistics (https://www.ssb.no/en/inntekt-og-forbruk/skatt-for-personer/statistikk/skatt-for-personer, fetched): assessed formuesskatt NOK 28.8bn (2023), NOK 31.1bn (2024). SSB national accounts (https://www.ssb.no/en/nasjonalregnskap-og-konjunkturer/nasjonalregnskap/statistikk/nasjonalregnskap, fetched): 2024 nominal GDP NOK 5,382.4bn. INE Contabilidad Nacional Anual, Revisión Estadística 2024 (https://ine.es/dyngs/Prensa/CNA2023.htm, seen in search results): Spain 2022 GDP at current prices €1,373,629M. Moncloa/Hacienda 20-9-2023 (fetched) and Newtral (search): ITSGF €623M vs ~€1.5bn forecast.

**Arithmetic.** Revenue multiple: 15,456.06/1,125.58 = 13.7×. Share of GDP: 15,456.06/1,373,629 = 1.13% (Spain, simulated NO design) vs 31.1/5,382.4 = 0.58% (Norway, actual 2024) — the simulation yields ~2× the design's home-country yield share, in a country with lower mean wealth per capita. Brülhart illustration: 15.5bn × (1 − 0.43) ≈ €8.8bn if affected taxpayers' reported base falls 43% (their statutory rate rises ~1 pp from ~0). Mas-Montserrat illustration: Sim. NO raises the average effective rate 0.29% − 0.02% = 0.27 pp = 2.7 × 0.1 pp ⇒ ≈ 2.7 × 3.21% ≈ 8.7% base erosion within 4 years even at the modest Spain-calibrated average elasticity. ITSGF realization rate: 623/1,500 ≈ 42% of forecast.

**Suggested question.** A constructive framing for Friday: your qualitative ranking (base breadth beats rate steepness) may well survive behavioural adjustment, but the headline magnitudes cannot — Switzerland's 43%-per-1pp response (your citation), your co-country evidence of 3.21% per 0.1 pp with 92.6% of losses via the IRPF–IP cap (which your foreign simulations remove), Norway collecting ~0.58% of GDP where your Norwegian design on Spain yields 1.13%, and Spain's own ITSGF landing at 42% of forecast. Would you present the €12.7–15.9bn figures explicitly as static upper bounds with a behavioural-erosion band — e.g., applying the Mas-Montserrat and Brülhart elasticities — and note that the 100-year projections compound the zero-response assumption? A fair summary might read: 'On 2022 register data with no behavioural response, broad-base designs raise 11–14× more revenue by taxing 10–25% of individuals rather than 0.4%, reduce disposable-income inequality by up to 1.3% (4–5% if recycled as a child benefit), and — mechanically compounded for a century — cut top-1% wealth shares by up to 8–9 pp, at the cost of lower measured progressivity.'

- **internal verifier — PLAUSIBLE.** Every internal element verifies: the p.33 conclusions quote, the slides' policy-implication box, Table 3's figures (15,456.06/1,125.58 = 13.7x; 0.29% vs 0.02% = 0.27pp; 7,647,459 taxpayers), the p.10 disclosure that foreign simulations drop the IRPF-IP cap (which, per the cap's description on p.4, can only reduce liability, so removing it mechanically raises simulated revenue relative to a cap-constrained Spanish implementation), the p.6 characterizations ('elasticities are non-negligible among the richest'; 'do not fully offset the redistributive gains'), the single-sentence p.34 limitation, and mean age 43.53 (A.1); the long-run exercise does compound a frozen 2022 cross-section with no demographics. However, the quantitative benchmarks doing the argumentative work -- Brulhart's 43% per 1pp, Mas-Montserrat's 3.21% per 0.1pp with 92.6% via the cap, Norway's 0.58% of GDP, Spain's EUR 1,373,629M GDP, and the ITSGF's 42% forecast realization -- are external fetches I cannot verify under this internal-evidence lens, so the specific haircuts (~EUR 9bn, 8.7% erosion, 2x Norway's yield share) rest on evidence outside the paper. The qualitative core -- static headline magnitudes presented as findings while the paper's own cited literature reports non-negligible top-end responses that are excluded -- is fully supported internally.
- **external verifier — CONFIRMED.** Every external benchmark re-verified on primary sources. Brülhart et al. 2022 (AEA page, fetched): 'a 1 percentage point drop in a canton's wealth tax rate raises reported taxable wealth by at least 43 percent after 6 years', decomposed as 24% mobility, 21% housing prices, minimal savings, sizable evasion. Mas-Montserrat, Durán-Cabré & Esteller-Moré 2025 (JPubE 246:105351; ScienceDirect 403'd, abstract confirmed via search): a 0.1pp increase in the average tax rate reduces taxable wealth 3.21% over 4 years, with use of the limit accounting for 92.6% of the revenue reduction — and the paper does strip that limit from all foreign-design sims (p.10). SSB (fetched): assessed formuesskatt NOK 28.821bn (2023) and NOK 31.11bn (2024); Norway 2024 GDP NOK 5,382,441M → 0.578% of GDP, vs the sim's 15,456.06/1,373,629 = 1.13% (Spain 2022 GDP €1,373,629M confirmed via INE's Revisión Estadística 2024 press release). ITSGF €623M vs ~€1.5bn forecast confirmed (Moncloa fetched; Newtral via search). Two tempering caveats that adjust framing, not facts: the ITSGF shortfall is substantially a forecast-design artifact — the €1.5bn assumed all regions kept 100% rebates, so IP crediting (not only behaviour) absorbed the base — and Brülhart's 43% bundles within-country mobility, easier across cantons than out of Spain; both leave intact the core point that the paper's headline magnitudes are static upper bounds it nowhere quantifies (one sentence on p.34).
  - *Corrected claim:* For the third benchmark only: 'Spain's own ITSGF delivered €623M against a ~€1.5bn forecast — a shortfall driven partly by the forecast's assumption that every region kept a 100% rebate (so IP crediting, a design feature, absorbed much of the base) and partly by behaviour — evidence that static projections for this tax family over-promise, alongside the two behavioural elasticity benchmarks.'

### What the verifiers flagged as missed by this lens
- **[internal]** Four internal-consistency defects this lens missed, all usable live: (1) Table A.8 (p.47) contains an impossible cell: the Sim. Piketty x Sim. 2 overlap is 297,219, exceeding Sim. 2's own total of 201,687 effective taxpayers (printed in the same table's Np row and in Table 3) -- an intersection cannot exceed a marginal, so at least one taxpayer count in the paper is wrong. (2) The paper cannot settle how many simulations it has: the abstract says 'eight simulated scenarios' compared with the current tax (p.1, i.e., 8 alternatives), Table 1's title says 'seven simulations' while listing eight rows (p.11), and printed p.16 says both 'nine simulated tax scenarios' and 'the seven simulations proposed' within ten lines. (3) The methodological worked example on p.15 ('an annual ts/r of only 0.05% results in a long-run burden of 0.83% after 25 years and 2.49% after 85 years') is inconsistent with the paper's own compounding rule: 1-(1-0.0005)^25 = 1.24% and 1-(1-0.0005)^85 = 4.16%; the printed pair implies t of about 0.03%. The paper's own tables DO follow the compounding rule (IP: ts/r 0.02% gives tl/r 0.51% at T=25, matching 1-(1-0.0002)^25 = 0.50%), while Table 5's note states a third, different definition ('ratio of cumulative tax liabilities to accumulated wealth') that matches neither the p.15 text nor the printed numbers (it would give roughly 0.40% for IP at T=25, g=2%) -- three mutually inconsistent statements of the long-run method. (4) Baseline-year contradiction relevant to the ITSGF finding: Sec. 4.2 (p.9) says 'the benchmark is the legislation applicable to fiscal year 2023', while footnote 6 (p.9) and p.5 say the 2022 framework -- and 2023 law includes the ITSGF by the paper's own account. Two smaller ones: the French thresholds are swapped between passages (p.5 gives ISF above EUR 1.3M and IFI above EUR 800k; p.10 gives ISF 'above 800,000'; Table A.2 and the slides give FR a EUR 1.3M threshold with no reconciliation); and Table A.1's 170,408 'Wealth Tax filers' vs 170,404 effective taxpayers implies only 4 fully-rebated filers in the data, hard to square with footnote 9's exclusion of 100%-rebated Madrid/Andalusia filers and with Sim. 2 finding ~31k additional payers once rebates are removed.
- **[external]** Three verified items this lens missed, all useful for the discussion: (1) Madrid's Ley 12/2023 (BOE-A-2024-5609, seen in search results) temporarily suspends Madrid's 100% IP rebate for as long as the ITSGF is in force, and ITSGF collections collapsed from €623M (ejercicio 2022) to about €38M in the 2024 campaign (AEAT data via OCU) as the revenue migrated to regional IP — i.e., part of Sim. 2's counterfactual (rebate neutralization) has ALREADY happened through state-regional interaction, which both sharpens the itsgf-baseline finding and gives Max a live 2024–26 institutional update. (2) AEAT's published IP statistics for 2022 report 230,365 declarantes (~185,100 with positive cuota; sede.agenciatributaria.gob.es statistics, via search), but Table A.1 (p.38) reports only 170,408 weighted 'Wealth Tax filers' and Table 3's Ntp is 170,404 — yet footnote 9 (p.20) says fully-rebated Madrid/Andalusia filers are EXCLUDED from Ntp, so only 4 weighted individuals separate 'filers' from 'effective taxpayers'; either zero-cuota declarants are largely absent from the PH (relevant because p.8 says business assets are only recorded for IP filers) or 'filers' already means positive-liability filers — worth one clarifying question. (3) The French design is internally inconsistent and externally checkable: p.5 says the IFI taxes real-estate wealth 'above €800,000' while Table A.2 and the slides say '€1.3 million threshold'; the actual IFI (CGI art. 977, confirmed via economie.gouv.fr/doctrine.fr search results) has a €1.3M entry threshold with a six-bracket scale starting at €800k (0/0.5/0.7/1/1.25/1.5% plus a décote to €1.4M), so which rule was coded materially affects Sim. FR's 117,585 taxpayers and €1,444M revenue. Minor: the 'flat 1%' Norwegian sim (p.10, Table A.2) omits Norway's actual 1.1% bracket above NOK 20M (2023 rates, confirmed via PwC/Life in Norway search results) — a small conservative bias worth acknowledging when contrasting the sim's 1.13%-of-GDP yield with Norway's actual 0.58%. Also note a page-render offset for figures: printed p.28 (Figure 5) is paper-30.png and printed p.29 (Figure 6) is paper-31.png in this render set, one page later than the task brief's mapping.
- **[advocate]** Three items this lens did not flag. (1) Table A.8 (p.47) contains a logical impossibility: Sim.1-x-Sim.2 overlap is printed as 72,186, but the same table prints Sim.1-x-IP = 170,404 and Sim.2-x-IP = 170,404, i.e. both scenarios contain every current IP taxpayer, so their intersection cannot be below 170,404 (Sim.1 only lowers the threshold; Sim.2 only removes rebates — neither removes an existing payer). Either the cell is a typo or the matrix's definition is undocumented; the discussant can raise it as a data-quality check. (2) The long-run effective tax rate t_l/r is defined two mutually inconsistent ways: p.15 defines it as 'the cumulative loss of wealth relative to the no-tax scenario' (which is 1-(1-t)^T, independent of g), while the Table 5 note (p.26) defines it as 'the ratio of cumulative tax liabilities to accumulated wealth over horizon T' (g-dependent, and matching the printed g-variation). This inconsistency both muddies the robustness discussion and explains part — but not all — of the g-dependence the robust-evidence finding attributes to implementation deviation; the Gini/top-share g-dependence remains unexplained by either definition. (3) Minor internal-count drift a discussant may note in passing: Table 1's title says 'the seven simulations performed' while listing eight (the abstract says 'eight simulated scenarios'); CH and Piketty taxpayer counts appear only as round numbers (12,400,000 / 11,010,000) against exact counts for all other designs; and the paper text (p.18) puts the IP's AETR onset 'around p99.3' / 'top 0.5%' while the slides say p99.5 and the actual effective-taxpayer share is 0.38%.

### Sources this lens fetched or saw
- [Brülhart, Gruber, Krapf & Schmidheiny (2022), 'Behavioral Responses to Wealth Taxes: Evidence from Switzerland', AEJ: Economic Policy (AEA article page, fetched)](https://www.aeaweb.org/articles?id=10.1257/pol.20200258) — A 1 percentage-point drop in a canton's wealth tax rate raises reported taxable wealth by at least 43% after 6 years; ~24% of the effect is taxpayer mobility, ~21% housing prices, with sizable evasion likely explaining most of the remainder.
- [Ley 38/2022, consolidated text at BOE (fetched)](https://www.boe.es/buscar/act.php?id=BOE-A-2022-22684) — Art. 3 creates the ITSGF: €700,000 mínimo exento; 0% to €3M, 1.7% to €5,347,998.03, 2.1% to €10,695,996.06, 3.5% above; IP paid is credited; applicable 'en los dos primeros ejercicios en los que se devengue' from entry into force (29 Dec 2022; devengo each 31 December); extended indefinitely by RDL 8/2023 DA 5.2 pending the review of patrimonial taxation.
- [La Moncloa / Ministerio de Hacienda press release, 20 Sept 2023: 'El Impuesto de Solidaridad de las Grandes Fortunas recauda 623 millones de euros' (fetched)](https://www.lamoncloa.gob.es/serviciosdeprensa/notasprensa/hacienda/paginas/2023/200923-impuesto-solidaridad-grandes-fortunas.aspx) — First ITSGF campaign (declarations July 2023): €623M from 12,010 taxpayers (~0.1% of filers); Madrid 10,302 taxpayers / €555M (89% of total); average payment ~€52,000; combined ITSGF + IP revenue in 2023 over €1,868M.
- [Newtral fact-check on ITSGF collections, 21 Sept 2023 (seen in search results)](https://www.newtral.es/impuesto-solidaridad-grandes-fortunas/20230921/) — The €623M collected in 2023 corresponds to ejercicio 2022 — 'primer ejercicio de devengo' — and is roughly 60% below the government's ~€1.5bn revenue forecast for the tax.
- [Statistics Norway (SSB), 'Tax for personal tax payers' (fetched)](https://www.ssb.no/en/inntekt-og-forbruk/skatt-for-personer/statistikk/skatt-for-personer) — Assessed Norwegian wealth tax (formuesskatt, state + municipal): NOK 28.8bn for 2023 and NOK 31.1bn for 2024.
- [Statistics Norway (SSB), annual national accounts (fetched)](https://www.ssb.no/en/nasjonalregnskap-og-konjunkturer/nasjonalregnskap/statistikk/nasjonalregnskap) — Norway's nominal GDP 2024 = NOK 5,382.4bn (preliminary); with SSB's assessed 2024 formuesskatt this gives an actual wealth-tax yield of ≈0.58% of GDP, versus 1.13% of GDP for the paper's Norwegian design applied to Spain.
- [INE, Contabilidad Nacional Anual de España, Revisión Estadística 2024 (press-note page; figure seen in search results)](https://ine.es/dyngs/Prensa/CNA2023.htm) — Spain's nominal GDP at current prices for 2022 = €1,373,629M, the denominator for expressing the paper's simulated revenues as shares of GDP (Sim. NO €15.46bn = 1.13% of GDP; IP baseline €1.13bn = 0.08%).
- [Mas-Montserrat, Durán-Cabré & Esteller-Moré (2025), 'Avoidance Responses to the Wealth Tax', Journal of Public Economics 246:105351 (abstract seen via search results / ScienceDirect listing)](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0047272725000490) — Spain-specific behavioural evidence (the paper's own citation): a 0.1 pp increase in the average wealth-tax rate reduces taxable wealth by 3.21% over 4 years; use of the IRPF–IP liability limit accounts for 92.6% of the revenue reduction — the very cap the paper removes from its foreign-design simulations.


## Citation check (all 47 reference-list entries)

**Summary.** Checked all 47 reference-list entries (paper pp. 35-37) plus the slides' References I-II (11 entries, all subsets of the paper's list), each against a fetched primary or bibliographic source. Result: 37 VERIFIED, 9 VERIFIED-WITH-ERRORS, 1 NOT FOUND. Every citation corresponds to a real work — there are no phantom references — but the list is carelessly produced. Headline items Max can raise to the authors' faces: (a) the two Scandinavian data-infrastructure citations on p.8 are scrambled — the Denmark wealth-records paper (Jakobsen, Jakobsen, Kleven & Zucman 2020, whose second author the reference also drops) is cited for Norway, while Denmark's wealth data is credited to Kleven et al. 2011, which is actually a randomized income-tax audit experiment; (b) the methodological backbone citation, Durán-Cabré & Esteller-Moré's long-run framework, carries an impossible ID ('2019 ... IEB Working Paper 2021/04' — it is IEB WP 2019/09), an error copied onto the conference slides; (c) 'Martínez (2017)' is really Francisco Adame Martínez with his surname split — the same Adame who is correctly listed inside the authors' own Libro Blanco entry, on whose committee author Cantó herself sat, and that Libro Blanco entry omits five of the fourteen committee members credited in the published volume; (d) Mas-Montserrat '(2024)' IEB Report is the 2017 four-author IEB Report 4/2017; (e) the Swiss Federal Tax Administration '2023' title appears to be invented — no publication with that name exists (closest: Brülhart's Wealth Tax Commission background paper), which matters because it is the stated source for the Canton-of-Zurich simulation; (f) the sole source for the cadastral-to-market revaluation ratio driving all wealth levels is an unpublished UAH dissertation (Petrov 2024 — real, supervised by Cantó); and (g) two citations mischaracterize their sources (Moore & Pecoraro on housing exclusions; Piketty's 0.1% bottom bracket rendered as 0%). All statuses carry URLs actually fetched or search records actually seen; the Libro Blanco committee page and the SEH appointing resolution were read directly from the PDFs.

**In-text mismatches.** All in-text citations have a corresponding reference-list entry and vice versa (no orphans in either direction, paper or slides). The problems are attribution and rendering errors: (1) SCRAMBLED COUNTRY ATTRIBUTIONS, p.8: 'Norway's population-wide wealth records based on tax registries (Jakobsen et al., 2020)' — Jakobsen et al. 2020 is the DENMARK wealth-tax paper (QJE 135(1), subtitle 'Theory and Evidence from Denmark'); the natural Norway-registry cite is Fagereng et al. (2020), which the paper cites elsewhere. In the same sentence, 'Denmark's integrated administrative panels combining income, wealth, and demographic data (Kleven et al., 2011)' — Kleven et al. 2011 (Econometrica 79(3)) is a randomized INCOME-tax audit experiment (0.3% evasion on third-party-reported vs 37% on self-reported income), not a wealth-data-infrastructure paper; the two Danish/Norwegian cites are effectively swapped/misused. (2) AUTHOR-NAME ERRORS: p.7 in-text 'Martínez, 2017' and list entry 'Martínez, F. A.' — the author is Francisco ADAME Martínez (compound surname mis-split; he appears correctly as 'Adame, F.' inside the paper's own Ruiz-Huerta et al. 2022 entry); p.30 '(Fagereng, 2020)' omits et al. for a four-author paper; Jakobsen et al. entry (paper and slides) omits co-author Kristian Jakobsen; Ruiz-Huerta et al. (2022) omits five of the fourteen committee vocales credited in the published Libro Blanco (López Rodríguez, Moreno González, Ruiz Almendral, Soler Roch, Villar Ezcurra); one author is rendered three ways across the list ('Díaz, S.' 2010 / 'Díaz, S. D.' 2011 / 'Díaz de Sarralde, S.' in Ruiz-Huerta et al.) — all Santiago Díaz de Sarralde. (3) WRONG YEAR/NUMBER: Durán-Cabré & Esteller-Moré (2019) numbered 'IEB Working Paper (2021/04)' — actually IEB WP 2019/09 (error also in slides Refs I); Mas-Montserrat '(2024)' IEB Report — actually IEB Report 4/2017, a four-author report (her piece inside is 'Impuesto sobre el patrimonio: particularidades y deficiencias del caso español'); Paulus et al. '(2017) Routledge' — book is Ashgate 2009 (Routledge e-reissue), with a scraped footnote marker in the title ('enlarged eu 1'). (4) KEY MISMATCH: fn.3 p.5 cites 'Code général des impôts (2023)' but the list entry is alphabetized under 'Direction Générale des Finances Publiques (2023)'; fn.3 also has stray periods ('Norwegian Tax Administration. (2023)'). (5) CONTENT-OF-CITATION ERRORS: p.6 says Moore & Pecoraro (2023) show 'excluding business equity and housing sharply reduces efficiency and fiscal capacity' — they find the housing exclusion quantitatively INSIGNIFICANT (only the business-equity exclusion undermines revenue); Table 2 p.11 presents 0% below €200k as Piketty's (2014) schedule — Piketty's ch.15 illustration puts ~0.1% below €200k (0.5% €200k-1M, 1% 1-5M, 2% >5M), so the bottom bracket is the authors' simplification; pp.5/10 describe Norway as 'flat 1%' — Skatteloven 2023 has 1.0% up to NOK 20m and 1.1% above; p.5 vs p.10 give inconsistent French thresholds (IFI 'above €800,000' vs ISF 'above 800,000€' vs the correct €1.3M liability threshold that appears elsewhere and in slides). (6) MINOR: '(OECD,2018' missing space p.4; fn.1 p.3 'see OECD Revenue Statistics' has no reference entry; 'Gómez (2023)' fn.11/p.26 vs 'Gomez' (refs, p.34) — author spells Gomez; Hérault & Jenkins (2021) and Díaz et al. (2010) carry no venue at all (the former is published: J. of Income Distribution 31(3-4), 2022; the latter is Revista de la CEPAL 102, Dec 2010). Slides-specific: slides cite the level/distance decomposition only as 'Díaz et al., 2010' and their References I-II omit the published Díaz et al. (2011, Revista de Economía Aplicada 19(57)) version; slides otherwise inherit the paper's Jakobsen and IEB-2021/04 errors.
